  • Students also raised austerity. Garland's answer: Spain tried to be fiscally careful at times because of big deficits, but not throughout.

4. Competitive devaluation ("rob your neighbour")

  • Recall GDP = C + I + G + X - M. When growth is weak, the options are expansionary fiscal policy (which raises the deficit and debt), lower interest rates (expansionary monetary policy), or the quick fix: a competitive devaluation.
  • A weaker peseta makes exports cheaper abroad, so X rises. Imports become dearer, so M falls. Both boost GDP.
  • Its informal name is "rob your neighbour": you artificially steal market share from your trading partners. France complained about cheap Spanish farm products, and France and Italy fought over luxury goods whenever the lira or the franc fell.
  • Downsides (Garland asked for them):
    • Imports become more expensive, which feeds inflation (including imported machinery and raw materials for production).
    • International transactions cost more.
    • Citizens get poorer: assets are worth less abroad, so travelling, moving or selling a house to move to Germany means losing money.
    • It is a short-term fix that removes pressure to raise productivity ("you're just comfortable").
  • EXAM: Garland's view: the main reason for the euro was to eliminate competitive devaluations inside Europe. Other reasons he gave were transparency and price equalization. EU members outside the euro (for example Poland) still have to keep their currencies within a band against the euro.
  • The Mercosur parallel: Brazil and Argentina have discussed a common currency for 30 years because big devaluations by one distort trade. A common currency needs similar fundamentals first, which is what the Maastricht criteria are for.

5. The Maastricht Treaty (1992): five convergence criteria

  • (1) Public deficit below 3% of GDP. (2) Public debt below 60% of GDP. These two are today's Stability and Growth Pact and still bind euro members. (3) Inflation close to the three lowest. (4) Interest rates close to the three lowest. (5) Currency stability: staying within the European Monetary System band for two years.
  • Logic: you cannot join a common currency with 10% inflation while partners have 1%, because at a fixed rate your products become uncompetitive. You must converge first.
  • Garland said "we're not going to be asking you about the exact" thresholds for inflation and interest rates (see Traps for the number conflict).
  • Spain's challenge: a deficit of 6.8% of GDP in 1994 made compliance look impossible. The PP government (from 1996) used privatizations and tax reform and, surprising almost everyone, brought the deficit to 2.9% (Garland's figure; the case gives 2.6% in 1998). Spain became an original euro member in 1999.
  • Debt flexibility: Italy and Belgium were around 120% and Greece around 110%. Countries above 60% were admitted if they showed a clear descending trend. Garland: "you're not going to get from 120 to 60 in 4 or 5 years".
  • Greece later admitted, after a change of government, that the data it gave to join had been incorrect.
  • Today almost nobody meets the debt rule. Luxembourg and Denmark do, and Germany is at about 60-63% ("the best among the larger ones"). Japan has the world's highest debt, then Greece about 146%, Italy, France about 110-120 and getting worse, Spain about 100, and Belgium, which improved to about 90. Countries over the limit must present a plan, and France was called in recently over its deficit.
  • Garland's Italy example (numbers approximate): its deficit was below 3% but its debt was very high. It asked to raise the deficit to about 2.9% to boost growth, and the EU said no because of the debt level.

6. Who controls which policy inside the euro

PolicyWho decidesGarland's comment
Monetary policyEuropean Central BankSpain has surrendered sovereignty
Trade policyThe EU (Commission)Common external tariff, no national trade policy. This is where the EU is strongest: China must negotiate with the whole bloc
Exchange-rate policyNobody directly; the market (the ECB only indirectly via interest rates)The euro is free-floating. Contrast India: limited capital mobility, and the RBI sometimes intervenes
Fiscal policyEach country, within the Stability and Growth PactThe only real short-run lever left
Structural policyEach country (education, investment and so on)Slow to take effect
  • His image: economic policy is like driving a car (accelerator, brake, GPS). In the eurozone, the country holds only the steering wheel while others control the accelerator and brake.
  • Robert Mundell (Nobel laureate) and others warned that if fiscal policy is the only national lever, there is a perverse incentive to be fiscally irresponsible (cut taxes, spend more), because that is the only way left to boost growth. Hence the 3% and 60% limits.
  • Mundell's phrase quoted: "great powers have great currencies" (Garland put it as "strong countries have strong currencies"). A united Europe needs a common currency.

7. Greece, austerity and pro-cyclical policy ("austercide")

  • In the Great Recession (2008-09), the ECB cut rates, which is expansionary monetary policy and countercyclical. The US did the same (the Fed cut faster, to almost zero) and ran expansionary fiscal policy (tax cuts, more spending).
  • A recession automatically raises the deficit, because tax revenue (tax rate times GDP) falls while unemployment benefits rise. Greece's deficit far exceeded 3%.
  • The EU demanded higher taxes and lower spending, a restrictive fiscal policy. That is pro-cyclical: it pushes a falling economy down further.
  • Critics called it "killing the patient". Garland: after seeing the post-2008 GDP paths of Europe and the US, it is hard to deny that the US did much better.
  • The defenders' case: the pain fixes structural deficits and inefficiencies, so you come out stronger in 3-4 years. Their example is Ireland, which has a similar population and similar austerity but was booming 3 years later while Greece struggled. The same policy does not work the same way everywhere.
  • Rescues: Greece, Ireland, Portugal, Cyprus, and Spain partially (its banks).
  • Grexit debate: going back to the drachma would let Greece devalue and export, but its debts were in euros, so leaving would make them unpayable. Even talk of it triggers capital flight to Switzerland. Greece is much healthier today, after about 10 years, but the cost was paid.
  • "We don't have laboratories in economics": we cannot rerun the policy to test the alternative.

8. Symmetric and asymmetric shocks; the common fiscal policy debate

  • Symmetric shock: hits the whole currency area (the pandemic, the Great Recession). The standard response is lower rates plus expansionary fiscal policy.
  • Asymmetric shock: hits one region (Detroit and the car industry in the US; Greece in the eurozone, which began as symmetric and became asymmetric). One monetary policy cannot be targeted at one region.
  • Optimal-currency-area remedies: (1) labour mobility: Detroit workers move to Texas, California or Florida, but Greeks rarely move to Finland because of language. (2) Federal fiscal transfers: Washington's money (FEMA is the example) flows to struggling states. In Europe there are almost no federal funds, only small transfers to the poorest regions.
  • Eurobonds: during the crisis Greece paid 15-20% to borrow. An EU-guaranteed bond might pay about 6% and be on-lent. Northern countries (now more Denmark, Sweden and Finland than Germany) resist, because they borrow at about 4% and do not want to subsidize fiscally irresponsible southern members.
  • Pandemic (a symmetric shock), lesson learned: the ECB cut rates back to zero, and the Stability and Growth Pact was put "in the freezer", so deficits rose to 6-8%. The EU borrowed jointly, which became the Next Generation EU funds. The suspension was extended to 2022 and then 2024. This was the opposite of 2010.
  • Today the south wants some permanent federal pooling (EU taxes and funds) and the north wants the old rules restored. The new pressure is defence and security spending: national or EU funds? There is also the question of what happens to regional and agricultural funds.

9. Closing: Spain's challenges

  • An ageing population with one of the highest life expectancies, which threatens pension sustainability.
  • Innovation and R&D: since you can no longer devalue, the only way to compete is to be more productive. This is a challenge for Europe, and especially for Spain.
  • Spain's strengths are renewables and infrastructure building. A student mentioned Spain's visa for entrepreneurs in target industries. Garland compared Spain with Germany's centuries-old strength in technical education, which Spain lacks and is trying to build.

The case, section by section

Introduction (2021)

  • Spain in 2021 is a modern democracy and a full EU member, but it has the highest unemployment among industrialized countries, an income-convergence gap, a high deficit and debt, and a tourism-heavy model "showing signs of exhaustion". With trade, exchange-rate and monetary policy out of national hands, the question is how to redesign the model using the EU recovery funds.

1970s: a difficult but successful political transition

  • Franco died on 20 Nov 1975 and Juan Carlos I was crowned on 22 Nov. Suárez was appointed in June 1976, and the referendum backed the transition with more than 94%. Parties were legalized in Feb 1977 (the Communist Party in April 1977). Suárez's UCD won the June 1977 elections.
  • Peseta devalued by almost 20% for competitiveness. Moncloa Pacts (Oct 1977): broad consensus, restrictive fiscal policy and tax reform (income, corporate and property taxes), which brought a sharp rise in revenue.
  • Late response to the 1973-74 oil shock (oil up 300%; Spain imported two-thirds of its energy). Politics came first. "Soft" income policies (generous wage settlements) fed inflation of 24.5% in 1977. Energy consumption was subsidized, so Spain was the only Western nation whose energy use rose in 1973-77. The trade deficit doubled (from $4.4bn to $8.3bn) and tourism, foreign investment and remittances fell. The crisis was deeper and longer than elsewhere.
  • Constitution by referendum on 6 Dec 1978. Catalan and Basque autonomy statutes in 1979. Coup attempt on 23 Feb 1981 (Tejero); the king's intervention was decisive. Calvo Sotelo then liberalized finance. By end-1981: inflation 14.5%, unemployment 14.2%, current-account deficit $5.4bn.

The decade of change and the EEC (1982-1992)

  • González's PSOE had a comfortable majority. Reforms covered the army, the autonomies, education, abortion and women's equality. It promised 800,000 jobs with no plan behind the promise.
  • Peseta devalued by about 25%. It should have been paired with tight money, but monetary policy was mildly expansionary. By 1985 inflation was down from 14.4% to 8.8%, growth was 2.3% and the trade deficit had halved, but unemployment was 21.6%. It stayed above 20% because of the baby boomers and more than a million women joining the labour force (1985-91), and because public jobs were duplicated across the autonomies.
  • EEC membership signed 12 Jun 1985, in force 1 Jan 1986: tariffs cut, foreign capital and EEC aid flowed in, and Spanish firms adapted helped by lower wages. The welfare state expanded (health coverage, pensions, unemployment coverage, free education to 16). ERDF money (FEDER in Spanish) financed motorways, high-speed rail and a satellite.
  • EMS entry on 19 Jun 1989 (65 pesetas per DM, ±6%). High rates, EU funds and opportunities brought capital inflows, so the peseta appreciated (from 170 per dollar in 1985 to 101.9 in 1990) despite intervention. The trade deficit went from $5.9bn to $31.1bn (1985-90). 1992 was "the year of Spain" (the Seville Expo, the Barcelona Olympics), followed by a crisis of confidence.

Maastricht and the European monetary storms (1992-93)

  • Maastricht signed Feb 1992 with five criteria. The case's version: deficit ≤3%, debt ≤60%, inflation ≤1.5 points over the 3 lowest, interest rates ≤2 points over the 3 lowest, and 2 years in the narrow EMS band.
  • Reunification (East marks converted 1:1) raised money supply, so the Bundesbank raised rates in June 1992. Add Denmark's "no" and doubts about the French vote, and money flowed into the DM. Speculative attacks hit the lira, the pound and the peseta. Italy and the UK left the ERM and the peseta was devalued 5%, after the Bank of Spain spent almost a trillion pesetas of reserves defending it. With the lira and the pound gone, attacks focused on the peseta: a further 6% devaluation (Nov 1992) and the band widened to ±15%.
  • The devaluations were inflationary, so rates rose. The Bank of Spain was given full control of monetary policy from 1994, which ended inflationary financing of deficits.

Spain in the early 1990s

  • 1993 was the worst recession since 1959: unemployment rising by 2,000 a day to 3.5 million, and FDI down 19%. A further 8% devaluation in May 1993 and a 7% devaluation in May 1995 (the fourth). The deficit was 6.8% in 1994 (the case elsewhere says 6.5%), debt rose from 41% to 58.7% (1989-94), and unemployment was 24.2% in 1994.
  • Response: austerity (a primary surplus in 1995), the new VAT, and partial privatizations of Endesa, Repsol, Ence and Telefónica. Labour reform led to a general strike in Jan 1994.

The PP in power: privatization and priority on convergence (1996-2004)

  • Aznar's PP (a minority government relying on nationalists) took joining the EMU as a strategic challenge. In 1996 Spain was still short on every criterion: inflation 3.8%, long-term rates 9.5%, deficit 5.9%, debt 65.4%.
  • Large-scale privatization (steel, finance, energy, telecoms) shrank the state's role. Deregulation increased competition and lowered prices. Labour reform ended legal wage indexation. Income-tax cuts. The Bank of Spain was made independent in 1998.
  • 1995-98: deficit from 6.8% to 2.6%, debt flat at about 64% but falling (allowed), inflation from 4.7% to 1.8%, unemployment from 22.7% to 18.7%. Spain met all five criteria. The euro replaced the peseta on 1 Jan 1999 (166.386 pesetas per euro).
  • Policy mix of the 2000s: expansionary monetary policy (ECB) plus contractionary fiscal policy. The 2003 Fiscal Stability Law required every level of government to be in balance. Debt fell to 45.4% (2004). Cheap euro rates fed construction: 700,000 homes in 2003, four times the UK's, with house prices up 20% that year.

Zapatero, the housing boom, the Great Recession and austerity (2004-2014)

  • The PSOE returned after the 11 March 2004 Madrid bombings. GDP grew 4.1% in 2006. The 2005 amendment made the fiscal rule "balanced over the cycle" instead of every year. Fiscal surplus of 1.2% (2005) and about 2% (2006-07). Debt about 40% (2006).
  • But the current-account deficit was close to 9% of GDP (2006), the second largest in the world after the US. 800,000 homes in 2006, record mortgages, heavily indebted builders, and about 800,000 illegal immigrants legalized in 2005.
  • Crash: growth of 0.9% (2008) and -3.8% (2009). Unemployment from 8.2% (2007) to 19.9% (2010). A 1.9% surplus in 2007 became a 9.5% deficit in 2010. Debt from 35.8% (2007) to 60.5% (2010), 69.9% (2011) and 100.7% (2014).
  • Root of the eurozone debt crisis: countries without their own exchange-rate or monetary policy could not restore competitiveness. Rajoy's PP (Nov 2011) applied EU-demanded austerity, criticized as pro-cyclical (Greece). EU bank rescue of up to €100bn (June 2012) via the FROB. Unemployment peaked at 26.1% in 2013.
  • Internal devaluation: with no currency to devalue, external adjustment came through falling labour costs plus collapsing imports. The current account went from a 9.5% deficit (2007) to a surplus of 0.1% (2012) and 2% (2013). Inflation fell from 4.1% (2008) to -0.5% (2015).
  • Political fallout: the 15-M protest movement (May 2011), which led to Podemos.

Fragmentation and regional tensions (2015-2019)

  • The end of two-party politics (Podemos, Ciudadanos, then Vox). The Catalan referendum and unilateral declaration of independence (Oct 2017), answered with Article 155. The first successful no-confidence vote (June 2018) brought Sánchez to power. By 2019: growth 2%, unemployment 14.1%, debt 95.5%, current-account surplus 2.1%, per-capita income €26,430.

Covid-19 and the emergency rescue (2020-21)

  • GDP fell by close to 11% in 2020, more than in other large EU economies, because of low diversification and tourism (almost 15% of GDP). The ECB stayed ultra-expansionary, and fiscal policy flipped: the SGP was relaxed ("in the freezer"). Next Generation EU: €390bn in grants plus €360bn in loans, the first EU debt to finance transfers, seen as a possible step toward fiscal union. Spain's share: €140bn over six years (€72.7bn non-refundable), about 12.5% of its 2020 GDP.

Future challenges

  • Rigid labour market: unemployment of 14.1% (2019) against 6.7% for the eurozone and 3.7% for the US. The causes are generous, long unemployment benefits (less incentive to search) and high social-security taxes (labour expensive relative to capital). Relaxing temporary contracts was an "escape valve" that postponed reform.
  • Ageing: life expectancy 83.2 (4th in the world), fertility 1.23 against 2.1 replacement. This threatens the pay-as-you-go pension system, in which today's workers fund today's retirees.
  • Energy dependence: about 75% imported (2019), with the trade balance in permanent deficit from 1973 to 2019. Renewables: 18.5% of energy, against 19.7% for the EU and 63.1% for the Nordics.
  • R&D: 1.24% of GDP (2018), against 2.18% for the EU, 2.82% for the US and 4.52% for South Korea. Productivity (GDP per worker) grew 6.9% between 1995 and 2019, against 19.4% for the EU and 43.9% for the US. Spain needs to compete through productivity, not wage cuts.

Allard's EU deck: the Spain slides

  • How can a eurozone country recover? It has no monetary or exchange-rate autonomy, only weak fiscal transfers and small flows of unemployed workers to other EU countries, and less fiscal autonomy (EU rules plus market fear). The only solutions are structural reform and exporting more through an "internal devaluation": lower wages, layoffs to raise productivity, aggressive marketing abroad. Lower wages and more unemployment cut consumption further.
  • Spain went from a "big spender" to a "big saver" country, using foreign demand for growth.
  • The Phillips curve for Spain (inflation against unemployment, 2000-2014): unemployment moved toward 25% while inflation fell to about zero.
  • Why is Spanish unemployment so high? OECD employment-protection legislation: Spain had a very rigid labour market (hard to dismiss workers), made somewhat more flexible after the 2012 reform.
  • A rigid, dual labour market (Allard's two-worker illustration):
    • Juan: 10 years in construction on a permanent contract, €30,000. Severance was €36,992 before the 2012 reform. After it, €16,438 for a fair dismissal or €27,123 for an unfair one. Plus unemployment insurance of €1,076 a month for 2 years.
    • Elena: a family doctor on temporary contracts with the Madrid government for 10 years, €28,000. Severance was €0 before 2012 and €900 after. Unemployment insurance of €1,000 a month for 2 years.
    • The point: insiders on permanent contracts are expensive to fire, while outsiders on temporary contracts absorb all the adjustment.
  • A Northern European solution: "flexicurity": a flexible labour market (easy hiring and firing), plus comprehensive social protection, plus active labour-market policies (training and placement).
  • Falling unit labour costs made Spain competitive, but reduced internal demand and delayed recovery. Spain regained its 2008 GDP level only in 2017, and per-capita income was still lower.
  • The internal devaluation occurred, but at what cost? Spain regained competitiveness and savings and is financially healthier, though deleveraging has been slower. The price: high unemployment, low and stagnant incomes, family savings used up, young people emigrating, and a four-year recession that destroyed huge wealth and jobs. Allard's open questions: was it worth it, was there a better way, and would it work in a health crisis like Covid?

What the professor added in class

  • EXAM: competitive devaluation: exports rise, imports fall, and GDP rises in the short run. The costs are imported inflation, dearer imported inputs, citizens and assets worth less abroad, no productivity pressure, and angry neighbours ("rob your neighbour").
  • EXAM: Garland's opinion is that eliminating competitive devaluation was the main reason for the euro.
  • EXAM: pro-cyclical (restrictive fiscal policy in a recession) against countercyclical (expansionary monetary policy in a recession). The EU's 2010s mix in Greece combined both, and the US's expansionary mix recovered faster.
  • EXAM: in the eurozone, a country controls only fiscal policy (limited by the SGP) and structural policy. Monetary policy is the ECB's, trade policy the EU's, and the exchange rate is market-floating.
  • EXAM: symmetric against asymmetric shocks, and why the US copes better: labour mobility plus federal transfers. Europe has neither, and no permanent eurobonds. This is core material for the EU-versus-US short answer.
  • Mundell's warning: a single currency without a common fiscal policy creates an incentive for fiscal irresponsibility, which is why the SGP exists.
  • The pandemic as the reverse of 2010: the SGP frozen, deficits of 6-8%, joint EU borrowing (NGEU) at near-zero rates.
  • Greece's entry statistics were false. Grexit was impossible in practice because debts were in euros and leaving would set off capital flight.
  • Ireland and Greece: same population and same austerity, yet different results. The same policy can work differently in different countries.
  • Defence spending is the new pressure on the fiscal rules and on the common-fund debate.
  • Spain's long-run weakness is the lack of the technical and vocational tradition that Germany has. Spain debates copying the German system (picked up again in Session 5).

Likely exam angles

  • MCQ: which is NOT an effect of a devaluation? "Imports become cheaper" is wrong. Devaluation makes imports dearer, exports cheaper, and citizens poorer abroad.
  • MCQ: which policy can a eurozone member still set? Fiscal policy (within SGP limits) and structural policy.
  • MCQ: inside a monetary union, how does a country regain competitiveness? Internal devaluation (cutting wages and unit labour costs), not exchange-rate devaluation.
  • MCQ: Spain ran fiscal surpluses in 2005-07 yet was vulnerable. Why? A current-account deficit of about 9% of GDP and a private credit and housing boom fed by low euro interest rates.
  • MCQ: which is NOT a Maastricht criterion? Anything about unemployment, growth or the current account.
  • MCQ: how were high-debt countries (Italy, Belgium) allowed into the euro? Debt above 60% was accepted if clearly falling.
  • Short answer, EU against US: one currency in both, but the US has labour mobility, federal fiscal transfers and expansionary crisis policy, while the EU has an ECB-only monetary policy, national fiscal policy capped by the SGP, almost no federal budget, eurobonds only once (NGEU), and post-2008 austerity that slowed recovery.
  • MCQ: what does a dual labour market mean? Strong protection for permanent workers and almost none for temporary ones, so temporary workers absorb the adjustment and youth unemployment is high.
  • MCQ: what changed in the EU response to Covid compared with 2010? The SGP was suspended and the EU borrowed jointly to give grants (NGEU).

Traps and look-alikes

  • Maastricht inflation and interest thresholds conflict. In class (both Session 2 and Session 5) Garland said inflation within 2 points of the 3 lowest and interest rates within 1.5 points. The Spain case (and the actual treaty) says inflation ≤1.5 points and long-term interest rates ≤2 points. Garland said he will not ask the exact numbers. If forced, use the case: inflation 1.5, interest 2.
  • Coup date: Garland said March 1981, but the case says 23 February 1981.
  • Deficit at euro entry: Garland said 2.9% by 1999, but the case says 2.6% in 1998. The 1994 deficit is 6.8% in one place in the case and 6.5% in another.
  • Unemployment low point: Garland said 7% in 2007, but the case says 8.2% (2007) and 8.4% (2006).
  • Current-account deficit: the case gives "close to 9%" for 2006 and 9.5% for 2007. Both are "second largest in the world after the US".
  • The transcript once says Spain joined the EU "on January 1st, 1996". That is a garble: it was 1 Jan 1986. 1996 is when the PP took power.
  • Pro-cyclical is not procyclical-good. Austerity in a recession is pro-cyclical, because it amplifies the cycle. The ECB's rate cuts were countercyclical.
  • "Exchange-rate policy" in the euro is not run by the ECB. The euro floats freely, and the ECB only influences it indirectly through rates.
  • Internal devaluation (wages and costs down) is not devaluation (currency down). Spain did the first in 2010-13 and the second repeatedly in 1977-1995.
  • The EMS appreciation of 1987-90 (peseta up, competitiveness down, trade deficit up) is the opposite direction from the 1992-95 devaluations. Read MCQ dates carefully.
  • The eurobond resisters are now mainly Denmark, Sweden and Finland, per Garland ("not so much Germany lately"). The Germany case says Germany and the Netherlands objected to corona bonds in 2020.
Session 3 · 17 Sep 2026 · Garland · complete notes

Session 3: Dutch disease, real exchange rates, fixed rates and Brazil under Lula (Prof. Gonzalo Garland)

Garland opened by reminding the class of the group midterm (two groups, one report each, maximum 5 pages, two questions on the Angola case) and then spent the first half of the session on the exchange-rate tools needed for that case: how a floating rate is set by supply and demand for dollars, why a resource discovery appreciates the local currency (Dutch disease), the tradables / non-tradables distinction, nominal versus real exchange rates (the 10 → 15 / 20 / 25 pesos example from his "Nominal and Real Exchange Rates" deck) and the two classic fixed-rate stories (China pegged above equilibrium, Argentina below). The second half was the HBS case "Brazil under Lula: Off the Yellow BRIC Road" (Musacchio, set in 2006–07): Pax Britannica, import substitution, inelastic commodity demand, the Brazilian miracle and 1982 debt crisis, hyperinflation, the Real Plan and debt restructuring, Lula's three pillars, inequality and Acemoglu–Johnson–Robinson institutions. This is the core of Garland's MCQs: the posted sample questions on "effects of the Dutch disease" and "a fixed exchange rate below equilibrium" both come from this material. His "Dutch Disease II: the cure" deck is filed here (it is the sequel to the mechanics taught today), but he actually walked through it in Session 9.

Slide-by-slide

Dutch disease I (mechanics, taught from slides in class; the Part I deck file is not in the sources)

  • The paradox: finding and exploiting a natural resource should be good news, but it can generate many negative effects. That is the core of both the Dutch disease and the wider natural resource curse.
  • Origin: the Netherlands after it discovered large natural-gas deposits in the late 1950s (Garland's slide). More than a decade later many argued the Dutch economy had grown faster and had lower unemployment before the discovery. The gas sector did well; industry suffered most, with lower production and lower exports.
  • Exchange rate as the price of a good (the dollar). Under a flexible / variable / floating / free rate (all synonyms) the market sets it by supply and demand.
  • Classical trade-based view: demand for dollars comes from importers (an expensive dollar means few imports; a cheap dollar means many imports). Supply of dollars comes from exporters (Brazilian coffee and sugar exporters are paid in dollars but pay wages in reais, so they sell dollars). An expensive dollar means a cheap real, so exports and dollar supply rise.
  • Discovery: the following year gas/oil exports flood the market with dollars. The supply curve shifts right, the dollar gets cheaper (his example: from 280 to 180 local units per dollar) and the local currency appreciates (the guilder strengthened).
  • Effect on non-tradables: little, because they cannot be exported or imported; local supply and demand decide.
  • Effect on tradables other than gas: manufactured exports become more expensive abroad and lose competitiveness; imports (German, French goods) become cheaper. Even tradables sold locally (agriculture) are hit because imports now undercut them, so farming is destroyed too.
  • Employment: non-gas exports fall and unemployment rises because the gas sector is not labor-intensive; the jobs lost in manufacturing far exceed those created in gas.
  • Net result: gas-driven GDP growth is offset by falling industrial production and exports, so overall GDP growth is lower. That is the Dutch disease.

Nominal and real exchange rates (deck "Nominal and Real Exchange Rates: the effect of inflation on exchange rates")

  • Start: 1 US$ = 10 pesos. A product costs 100 pesos, so it sells in the US for $10.
  • Domestic inflation is 100% in a year, US inflation 0%. The product now costs 200 pesos. If the rate stays at 10, it must sell at $20: competitiveness is lost and exports collapse.
  • Rule: to keep competitiveness the nominal rate must change by the inflation differential (home inflation minus partner inflation = 100% − 0% = 100%). New rate 1 US$ = 20 pesos, so the 200-peso product still sells at $10. That is a nominal depreciation with the real rate unchanged.
  • Case 10 → 15 pesos (a 50% adjustment, less than the differential): the 200-peso product sells at $13.33. Nominal depreciation (10 to 15) but real appreciation of the peso, because the country's goods are now less competitive.
  • Case 10 → 25 pesos (a 150% nominal depreciation, more than the differential): the product sells at $8. Nominal depreciation and real depreciation: the country is more competitive than at the start.
  • Summary Garland gave: in all three cases the nominal rate depreciated (10 → 15 → 20 → 25); only the comparison with the inflation differential tells you what happened in real terms. Any country with inflation above its trade partners must let its currency depreciate or it loses competitiveness. Under a float this is a natural market adjustment (higher prices → fewer exports → less dollar supply), not necessarily a government decision.

Dutch Disease II: "The Cure" (deck; presented in class in Session 9, see that unit)

  • Cause: the main driver of the negative effects is the strong appreciation of the local currency caused by large inflows of foreign currency. So the main way to avoid some of the damage is to control that appreciation.
  • Exchange-rate chart: the new resource shifts dollar supply right (S1 with the new natural resource), the local currency appreciates.
  • Dirty / managed floating: S shifts to S2 because resource sales create a large supply of foreign currency; the central bank buys dollars (demand D1 → D2), selling local currency and pushing against the appreciation.
  • Fixed rate: if supply and demand stay at S1/D1 the central bank does nothing. If oil prices rise (S → S2) it must buy dollars; if supply moves left it must sell dollars and withdraw local currency.
  • Pros and cons: under dirty floating with heavy inflows the central bank keeps buying dollars and injecting local currency, which raises inflation. Under a fixed rate the same happens when oil is high, and when oil falls it must spend reserves and drain liquidity.
  • Conclusion: if a country expects large resource inflows it is better to "sterilize" them, keeping the foreign currency outside the country; many countries use sovereign wealth funds for this.

What the professor taught in class, in order (exchange-rate part)

Tradables and non-tradables

  • Tradable: anything that can be produced locally, exported or imported (most physical goods, commodities, cars, computers). In today's free-trade world most goods are tradable.
  • Non-tradable: hard or uneconomic to trade, so local supply and demand rule. Examples given: real estate/houses (prefabricated houses are an exception), cement (traditionally), basic glassware (IKEA glasses are made locally; Bohemian crystal is tradable), water, and most services (the textbook haircut; many medical and legal services).
  • Tradability can change: Cemex (raised by a student) as the leader in making cement tradable; Garland mentioned "liquefied" cement as the change. A tradable good can be made non-tradable by policy: Nvidia's most advanced chips under US export restrictions; goods banned in old protectionist India.
  • Labor: tradable within the EU (free movement), largely non-tradable worldwide; even in Europe doctors and lawyers cannot move easily. Energy: depends on interconnections (Spain–Portugal–France grids).
  • Tourism: a sudden large tourism boom could create a Dutch-disease-like effect, because tourism counts as an export (visitors bring and exchange currency). The problem is the sudden surge, not a stable level.

Reserve currency side discussion

  • A student asked about the US. Garland: the dollar is set worldwide with essentially no intervention (he said since about 1995). The world's huge appetite for dollars strengthens a currency that would otherwise be weaker. Negative for the US trade balance (part of the Trump complaint about deficits), positive for US wealth (Americans are "rich" abroad; US GDP looks very large in dollars). In economics most things have a positive and a negative side.

Fixed (pegged) exchange rates

  • Under a float currencies appreciate / depreciate; under a fixed rate they revalue / devalue (technically, though many people mix the words).
  • China 1995–2004: pegged at 8.28 renminbi per dollar, ABOVE equilibrium (axis = local currency per dollar, so "above" = expensive dollar = weak renminbi). Effects: Chinese products extremely cheap worldwide, imports suppressed, huge trade surplus, growth around 9%. The excess dollar supply flooded the banking system and the central bank bought it all (at 8.28), building gigantic reserves. Negatives: external debt is expensive (paid in dollars) and inflation is worse (imports such as oil cost more). US pressure ("you grow by artificially weakening your currency and don't buy my products in a recession") led China to open a band. Today about 6.9–7.1, i.e. it appreciated as expected.
  • Argentina, 1 peso = 1 dollar from the early 1990s: pegged BELOW equilibrium (cheap dollar = overvalued peso). Bad for growth (lower exports, higher imports), but chosen because Argentina had huge external debt and had just had hyperinflation: priority was cheap debt service and cheap imports to fight inflation. When it was abandoned the peso completely overshot.
  • Saudi Arabia: fixed rate for 40 years this year. Changing the level would be a devaluation or revaluation (Garland hesitated on the number in this session; in Session 9 he gave 3.75 riyals per dollar).
  • Black markets: a peg below equilibrium creates excess demand for dollars (you get to the bank late and there are no dollars), so a black market appears, sometimes at dramatically higher rates (Nigeria's naira; Ethiopia raised by a student). Argentina's first peg had no black market because the convertibility law meant the central bank always supplied any dollar demanded (financed by capital inflows and IMF loans); the system exploded when reserves ran out.
  • China could hold its peg partly because it can restrict capital flows (student point; Garland agreed and deferred details).
  • The "Lula effect" (capital-flow panic, even under a float): as Lula rose in the 2002 polls (union leader, had not finished secondary school), investors ran, the real depreciated sharply and the government worried about dollar debt. Responses: sell reserves, raise interest rates to attract capital. Fear of exactly this is one reason China and India refuse full capital-account liberalization.

The case, section by section: "Brazil under Lula: Off the Yellow BRIC Road" (HBS 707-031)

Opening: Lula's second victory (Oct 2006) and the 5% question

  • Lula promised growth above 5% a year with redistribution. Goldman Sachs' BRIC (Brazil, Russia, India, China) would be the largest bloc by 2050, but Brazil was the underperformer with some of the worst business-environment indicators.
  • Lula's first term cut poverty and brought the Gini to its lowest recorded level; he kept price and exchange-rate stability, paid off the IMF and lowered interest rates. Problems: crime, corruption, almost half of workers informal, land conflict (MST).
  • McKinsey (2006): five obstacles ("Brazil cost") — informality, macro deficiencies (high real interest rate, high debt), red tape, poor public services (education, justice, security), infrastructure.

Latin America's giant

  • 2006: 6th largest by area, 8th largest economy, 186 million people (5th most populous), but 73rd in GNI per capita at PPP.
  • Gini 0.56 (2005): richest 10% earn 45% of income, poorest 50% get 14%; 1% of farmers control 46% of land. Academics trace this to Portuguese colonization.

A history of inequality, diseases and good hope

  • Large land grants to noblemen, sugar plantations, Indian then African slave labor; high settler mortality (malaria, yellow fever).
  • Institutions thesis: temperate, low-mortality areas got mass settlement and institutions with equal economic and political rights (northern US, Canada); high-mortality areas such as Brazil got elite institutions, unequal land and political rights, less investment in public goods, slower growth, more inequality.

Brazil after independence

  • Peaceful independence: the Portuguese court fled Napoleon to Brazil (1807), capital moved to Rio (decline of the northeast); Prince Pedro declared independence in 1822. Constitutional monarchy until a republic in 1889.
  • Republic (1889–1930) regulated the international coffee supply to control the price; benefits concentrated in white southerners and new immigrants; after abolition (1888) blacks and northeastern migrants ended up in favelas.
  • 1930: Getúlio Vargas' authoritarian regime and import substitution: controlled exchange rates, coffee revenue used to import machinery. Generous benefits for registered workers made hiring and firing costly, creating a large informal sector.
  • Military regime from 1964: forced savings, financial liberalization, subsidized loans; the "Brazilian miracle" of over 10% growth (1968–1976), financed by excessive foreign borrowing. In 1982 Brazil suspended payments on its foreign debt; crisis, fall of the regime, democracy in 1985.

The emergence of Lula

  • Born 1945 in the poor northeast, factory worker, union leader, founded the Workers' Party (PT) in 1980; only elementary education; jailed and tortured under the military. The 1988 Constitution made voting compulsory. Lost the 1989 election to Collor.

From hyperinflation to the Cardoso era

  • The 1988 Constitution created earmarked spending without revenue; deficits were monetized; government bonds and all contracts were indexed to inflation, so inflation spiraled. Brazil became "world champion in signing unfulfilled agreements with the IMF".
  • About 35% poor; the Gini passed 0.6 in 1990. Collor impeached (1992).
  • Finance minister Cardoso's Real Plan (1994): balanced budget, new currency (the real), fixed exchange rate, very high interest rates. Inflation fell from about 2,000% (1993) to 16% (1996).
  • Cardoso president 1994–2002: privatizations, cash transfers (poverty 35% → 28%).
  • Shortcomings of the Real Plan: high rates drew inflows mostly to roll over public debt; the 1997 Asian crisis and 1998 Russian default hit; reserves fell from $52bn to $36bn (1997–99); an overvalued exchange rate plus high interest payments drove unsustainable current-account deficits. In early 1999 Brazil was forced to float. The depreciation boosted exports from 7% (1998) to 10% (1999) to 12% of GDP (2001); inflation targeting took over as anchor.

Brazil under Lula: signaling left, turning right

  • 2002: debt 77% of GDP, interest 8.5% of GDP a year, mostly short-term dollar bonds. Markets feared an Argentine-style default (Soros: "meltdown"); country risk went from 700 to 2,000 basis points.
  • Pillar 1, macro stability: moderate finance minister Palocci, ex-FleetBoston banker Meirelles at the central bank; rates raised to 26%; primary surplus target 4.25% of GDP (above IMF advice); swap of short-term dollar debt for longer domestic-currency debt (dollar debt ended below reserves); debt/exports from almost 3 to under 1; ratings B+ → BB; modest pension reform.
  • Pillar 2, trade and "the hand of God": leading role in Doha; created the G-20 of developing countries (Cancún 2003) to cut rich-country farm subsidies; Mercosur (Venezuela invited); hostility to the FTAA. China's commodity demand ("the hand of God") pushed growth to 5.8% (2004); China rose from 12th to 3rd market; current-account surpluses of $11.7bn (2004), $14.0bn (2005), about $13.6bn (2006).
  • Land: MST wants redistribution; critics say large farms are more productive (ROI 2.5x) and made Brazil a world leader in sugar, soy, orange juice, chicken and beef.
  • Pillar 3, inequality: minimum wage from under $80 to over $160 a month (2003–06); expansion of transfers, Bolsa Escola renamed Bolsa Família (conditional on school attendance, medical visits and vaccination). Transfers cost under 1% of GDP and explain most of the fall in poverty after 2001.
  • Kuznets: inequality rises with industrialization then falls; some inequality may help saving (top 5% of US earners = 75% of savings), but Brazilian data show the opposite.

Life at the bottom of the pyramid

  • Becker: people commit crimes when expected illegal returns exceed labor-market returns; Brazilian studies link inequality to violent crime. Homicide rate 27 per 100,000 (US 5). Crime enters investors' risk assessment (2006 São Paulo wave led to rating downgrades).
  • Bullet-proof SUVs, helicopters: São Paulo had 220 heliports in 2002, more than seven major world cities combined.
  • Business at the bottom of the pyramid: FEMSA small and returnable Coca-Cola bottles, Magazine Luiza and Casas Bahia credit to informal workers, ABN AMRO Real microcredit in favelas. Social mobility via soccer (Roberto Carlos, Ronaldinho).
  • Education: average 3 years of schooling until 1980; near-universal enrollment by 2005 but poor quality (low PISA); spending skewed to universities; only 20% of public-university students from poor families (2006).

BRIC, CRIB or BIC? and the "Brazil cost"

  • Brazil had the lowest growth of the four ("CRIB"); Fortune proposed dropping Russia ("BIC").
  • Delfim Netto's three causes of slow growth: high tax burden, overvalued exchange rate, high interest rates; others add red tape and infrastructure. 152 days to start a business; firing cost 37% of annual salary; tax burden 72% of profits; savings rate 22% (Russia, India about 30%, China about 52%).
  • Brazil ranks higher than China and India on the HDI (literacy, enrollment, life expectancy, GDP per capita), lower than Russia.

Lula's challenge

  • Netto: inequality fell only because growth was low; faster growth with imported skill-biased technology will raise it. Lending rates above 48% shut small firms out; shoe makers protested an overvalued real. A fragmented Congress (20+ parties) and the cash-for-votes scandal (Palocci resigned) made reform hard. FT warning: without restructuring during the commodity boom, a boom-and-bust cycle may follow.

What the professor added in class

  • Pax Britannica (Waterloo 1815 to 1914): British naval dominance, relatively peaceful (despite wars such as Franco-Prussian 1870), above all free trade. Adam Smith (markets) and David Ricardo (comparative advantage). Britain lacked resources, so it imported raw materials and exported manufactures; Garland cited estimates that close to 60% of the world's manufactures were made in Britain then. Opium Wars, opening of Japan, East India Company as part of the same story.
  • Comparative-advantage logic: Brazil coffee and sugar, Argentina farm goods, Zaire mining, Chile copper. From the 1920s and the Great Depression, emerging countries argued they would keep buying ever-dearer manufactures while their prices fell, so they switched to import substitution: tariffs to protect "infant industries". Same model in India (the "Permit Raj", garbled in the transcript).
  • Inelastic demand for coffee and sugar (like oil): once the crop is in, supply is vertical. Revenue = area P × Q. A bumper crop makes the price collapse and revenue falls, so Brazil famously destroyed part of the crop (taking it out on boats); Spanish farmers occasionally pour milk into rivers. The same logic explains oil: small supply cuts send prices sharply up; recessions push them down.
  • Monarchy: Garland was unsure of the end date (said 1881 or 1879); the case says 1889.
  • Debt crisis 1982 started in Mexico, which defaulted first; domino effect; almost all Latin American countries except two (not named; UNSURE which) stopped servicing external debt. The previous "miracle" growth had been financed by borrowing from private banks.
  • How to finance a deficit: raise taxes (slow), borrow abroad, borrow at home, or print. After a default nobody abroad lends and citizens will not lend either, so governments print → hyperinflation. Bolivia about 40,000% in 1985, then Argentina, Brazil, Peru, Nicaragua; later Zimbabwe and Venezuela, always the same story. Exhibit 5: Brazilian inflation 2,001% in 1993, 94% in 1995 (GDP deflator), because they stopped printing.
  • CPI vs GDP deflator: CPI is consumption-based and includes imports; the deflator covers only domestic production (Chilean copper moves the deflator, not the CPI). Usually similar in magnitude.
  • Cardoso deserves more credit than he got: as finance minister he ran the Real Plan, stopped money printing and restructured the debt (the IMF/World Bank would only help after a deal with creditor banks). Restructuring has three elements: lower interest rate, longer maturity, and a "haircut" (part of the principal forgiven, e.g. owe 100, repay 80 or 70). Same tools seen later with Greece-style support.
  • A student mentioned the artificial unit of account used to keep prices stable during the transition (price in units fixed, cash price changing). Garland linked it to maintaining competitiveness; the case text does not describe it (it was the URV; UNSURE as Garland did not name it).
  • Lula's three contributions: (1) stability — merit was continuing Cardoso's prudent policy, not inventing it, after talking of default in earlier campaigns; (2) "hand of God" — luck: he governed during the great convergence when China's thirst for resources lifted Brazil; China is today Brazil's first trade partner and very connected to Chile, Argentina, Peru; (3) inequality — his own contribution, conditional cash transfers, copied worldwide (India experimenting), not expensive. Criticisms: political use (Venezuela), possible incentive to have more children.
  • Disclaimer on Lula: after the case he was tried and jailed for corruption; the Supreme Court later annulled the trial. The country is split roughly half and half on whether he is a hero or corrupt. He is president again and running for re-election (elections about a month after the class, polls slightly ahead).
  • Why inequality matters beyond ethics: insecurity and crime (Becker: if poor in Brazil it can be rational to be a criminal; the only exits are soccer or crime), less FDI, a smaller consumer market, weaker local talent.
  • Bimodal income distribution: most countries have roughly normal income distributions that shift right over time; research by a Columbia professor (UNSURE who) finds Latin America and Sub-Saharan Africa are bimodal — a rich group living like Americans or Europeans, a thin middle, and millions of poor. Market-size lesson: Brazil has about 214 million people, but a middle-class product may face only about 40 million customers. Do not equate population with market.
  • Informal sector from over-taxation; USP like India's IITs/IIMs: excellent but only about 20% of students from underprivileged backgrounds; the real problem is primary and secondary schooling.
  • Acemoglu, Johnson and Robinson, "The Colonial Origins of Comparative Development" (transcript says "Samuel Johnson"; the co-author is Simon Johnson): extractive colonies (Portugal in Brazil, Spain) built institutions to send resources to the metropolis; "Neo-Europes" (US, Canada) replicated European institutions. They first linked this to climate, then dropped weather in Why Nations Fail: North vs South Korea, Nogales (Mexico) vs Nogales (US).
  • 2x2 grid: political and economic institutions, each inclusive or extractive. Development needs both inclusive (democracy, rule of law; competition, no dominant monopolies). US, Canada, Western Europe in the inclusive box; China moved to more inclusive economic but still extractive political institutions, and the book warns it must change or face trouble. Chinese critics call it a Western view. The Narrow Corridor: inclusive institutions are not permanent; you can slide back.
  • "Democracy is a superior good" theory: demand for democracy rises with income (Hong Kong). Counter-example: Gulf countries at $40,000–50,000 with no pressure for democracy; no income tax creates a strange citizen–state relationship, which is the political resource curse (to be seen in Angola).

Likely exam angles

  • MCQ "Which is NOT an effect of the Dutch disease?" → the odd one out is anything that makes non-resource tradables MORE competitive (e.g. "increase in competitiveness of local agricultural products"); real effects are local-currency appreciation, less competitive manufactured exports, cheaper imports, higher manufacturing unemployment, lower overall growth.
  • Rate 10 → 15 pesos with a 100% inflation differential → nominal depreciation, real appreciation (product sells at $13.33, less competitive).
  • Rate 10 → 20 → nominal depreciation, real rate unchanged ($10). Rate 10 → 25 → nominal and real depreciation ($8).
  • Peg ABOVE equilibrium (China 8.28) → undervalued local currency: exports up, imports down, trade surplus, growth, reserve accumulation; costly external debt and imported inflation.
  • Peg BELOW equilibrium (Argentina 1:1) → overvalued local currency: cheaper external debt, cheaper imports (fights inflation), positive wealth effect; but fewer exports, more imports, trade deficit, black-market risk.
  • Why a non-labor-intensive resource sector worsens Dutch-disease unemployment → jobs lost in manufacturing are not replaced.
  • Short answer "resource curse": define Dutch disease via real appreciation hurting other tradables; use Netherlands as origin.
  • Three elements of a debt restructuring → lower rate, longer term, haircut.
  • Lula's three pillars → macro stability, export promotion (hand of God = China), redistribution (Bolsa Família).
  • Why Brazil floated in 1999 → overvalued peg, current-account deficits, reserve losses after Asian/Russian crises.
  • What finances deficits after a default → money printing → hyperinflation.

Traps and look-alikes

  • Garland's axis is local currency per dollar. "Above equilibrium" = more pesos per dollar = undervalued local currency (China). "Below equilibrium" = fewer pesos per dollar = overvalued local currency (Argentina). Do not read "below" as "weak".
  • Nominal depreciation can coexist with real appreciation (10 → 15 case). Do not assume a rising nominal rate means more competitiveness.
  • "Revalue/devalue" is for fixed rates; "appreciate/depreciate" is for floating rates.
  • Dutch disease hurts tradables (manufacturing, agriculture); non-tradables (real estate, haircuts) are largely unaffected or even boom.
  • Date of the Dutch gas discovery: Garland's slide says late 1950s; the Angola case footnote says the Netherlands had the problems in the 1970s. Both refer to the same Groningen-era story; know which source says what.
  • In class Garland misspoke twice in the real-rate example ("depreciate 10%" and "from 10 to 13"); the deck numbers are 10 → 15 (50%) and $13.33.
  • Brazilian peak inflation: class quoted 2,001% in 1993; Exhibit 5 also shows 2,303% (deflator) in 1994 and CPI 1,928% (1993) / 2,076% (1994). The case text says "about 2,000%".
  • China as Brazil's partner: case says it rose from 12th to 3rd market; Garland said 5th then, 1st today.
  • Population: case 186 million (2006); class 214 million (today).
  • The Real Plan was a fixed-rate anchor; the 1999 float + inflation targeting came after it failed. Do not confuse it with Lula's policies.
  • "Lula effect" = capital outflow causing depreciation under a float; the central bank sold dollars (fx notes: "sold dollars to weaken the dollar and strengthen the real").
Session 4 · 21 Sep 2026 · Allard · complete notes

Session 4: The US economy (Prof. Gayle Allard)

Allard's first session. She used the US to review the core macro toolkit from Managerial Economics, applied to real data, and then framed each indicator as an investor risk question. Topics: GDP growth, the output gap and potential GDP; why rankings need nominal GDP; unemployment, the NAIRU, non-farm payrolls and the Sahm rule; headline and core inflation; the Phillips curve; government spending, revenues, deficits and debt, and when debt is a problem; healthcare and the welfare state; and US and European attitudes to poverty. The material comes from the deck "The US Economy" (mostly charts, little text) and the case "The U.S. Economy: A Case Study" (Allard, 2006, revised April 2021). The session feeds two of the named short-answer topics, the EU vs US comparison and the indicators of risk or opportunity for an investor, and it supplies the sample MCQ on the EU vs US (the correct reading is that the US welfare system is means-tested, not absent). The deck's later slides (current account, tariffs, inequality, fiscal transfers, demography) were not reached in S4. Allard covered the current-account and fiscal-transfer slides at the start of S6, so they appear in both units.

Slide-by-slide

Slide 2: Where are we going?

  • Review of basic concepts applied to the US: GDP and cycles, inflation and unemployment, fiscal issues ("does the US have a debt problem?"), monetary policy, the current account and its risks, institutions, special issues, "American exceptionalism?"
  • Investor questions: will US interest rates and the dollar rise or fall? Are US bonds safe? Does the US offer good business opportunities? Is a recession coming?

Slides 3-4: Real GDP growth compared, and the output gap

  • US real GDP growth has recently outpaced nearly all developed countries, except Spain (OECD chart: USA, Japan, Germany, China, India, Spain).
  • Output gap = the % difference between actual and potential GDP. A positive gap is an inflationary gap (actual above potential). A negative gap is a recessionary gap (actual below potential).
  • Deck: on two accepted estimates (OECD and IMF), the US has been in a positive (inflationary) output gap since Covid. In class Allard said the latest estimates put the US close to potential, with one showing a small negative gap (see Traps).

Slides 5-6: GDP per capita, and "Will fast US growth continue?"

  • Annualized growth in Q2 2026 was 1.5%. Average growth was 2.1% in 2025 and 2.8% in 2024, so growth is slowing.
  • The outlook depends on stability, policy developments and the performance of the tech/AI sector.

Slide 7: Where is GDP growing?

  • Growth varies widely by state, and some states are actually in recession.
  • Tech, health and AI are booming while other sectors suffer. In class: growing sectors are healthcare, real estate, government, technology and financial services; shrinking ones are manufacturing and construction.

Slides 8-10: Unemployment, the NAIRU, non-farm payrolls

  • OECD unemployment chart (Canada, Mexico, Spain, US): the US rate is low and fairly stable apart from the Covid spike.
  • NAIRU (non-accelerating inflation rate of unemployment) is the "just right" unemployment rate, estimated rather than observed. Above it, unemployment is too high and growth is weak. Below it, unemployment is too low and inflation picks up. The US is at or slightly below the NAIRU.
  • Non-farm payrolls (new jobs created) are released the first Friday of each month and are a key labor-market indicator. Job creation is trending down, with some months of net job destruction.

Slides 11-12: The Sahm rule and other recession indicators

  • Sahm rule (Claudia Sahm): a recession has normally begun when the 3-month moving average of the unemployment rate rises by 0.5 percentage points or more above its lowest level in the previous 12 months.
  • Application in Sep 2026: the lowest 3-month average in the past 12 months was 4.2%, and the latest 3-month average is 4.2%, so the rule shows no recession.
  • "Two recession indicators have flashed red or yellow": housing starts and the Sahm rule. In class she said both had flashed in 2024 but have since calmed down. In her view there is no real chance of a recession right now.

Slides 13-14: Labor markets in Trump 2.0; the AI hiring boom

  • Manufacturing employment has shrunk since the tariffs: "you don't save manufacturing with tariffs."
  • Demand for construction and infrastructure workers (data centers, power generation) is high, at high wages. White-collar AI jobs are growing too: engineers, data annotators, heads of AI.
  • About 1% of professional jobs are now AI jobs, rising to 4-5% in computers and life sciences. Higher productivity also creates jobs, and so far these gains are much bigger than the job losses from AI.

Slide 15: Headline and core inflation

  • Headline inflation covers all prices. Core inflation strips out fresh food and energy (fuel).
  • Latest figures: headline 3.4%, core 2.4%. Both are above the 2% target. Much of the inflation comes from food and fuel, and fuel also raises food prices through transport and fertilizer costs. Even core inflation is too high.

Slides 16-17: Affordability; the war in Iran

  • Affordability is the biggest political issue going into the November 2026 midterms. The war in Iran has made it much worse through fuel prices, and California prices are higher because of its gas tax.

Slide 18: The US Phillips curve, "nonexistent?"

  • The Phillips curve is the inverse relationship between unemployment and inflation.
  • For the US from 1978 to 2026 (World Bank data), the fitted line slopes slightly upward (y = 0.1115x + 2.9976, R² = 0.005), so there is effectively no Phillips curve. A separate 21st-century chart (2000 onward) does show the normal downward slope.

Slides 19-21: Spending, revenues, deficits; Covid stimulus

  • Government spending (all levels, everything) is about 40% of GDP, and revenues (every tax) are about 32% of GDP, so the gap is the deficit.
  • Spending peaks in the 2009 and Covid crises reflect deliberately expansive fiscal policy.
  • Comparison of fiscal deficits: Japan, USA, Eurozone 17, China, India.
  • The huge US Covid stimulus drove a fast recovery but boosted both deficits and debt.

Slides 22-25: US debt at its highest level ever; international comparison

  • Debt is at a record in absolute terms (above $40 trillion) and as % of GDP, and it is projected to keep climbing (to about 150% of GDP in the 2030s, per class).
  • To compare countries you must use debt as % of GDP, not dollars. On that measure the US is now 4th among developed countries, after Japan, Greece and Italy. In 2016 it was below many of them.

Slide 26: Why is US debt so high? The business cycle?

  • A fiscal deficit in a recessionary gap is appropriate. A fiscal deficit in an inflationary gap is inappropriate.
  • The ideal is cyclical balance: deficits in recessionary gaps and surpluses in inflationary gaps. The US deficit grew even during the post-Covid inflationary gap, so the deficit is structural.

Slides 27-29: Interest payments; nominal GDP growth vs interest rates

  • Net interest payments as % GDP (USA, Japan, Eurozone 17, UK): US payments "have not been high... but they will be".
  • Debt rule: if nominal GDP growth exceeds the interest rate on the debt, the debt is broadly sustainable, because the debt ratio tends to shrink. US nominal GDP normally grows faster than long-term interest rates, but the margin narrows as rates rise and inflation falls.

Slides 30-32: Is it because the US spends a lot? How the US spends

  • Government spending as % GDP: the US is the lowest of the group (Japan, Spain, Sweden, UK, US, Euro area 17), roughly matching Japan. So high spending is not the explanation.
  • Federal spending breakdown: social or "welfare" payments 68%, military 13%, interest 11%.
  • OECD social spending by category (Australia, Canada, Colombia, France, Germany, Italy, Japan, Mexico, Spain, Sweden, UK, USA, OECD): the US spends little on old age, unemployment and incapacity but a lot on public health.

Slides 33-34: Why Europeans support a generous welfare state: attitudes

Statement (% answering yes)USAEU
The poor are trapped in poverty29%60%
Luck determines income30%54%
The poor are lazy60%24%
  • The two sides also view the role of government differently: Americans prioritize freedom and opportunity, Europeans prioritize equality and guaranteeing that no one is in need.

Slides 35-36: Is the deficit due to low taxes? US tax rates are low

  • Tax revenues as % GDP (Japan, UK, Eurozone 17, Spain, Sweden, USA): the US is the lowest. The Eurozone is near 50% and Sweden is high.
  • US personal income tax rates are low, especially on the rich and on corporations. A chart shows the Reagan-era cuts.

Slide 37: When is a public debt a problem? (checklist, with US answers)

QuestionUS answer (deck)
How large is the debt as % of nominal GDP?Gross debt passed 100% of GDP in 2012 (its highest ever in peacetime) and passed 125% in 2025
What currency is it in?Dollars, still the world's reserve currency
How much does it cost to finance?The debt-service/GDP ratio was relatively low but is rising, and market turbulence has pushed rates up
Why did it rise? Was that appropriate?A clear and growing structural deficit: Republicans keep cutting taxes and Democrats keep spending
Is nominal growth above interest rates?US growth has been higher than in the EU or Japan, and inflation usually higher
Overall: sustainable, repayable?Low tax rates mean taxes could be raised. Pension liabilities will rise as baby boomers retire, though ageing is less severe than in the EU or Japan
  • Open question on the slide: does debt have to be repaid in a country with a strong currency and a credible central bank? (the market monetarists' view)

Slides 38-39: Monetary policy and Fed independence

  • Arguments for higher rates: inflation is well above target. Unemployment is at the NAIRU. Against: the economy is slowing, which argues for cuts. Trump has pressed for much lower rates and threatened the Fed's independence.
  • Only Fed action has brought inflation back toward target, which supports confidence in the US economy and the dollar. Less Fed independence combined with expansive fiscal policy would "practically guarantee" overheating and higher inflation.

Slides 40-43: The current account and its risks (shown in S6)

  • The US has run a chronic current-account deficit (OECD chart, % GDP).
  • Diagram: excess demand in the big spenders stimulates growth in the big savers, who lend their excess savings to the spenders so they keep buying. Debt builds up on the spender side.
  • Global imbalances, 2024. Biggest surpluses: China $424bn, Germany $270bn, Japan $194bn, Netherlands $111bn, Ireland $105bn, South Korea $99bn, Singapore $96bn, UAE $80bn (sum about $1.38trn). Biggest deficits: USA -$1.18trn, UK -$80bn, Brazil -$66bn, Australia -$35bn, India -$32bn (sum about -$1.4trn).
  • Deficit and surplus country characteristics are compared in the S6 unit (same slide in both decks).

Slides 44-46: Tariffs

  • The tariff wars may destroy the globalized system the US created after WWII. They are more disruptive than any earlier tariffs because the world is so globalized. Tariffs usually reduce growth and raise inflation.
  • Even though Trump put his "TACO" tariffs on hold and partly reversed them, the uncertainty harmed markets and trade prospects. The US Supreme Court declared them illegal, but he vows to continue. Tariffs are at levels not seen since the 1800s.
  • Tariffs are meant to bring back US manufacturing, but that is "almost impossible" (Ease of Doing Business ranking chart).

Slides 47-50: Inequality

  • Gini chart (Japan, UK, India, Spain, US, China): the US is the most unequal in the group.
  • Before taxes and transfers, the US is more equal than Britain, France, Germany and Denmark. Those countries tax and transfer more aggressively and end up with greater net equality.
  • Inequality threatens social peace and democracy and can slow growth. The US middle class is shrinking.
  • Problems: less inclusive growth is slower and less sustainable, and inequality fuels unrest and populism. Remedies: more progressive taxes, a tax-code overhaul, better education and jobs (the most lasting fix), wage subsidies for low incomes, and training and placement spending (ALMP).

Slides 51-53: How does the single US monetary policy work? (shown in S6)

  • Huge federal fiscal transfers smooth differences among states. Net transfers through the EU budget are far smaller (chart, % of national GDP, 2007-2010).
  • US households pay most of their taxes to the federal government, which redistributes them through unemployment benefits, food aid and disaster relief. Quote on the slide: what Germans might call a permanent bailout, "we just call Missouri".
  • US labor is also more mobile, which a single monetary policy needs in order to work.

Slides 54-57: Demography and immigration

  • The 2020 census counted 331.4m people, up from 308.7m in 2010 (+7.35%). Density is low (87.4 per sq. mile). Unlike Europe and parts of Asia, the population is still growing.
  • With fewer immigrants the demographic profile worsens. The impact of immigration controls could end up greater than the impact of the tariffs.

Slide 58: The US is still in a privileged position

  • The economy is huge, relatively closed and rich in resources. Underlying inflation was usually low, growth higher than in other developed countries, and unemployment at record lows.
  • The dollar is still the main reserve currency. Taxes are low, so raising them would cut the deficit and leave room for fiscal policy.
  • Population growth is strong (0.6%) and the fertility rate (1.8) is one of the highest in the developed world. The US is the world tech leader and the home of AI.
  • Closing slides: "The US right has diverged from the developed world."

The case, section by section

Introduction: model to emulate or avoid?

  • The US is the world's largest economy (nearly a quarter of nominal world output) and leads in high tech, innovation and R&D. Before Covid it had one of the lowest unemployment rates and a top-tier per-capita income.
  • Critics point to weak protection against poverty, job loss and illness, overconsumption that endangers financial stability, and the least equal income distribution in the developed world.

Diversity and development: no barriers to growth

  • The factors of growth are land and natural resources, labor, capital, technology and the quality of institutions. The young US was abundant in all of them.
  • Institutions: British property rights, the world's oldest liberal democracy, and small land grants that created a landed middle class that supported democracy and markets. There was no feudal aristocracy.
  • "Neo-Europes" with inclusive institutions are contrasted with extractive colonies (Acemoglu, Johnson, Robinson). Douglass North: better institutions and secure property rights bring more investment in physical and human capital.
  • Labor: more than 33 million immigrants arrived between 1820 and 1920, and by 1910, 40% of the population was foreign-born or had a foreign-born parent. Immigrants make up 13-14% of the population in the 21st century. Literacy around 1850 was 85-90%, among the highest anywhere.
  • Slavery: about 661,000 Africans were imported. The South specialized in export crops and lagged in industrializing.
  • Capital and technology came from Britain. Resources: the US was the leading mineral economy by 1913 (65% of world petroleum output).
  • Other advantages: a large, connected domestic market and no external military threats. Per-capita income grew about 1.5% a year in the 19th century, the US passed the UK in GDP per capita by 1913, and it began shifting to services by the 1920s.
  • Today immigration keeps the population younger, eases pension pressure, adds skilled workers and makes labor highly mobile. Drawbacks: ethnic tension and rising inequality. The pay gap between college graduates and non-graduates has doubled since 1979.

GDP and well-being: the problems of an indicator

  • The US has about 4% of world population and about a quarter of nominal world GDP. At PPP, China has surpassed it.
  • GDP per capita is a better welfare measure than total GDP. The US ranks slightly lower on the HDI, and its ranking drops 45 places on the planetary-pressures-adjusted HDI (PHDI).
  • It has the highest Gini in the developed world. The middle class has shrunk to about 59% of the population (vs about 80% in Norway, Denmark and the Netherlands), and political polarization and populism have followed (Rodrik).
  • Easterlin paradox: more GDP per capita does not reliably bring more happiness. "Deaths of despair" (Case and Deaton).

Policy and economic performance

  • Growth has been above most developed countries, inflation subdued (the "Amazon effect" and "China effect" plus good monetary policy) and pre-pandemic unemployment the lowest since 1969.
  • The US weathered 2001, 2009 and Covid relatively well.
  • A single monetary policy works across a diverse economy because of flexible labor markets (one language and culture), freedom of trade and enterprise, and federal fiscal transfers that dwarf the EU budget's.
  • Agile fiscal policy: Covid stimulus totalled about 25% of GDP, and some estimated the third package was three times what was needed. Spillovers helped China and the world.
  • Public debt reached 130% of GDP by early 2021, a peacetime record. Deficits even at or above potential mean a structural deficit, aggravated by the 2017 Trump tax cuts. Other large developed countries also run chronic structural deficits.
  • Why debt is less worrying than the ratio suggests: nominal GDP growth exceeds interest rates (Krugman: the debt "melts" rather than snowballs), all of it is in dollars (no exchange-rate risk for the US, though foreign holders bear it), and taxes are low, so they could be raised. The case argues that borrowing at near-zero rates is financially intelligent.
  • The US is almost the only developed country with nearly no legal protection against dismissal (only the WARN Act's 60-day notice).

Overconsumption and the twin deficits

  • Consumers, investors and government together spend more than they earn.
  • The current account moved from surplus up to the 1980s to large deficits after the Reagan tax cuts of 1981 and 1986. It reached nearly 6% of GDP before 2008.
  • The underlying cause is overconsumption, not unfair trade: domestic demand exceeds GDP, so the gap is filled with imports paid for by foreign borrowing.
  • Twin deficits = the fiscal deficit plus the current-account deficit. Foreigners put their excess savings into US assets (Treasuries), so the US net foreign asset position worsens. Eventually income outflows could pressure the current account through the income account.
  • Government dissaving (a deficit above 15% of GDP in the pandemic) offsets private saving and keeps the current account in deficit.
  • The US supplies about one-third of world demand. If it corrected its overconsumption, the rest of the world would grow more slowly.

Automation, employment and a dearth of good jobs

  • Labor force participation is about 67%, and prime-age male participation has been below 90% since the financial crisis. Low unemployment hides discouraged workers.
  • Pay tracked productivity until the early 1970s and diverged after that. The bottom 90% gained only about 15% in more than three decades.
  • Manufacturing's share of employment fell from about a quarter to under a tenth while its share of real GDP stayed flat. Most studies blame automation more than globalization. McKinsey estimates 15% of work hours and about 400m workers displaced by 2030. Emerging economies face "premature de-industrialization."
  • Policy options: a higher minimum wage, lower taxes on labor, education and training, relocation aid, the EITC (a wage subsidy that keeps low-wage workers employed), UBI (costly and may weaken work incentives), a wealth tax (Piketty) and a global minimum corporate tax (OECD).

A different model of advanced capitalism: an alternative to the welfare state

  • The US has no large universal welfare state. Public services are mostly means-tested (free only if you prove you are poor). The exceptions are old-age pensions and basic medical care for the retired. This model gives the US lower spending and taxes than Europe.
  • It spends little on old age, unemployment and disability but has some of the highest health spending, without universal coverage. Total health spending (public plus private) is the highest in the world. Obamacare (the ACA) extended coverage, Trump partly reversed it, and Biden promised to expand it again.
  • Public social spending is under 19% of GDP, but net social spending (adding private spending and charity and adjusting for taxes on benefits) is the second highest after France.
  • Why a different model: a preference for opportunity over equality (the "American dream"; table of US vs EU attitudes); racial and ethnic diversity reducing support for transfers (Alesina and Glaeser); and the Reagan-era and Tea Party push for small government.
  • When the economic center hollows out, the political center shrinks too.

What the professor added in class

  • EXAM: Course framing: the reason to use this course is to evaluate an investment (a country, an instrument, an asset class), so every indicator should be read as risk or opportunity. The final simulation game uses real countries with invented names, analyzed from data only.
  • She starts with the US because it is about 25% of the world economy and a fairly free-market economy, so the textbook relationships show up in its data.
  • Growth: India is the fastest in her sample and Spain is outgrowing the US. Germany and much of Europe are struggling. China has slowed sharply, its 2025 figure is probably overstated, and investment is collapsing.
  • EXAM: Potential GDP growth = productivity growth + labor force growth. Spain grows fast because immigration expands its labor force, and Latin American immigrants share the language and culture, which eases assimilation. Potential is a moving target that shifts with productivity and demography. US productivity is strong now thanks to tech innovation.
  • From an investor's view growth is good (markets, easier debt repayment) as long as it is not far above potential, which would mean an inflationary gap.
  • China aside: government, not markets, finances its AI and tech investment, so it does not pull back when returns disappoint. That is why China ends up with oversupply. Compare US high-speed rail, slowed by expensive land that must be bought and by spending that needs voter approval.
  • EXAM: To rank countries by size you must use nominal GDP in a common currency (US dollars), not real GDP. The US was about 25% of world GDP in 1980 and still is today. China's rise took share mainly from Europe (UK, France, Italy) and Japan. State comparisons: California is the size of a major economy, Texas about Canada, Florida about Mexico. China is larger than the US only at PPP.
  • EXAM: Annualized growth means what annual growth would be if the latest quarter's pace continued for four quarters. It is a projection, more forward-looking than backward-looking. "Always read your labels." Q2 2026 annualized growth was only 1.5%.
  • Headwinds: tariffs, immigration policy and high fuel prices (California: about $6/gallon for regular and $9 for diesel). Tailwind: the tech sector. Which force wins is uncertain. Apple shows tariffs can move production from China to India rather than to the US.
  • EXAM: The current situation looks like mild stagflation, caused by a supply shock (oil). The central bank's dilemma: tighten to tame inflation or ease to protect growth. The Fed recently raised rates (she believed unanimously) because even core inflation is too high. She saw that as a sign the institutions are holding up despite Trump appointing someone he hoped would cut rates.
  • Health and pharma: Europeans pay less for drugs because their governments negotiate fixed prices as large single buyers. In effect American consumers subsidize Europeans through Big Pharma's US profits. Biden controlled the insulin price and Trump let it float again; she knew two people who moved to Spain to afford insulin. Every country is capitalist and chooses which sectors it will not leave to the market. Europe and Canada made healthcare public, while the US let capitalism run it, with exceptions.
  • NAIRU: most institutions no longer estimate it. Her chart extended a trend line beyond 2023. The US is "very close to just right," a little below.
  • Sahm rule: she calculated it herself. The lowest 3-month average was 4.2% and the latest is 4.2%, so there is no signal. In 2024 the Sahm rule and building permits both pointed to recession. She thought there was no possibility of a recession now. Oil prices have to stay high for 3-6 months before they do real damage.
  • Manufacturing is declining in every advanced economy and moves to lower-wage countries. The biggest recent manufacturing job losses were in China.
  • AI: the expectation was that AI would destroy jobs, but the US, where AI is most developed, is having a hiring boom. AI security jobs pay six figures at entry level. Productivity growth lets the economy grow faster with less inflation. (She promised to post an article comparing AI regulation in China and the US.)
  • EXAM: Headline vs core: core strips out fresh food and fuel. Policymakers ask where inflation comes from. If it comes from outside (fuel and food supply shocks), cooling demand will not remove it, so they might look through it. This time core was also too high, so the Fed acted.
  • Phillips curve: her own 1978-2026 data gave no Phillips curve (the line slopes upward). The 2000-onward subset slopes down.
  • EXAM: Fiscal: spending is about 40% of GDP and taxes about 32% of GDP. The US has run deficits essentially every year since 1961, with one tiny surplus. Near potential, the deficit should shrink toward balance, and it has not.
