  • EXAM: Inflation helps the debt and deficit ratios "accidentally": the ratios are divided by nominal GDP, so higher inflation inflates the denominator and makes any given deficit or debt look smaller. Recent improvements in the US, Eurozone and Japan are partly this effect, so we should not get too excited. She joked that governments would love inflation to continue.
  • In Covid the US spent far more than Europe and pulled itself right out. The parties alternate: Democrats raise taxes and spending, Republicans cut both. The pattern follows 4-year terms, with each side reversing the other.
  • The "fiscal impact" measure (the combined effect of changes in spending and taxes on GDP) is currently negative, mainly because of cuts to Medicaid, health subsidies, USAID and food aid.
  • EXAM: Debt: the US passed $40 trillion "the other day." Compare countries by debt/GDP: the US is above 120% (her chart said 127%) and 4th in the developed world after Japan, Greece and Italy, "not a very nice club." In S4 she said Japan was about 200%. Reagan cut taxes drastically (some rates by about 50%), and the US never again balanced spending and taxes. Debt is projected at about 150% of GDP in the 2030s. War debt was once financed with war bonds.
  • There is no plan to reduce the debt, which is why bond markets demand higher yields (the 10-year yield is about 5%). Markets may be the only force that compels action. Raising taxes is the democratic dilemma, because voters punish it. Examples of state ballot propositions: California's carbon/fuel tax and a proposed one-time 2% wealth tax in California, with wealth measured as of early in the year so people cannot escape it by moving (details UNSURE).
  • EXAM: Debt sustainability rule: if nominal GDP growth is higher than the interest rate, debt is probably sustainable. It held for the US up to 2020 and still holds thanks to inflation, but rates have risen, so "if we get inflation down, we're going to have a problem."
  • EXAM: High spending is not why the US has a deficit. US government spending is the lowest in her group (matching Japan; Spain about 43%, Eurozone and Sweden near 50%). China's and India's government spending is lower still. The federal budget goes 68% to social payments, 13% to defense (one reason Trump went after NATO) and 11% to net interest.
  • EXAM: Health: US public health spending is about 8% of GDP and covers only the old (Medicare) and the poor (Medicaid). Germany covers everyone for about 8% of GDP. Care is privately provided and US prices are not capped the way Europe caps them: an operation that costs about $5,000 in Europe can cost $40,000-50,000 in the US. She called this probably the most serious US problem. Medical debt and "health bankruptcy" are common, and you are "one medical emergency away from homelessness."
  • EXAM: The welfare state grows with income across countries (Colombia and Mexico spend much less than rich countries), but the US has held its welfare state back. It spends more than anybody on health, and that spending covers only a fraction of what a welfare state covers.
  • Obamacare kept healthcare private but subsidized insurance for people up to roughly average income, covering many for the first time. Republicans have removed the subsidies, so people pay more out of pocket, which is one reason health spending is rising.
  • EXAM: Attitudes explain the different models. Americans tend to say the poor are not trapped, luck does not determine income, the poor are lazy, and government should provide freedom and opportunity. Europeans say the opposite and want government to guarantee no one is in need. She linked this to the US being an immigrant society ("my grandfather came with nothing").
  • EXAM: Taxes as % of GDP are the lowest in her group (Eurozone near 50%), so the US could fix the deficit "if we had the will to raise taxes." The implicit bet is that borrowing from the rest of the world beats taxing, because the US will grow faster and repay more easily. She called this "not irrational" but a gamble.
  • Class vote: does the US have a public debt problem? Her answers on the checklist: size about 125% of GDP and 4th in the developed world (a problem); currency is all dollars, still the global reserve currency (a reason not to worry); financing costs are rising (about $1 trillion a year in interest, similar to the military budget; "watch that"); taxes are low, so there is room to raise them; the cause is a structural deficit since the 1960s with divided parties.
  • If the AI bubble burst it would hit stock-market wealth, then consumption and investment, and could cause a recession. Foreigners are heavily invested in US assets, so the damage would spread abroad. P/E ratios are high but not at dot-com levels.
  • Closing idea: the rest of the world needs the US to be the overspender, the consumer of last resort. The cake analogy: surplus countries produce more cake than they eat and need someone to eat the extra.

Likely exam angles

  • Which comparison of the EU and US is correct? US government spending and taxes as % GDP are lower than the EU's. The US has welfare programs, but they are means-tested and target the old and the poor rather than being universal.
  • Why can the US run one monetary policy across very different states? Labor mobility plus large federal fiscal transfers, which the EU lacks at that scale.
  • US twin deficits: a fiscal deficit plus a current-account deficit, rooted in overconsumption (low national saving, government dissaving), financed by foreigners buying Treasuries.
  • When is US debt sustainable? When nominal GDP growth exceeds the interest rate on the debt. Debt in its own reserve currency and low taxes also reduce the risk.
  • Sahm rule: a recession signal when the 3-month average unemployment rate rises 0.5 points or more above its 12-month low. In Sep 2026, 4.2 vs 4.2 meant no signal.
  • Core vs headline: core excludes fresh food and energy. When headline is above core, food and fuel are driving inflation. Supply-shock inflation is hard to fight with rate hikes.
  • Which measure to rank economies by size? Nominal GDP in a common currency (PPP gives a different ranking: China above the US).
  • Deficit in an inflationary gap = inappropriate and structural. Deficit in a recessionary gap = appropriate (countercyclical).
  • Short answer, EU vs US: compare spending and taxes (US about 40% and 32% of GDP vs Eurozone near 50%), the welfare model (means-tested vs universal), health (US 8% public for the old and poor vs Germany 8% for everyone), attitudes (opportunity vs equality), inequality (US more equal before taxes and transfers, less equal after), labor mobility and fiscal transfers, and debt.
  • Short answer, investor risk indicators for the US: growth vs potential (output gap), unemployment vs NAIRU, the Sahm rule, core inflation vs the 2% target, the structural deficit, debt/GDP and nominal growth vs interest rate, the current-account deficit and dollar reserve status, Fed independence, tariffs, inequality and polarization, and demography and immigration.

Traps and look-alikes

  • "The US has no welfare programs" is false. It has a means-tested safety net, and its net social spending is the second highest in the OECD after France (case). The sample EU vs US question uses this trap.
  • US debt/GDP: case 130% (early 2021); deck "surpassed 125% in 2025"; class "above 120%" with a chart showing 127%. Use about 125% for 2025-26.
  • Japan debt in S4 class: "Japan is 200." In S6 class and the Japan deck: close to 250% / 248%. Trust the Japan deck.
  • Output gap: the deck says the US has been in a positive (inflationary) gap since Covid on both OECD and IMF estimates. In class she said the latest estimates show it near potential, one with a small negative gap. Both agree the post-Covid period was inflationary.
  • The Sahm rule is a rise of 0.5 points above the 12-month low of the 3-month average, not a fall. (The student's own notes said "0.5 lower": wrong.) It uses a 3-month moving average, not a single month.
  • Core inflation excludes food and energy. It is not "core = food and fuel only." Headline 3.4% vs core 2.4%. Do not swap them.
  • Debt rule: compare nominal GDP growth (not real) with the interest rate.
  • Annualized growth projects one quarter's pace over a year. It is not the growth achieved over the last year.
  • Rankings use nominal GDP in dollars, not real GDP and not PPP. At PPP China is ahead of the US.
  • Inflation improving the debt ratio is "accidental," not a sign of fiscal discipline.
  • Low US spending, not high spending, plus low taxes: the deficit comes from taxes, not spending.
  • Inequality: the US is more equal before taxes and transfers than the UK, France, Germany and Denmark. It is less equal after them. The highest Gini in the developed world is not the highest in the world.
  • Twin deficits = fiscal + current account. They are not the trade deficit plus the financial account. The case blames overconsumption, not unfair trade, and automation more than globalization for lost manufacturing jobs.
  • Tax revenue: the S4 transcript once reads "about 3%", a garble of 32% (she later said "the tax rate is only 32%").
Session 5 · 23 Sep 2026 · Garland (UNSURE, but strongly indicated: the speaker refers back to his own Sessions 1-2, mentions "you did talk with Gail about the US", and says he will teach Angola next) · complete notes

Session 5: GDP, GNP, GNI, PPP, openness, and the German export engine (Prof. Gonzalo Garland)

The session had two halves. First, a concept block building on Session 1: GDP per capita, GNP, and why the World Bank now ranks countries by GNI per capita (Atlas and PPP), and the degree of openness (trade-to-GDP ratio) and what drives it. Second, the HBS case "The German Export Engine" (Trumbull, Schlefer and Reinert, rev. Oct 2024): unification under Bismarck and Friedrich List's infant-industry logic, Weimar hyperinflation, the post-war "German Model" (relationship banking, codetermination, unions, Mittelstand, vocational training), the costly 1:1 reunification, the euro, and today's stagnation (energy, Chinese EVs, AfD). Exam relevance: GDP, GNI and PPP and openness are ideal MCQ material. Germany feeds the "growth strategies in the 20th century" and EU-versus-US topics. The closing discussion of fixed and undervalued currencies (Japan 360, China 8.28) leads into the exchange-rate sessions.

What the professor taught in class, in order

1. GDP and PPP recap

  • At market exchange rates, GDP is overestimated for expensive countries and underestimated for cheap ones. India's GDP multiplies by about 4 at PPP because it is valued at US prices. The US does not change.
  • Today, among the larger economies, only Switzerland is still more expensive than the US. Even Norway, once dearer, is now cheaper, meaning US prices have risen.

2. GDP per capita, GNP, GNI

  • GDP per capita = GDP divided by population. It was the standard way of comparing living standards and classifying countries 20-30 years ago.
  • GNP (gross national product): output by nationality, not location. In his IBM example, the profits of IBM's Mexican operation are produced inside Mexico, so they count in Mexico's GDP, but they belong to US owners, so they count in US GNP. The same applies to wages earned by foreign nationals.
  • GNP is "almost impossible to measure". You would need to know, for example, the wage of a Spanish chef in a restaurant in Thailand. GNP still exists but is little used.
  • GNI (gross national income): "counts only the amount that really moves from one place to another". Start with GDP, subtract money leaving (remittances sent abroad, profits repatriated) and add money arriving (remittances received, profits returning home).
  • Mexico example (numbers as Garland intended; the transcript is partly garbled): Mexican immigrants produce 40 in the US. All 40 is US GDP. If 30 is spent in the US and 10 is sent home as remittances, those 10 become income (GNI) of Mexico.
  • A student asked whether remittances are GDP. Garland: no. GDP is production. Remittances are income. They contribute to GDP only indirectly, if they are spent (not if saved).
  • Mexico receives the most remittances from the US in absolute terms, but relative to GDP El Salvador and Haiti are far higher (and the Philippines too). El Salvador: GDP about $100bn plus remittances worth almost 30% more, which makes "a huge difference" to purchasing power. So GDP per capita is misleading for such countries, and GNI per capita is better.
  • Garland's verdict: moving from GDP or GNP per capita to GNI per capita makes sense. It is more representative and more accurate, because cross-border payments are easier to record (banks, Western Union-type transfers, with some cash carried by hand). The World Bank now uses GNI per capita to classify countries.
  • On measurement in general: no measure is perfect, and perfection would cost too much. Measurement is like mapping, always imperfect, and sampling is fine.
  • The highest GNI per capita includes tax havens (Bermuda first, the Isle of Man, the Cayman Islands, the Faroe Islands), because profits are booked there. Then Switzerland (about $110,000 per person), Norway, Luxembourg and Iceland. The US is about 6th.
  • GNI per capita at PPP (World Bank, US prices): the cheaper countries rise. Singapore rises to about $135,715 and becomes the highest. Qatar goes from about $74,000 to about $126,000, almost doubling. Norway is about $107,000. Switzerland is the only one that falls (about 110 to 109 thousand). Germany is about $78,000 at PPP. India: about $2,760 rises to about $11,600 (×~4).
  • World GDP: $118tn at market rates becomes $211tn at PPP, so most countries are cheaper than the US.

3. Degree of openness (trade-to-GDP ratio)

  • Degree of openness = (X + M) / GDP.
  • Garland's example: country A has C 500, I 300, G 200, X 100, M 100, so GDP is 1,000 and openness is 200/1,000 = 20%. Country B has C 500, I 300, G 200, X 500, M 500, so GDP is also 1,000 but openness is 1,000/1,000 = 100%. The same GDP can come with very different openness.
  • Germany (case Exhibit 1, 2022): exports 50.6% and imports 48.7% of GDP, so openness is about 100%, "super open" for a large economy. That brings big benefits and big risks if export markets weaken.
  • Rest of Germany's GDP (2022): private consumption about 51% and government about 22%. In the US, private consumption is about two-thirds (67-70%) of GDP. That is why Black Friday and Christmas spending matter so much there.
  • What drives openness: (1) trade policy (trade agreements, tariffs, protectionism). A student also raised capital controls, which Garland said relate to capital flows rather than directly to trade in goods and services. (2) Size and resource endowment: a very large, resource-rich country (the US) produces almost everything it needs, such as oil, copper, wheat and rice, so it trades less. The "size" factor depends on resources: Russia is huge but exports oil and imports a lot.
  • Traditionally low openness: Japan (size plus a dislike of foreign products), Brazil, and the US (his recollection: about 30-40%). India before 1991 was about 22-23%, among the world's lowest, under the protectionist Permit Raj. Openness rose sharply after the 1991 reform. A student found a current figure of about 46% for India (UNSURE). China is an exception: very export-oriented but large, so roughly 50-60% (his estimate).
  • Germany is the exception among big countries: large and very open.

4. Germany: history (case plus class)

  • Student update (Natalie, a German student): Friedrich Merz's CDU won but governs with the SPD, which limits reform. The AfD is rising in state elections (it won Mecklenburg-Vorpommern) and the Left won Berlin, where the CDU struggled with the 5% hurdle for the first time (UNSURE detail). The economy has stagnated since Covid and the Ukraine invasion, with high energy costs pushing production abroad and difficulty attracting talent. Garland added competition from Chinese electric cars as a threat to the core car industry.
  • Germany is a young country: unified in 1871, around the same time as Italy (1870-71). Bismarck was the key figure. Unification happened during the Pax Britannica (1815-1914), a relatively peaceful free-trade era, but the Franco-Prussian War (1870) left France paying heavy reparations and losing territory.
  • Friedrich List: Britain preached free trade only after getting rich through intervention, "kicking away the ladder". Free trade only works between economies at similar levels of development. So Germany first formed a customs union (the Zollverein, 1834), built strength behind some protection, and opened up gradually once strong. "The land of big industry, big agriculture, big banks, and big government."
  • Bismarck's welfare state: to defuse the socialist movement he introduced health insurance and pensions financed by employer and employee payroll contributions. That is still how it works today, and it is the origin of the European welfare state.
  • Versailles and Weimar: reparations after WWI were too harsh. Keynes (for Britain) and the Americans (Wilson) argued for leniency, but France, punished in 1870, insisted. Weimar was culturally brilliant but an economic disaster: it could not pay, so it printed money and got hyperinflation, until 30-40 years ago the world record (Germany and Austria). The Nazis took power, stopped the printing and controlled inflation.
  • WWI killed about 20 million and WWII about 80 million. Germany was divided into West (US, UK and French zones) and East (Soviet). Before the wars, Germany had the world's best universities, especially in science.
  • Post-war: the Marshall Plan mattered for the recovery (Garland noted the case does not mention it), plus German merit. The CDU (new in 1945) and SPD became the main parties.

5. The German Model (class discussion)

  • Relationship banking (Hausbank): banks lend, own shares and sit on supervisory boards, so they care about the long-term health of firms rather than short-term returns. It is tiered: national, regional and local banks. German banks are weaker today, and there is an EU-level banking debate.
  • Two-tier boards: a supervisory board (strategy) and a management board (day-to-day).
  • Codetermination (Adenauer, 1951): 5 supervisory-board members chosen by labour, 5 by management, and a chair jointly chosen but approved by stockholders (effectively the deciding vote). At first only coal, iron mining and steel. Extended to other industries in 1952 and strengthened for all firms with more than 2,000 employees in 1976. Employee representatives have the same rights and confidentiality duties as shareholder representatives. Garland: very unique in the world, and it probably reduces union pressure.
  • Works councils at plant level (students called them a place to complain if HR does not help).
  • Unions (IG Metall, Ver.di and others): strong, yet labour relations are amicable. Germany lost only 11 days per 1,000 employees in the 1990s, against 40 in the US, 73 in France and 158 in Italy. Students cited a 90%+ vote needed for strikes (see Traps).
  • Recession response: US firms lay off fast, protect profits and rehire fast in recovery. Most European firms cannot fire, so their wage bill is stuck. German firms instead negotiate fewer hours and lower pay in exchange for no layoffs. Garland's example was Siemens in the 2008-09 crisis. This is unique to Germany.
  • Pensions: a student noted the replacement rate rose from 60% to 70% of pre-retirement wages (case: in 1972).
  • Industry is spread across regions (cars and engineering in the south, finance in Frankfurt, the port of Hamburg). It is export-led.
  • Mittelstand: small and mid-sized, highly technical firms that supply the majors and often lead the world in narrow niches. Garland recalled near-100% world shares in dental equipment and currency-printing machines. They also enjoy relationship banking.
  • Vocational education (the dual system): highly respected technical training alongside universities. Students are tracked after grades 9-10 into Gymnasium and the Abitur (university), Realschule or Hauptschule. There are about 400 recognized trades (case: 378), with some mobility: switching tracks, evening schools to retake exams later. Japan tracks even earlier. Spain constantly debates copying it. "At the end of the day, productivity is fundamental."
  • Undervaluation: under Bretton Woods (managed rates), low German inflation (never over 3.5% in the 1960s) kept the DM undervalued. With productivity growth and the EEC, exports rose from 8% of GDP (1950) to 24% (1974), and to about 50% by 2022. Many countries weakened their currencies on purpose: Germany, Japan and China. A student noted India's rupee at 35 per dollar years ago.

6. Reunification and the euro

  • Reunification (1990): more important politically than economically. To keep East German firms competitive, wages should have converted at about 5:1. For political reasons it was 1:1. East German firms, the strongest in the Soviet bloc, became uncompetitive overnight and lost their markets.
  • Today the East still has higher unemployment and lower wages, which some link to the AfD vote. West Germany subsidized the East heavily, so budget deficits rose and inflation pressure followed.
  • Inflation phobia: the Weimar hyperinflation explains why Germany and the Bundesbank are so strict on inflation (images of bread bought with suitcases of banknotes). The transcript's figures here are garbled (UNSURE).
  • Maastricht (1992): the road to a common currency by 1999, the change of name to the European Union (from the EEC), and the French and Danish referendums and monetary storms. The five criteria again: deficit 3%, debt 60% (Germany slightly above but close), 2 years in the EMS (no longer relevant inside the euro), and inflation and interest rates near the 3 lowest. Garland's thresholds, inflation +2 and interest +1.5, conflict with the case (see Traps).
  • Why the euro? The case says Mitterrand pushed a single currency in exchange for French support for reunification ("keep Germany under control"). Garland: "probably an element", but not the only one. Others are Mundell's "great powers have great currencies", transparency and price integration, and, in his opinion most important, ending competitive devaluations ("rob your neighbour") and consolidating the union.
  • A student argued Europeans have become weaker since the euro. Garland disagreed: Europe's relative decline is the Great Convergence (China, India, Brazil), not the euro, and Europe's per-capita income is still very high.
  • EXAM: the trade-off for Germany: without the euro, the DM would be much stronger. That would hurt exports and the surplus (partners complain Germany floods them with relatively cheap cars thanks to the euro) but make Germans richer internationally. The opposite holds for Spain, which runs trade deficits: without the euro a weaker peseta would boost exports, cut imports and favour production, but make Spaniards poorer. Trade on one side, wealth on the other.

7. Germany today

  • Still the strongest economy in Europe, but struggling with the energy crisis after the war in Ukraine. Some blame Schröder's gas deals with Russia, and Merkel's decision to shut down the nuclear plants is now widely questioned ("it made sense when Russia was a friend").
  • Immigration is a major issue, less than under Merkel, and Europe-wide.
  • The case ends with Germany as "the leading European power" whose lead France, Italy and Spain follow (Marine Le Pen's jibe). Garland called that an opinion.
  • Germany is raising military spending. Security is a big European issue.

8. Closing: fixed and undervalued exchange rates

  • Deliberate weakening: Germany (post-war), Japan (fixed at 360 yen per dollar), and China (fixed at 8.28 from 1995 to 2004).
  • China's peg flooded the world with cheap goods while it grew 9-10%. The US protested that China was growing by weakening its currency and not buying US goods during a US recession, and US pressure forced China to let the currency appreciate.
  • Saudi Arabia: fixed at 3.75 per dollar for about 40 years, but nobody objects because it exports little besides oil.
  • Lesson: a small country may be tolerated, but a big one faces global pressure.
  • The downside of a weak currency: external debt becomes more expensive and national wealth falls. More on fixed rates with Angola in the next session.

The case, section by section

Opening (2022)

  • Gazprom cut Nordstream I to 40%, German gas prices quintupled ($2 to $10 per Btu), and the Nordstream pipes were sabotaged on 26 Sep 2022 (culprit unclear). Scholz's SPD-Green-FDP coalition had to shift to zero carbon, keep industry competitive and hold the EU together.

Forging a nation

  • An industrial power before it was a nation: 39 German states after 1815, a sense of being "latecomers". List's National System of Political Economy and "kicking away the ladder". The Zollverein (1834), then unification (1871) after Bismarck's wars with Austria (1866) and France (1870). Steel output passed France's. Bayer, Hoechst, Siemens and Thyssen emerged. French reparations drove up the currency, so "iron and rye" (industry plus Junker agriculture) won the 1879 tariff, and state-backed cartels formed behind it. Germany overtook Britain in industrial output by WWI. "Finance capitalism" (Hilferding): banks supply capital, hold shares and sit on supervisory boards. Bismarck banned labour parties (1863) but created sickness insurance (1871 in the case), accident insurance and pensions over 70, all financed by payroll contributions, to stop the socialists.

The world wars

  • WWI (about 20 million dead), reparations, the Weimar Republic (cultural vibrancy, hyperinflation, extremism and antisemitism), the Nazis elected in 1933, WWII (about 80 million dead, about 3% of world population) and the Holocaust (6 million Jews).

The German miracle

  • Bombing destroyed 20% of industry and 80% of urban housing. The DM came in 1948 and the Basic Law in 1949 (Adenauer, CDU). The Bundestag and Bundesrat, a 5% threshold, and anti-democratic parties banned. Volksparteien and coalition politics. Erhard's hard money. Low inflation under Bretton Woods meant an undervalued DM, and exports rose from 8% to 24% of GDP (1950-74). Unemployment never exceeded 1% in the 1950s-60s. Growth averaged about 6%.

The Hausbank relationship

  • Relationship banking, against arm's-length Anglo-American finance. Two-level boards (Aufsichtsrat and Vorstand). In the mid-1990s Deutsche Bank chaired the supervisory boards of 29 of the top 100 firms, and about 60 of the top 100 were linked in one ownership network. The Mittelstand is financed through public savings banks. Industrial lending is roughly a third each from big private banks, savings banks, and cooperative and specialized banks.

Worker participation

  • Industry unions (IG Metall, Ver.di; DGB umbrella). Pattern bargaining: IG Metall and Gesamtmetall set the pattern, and agreements bind all firms in the employers' association, including non-union workers (about 80% coverage). Strike days: 11 (Germany), 40 (US), 73 (France), 158 (Italy). Codetermination (1951, extended 1952 and 1976). Mine and steel unions had voted by more than 90% to strike if necessary to win board representation. Works councils in plants with more than 5 employees (90% of firms with more than 500 have one). The dual vocational system: two-thirds of under-22s in vocational training, 378 occupations by the 1990s (1969 Vocational Training Act).

Social protection

  • Bismarck-style insurance: pensions at 60% of pre-retirement wages, raised to 70% in 1972. Universal health coverage through sickness funds. State-financed university.

The 1990s: the sick man of Europe

  • The 1979 oil shock and the Volcker rate hikes. An SPD Keynesian stimulus (deficits of 4-5%) offset by the Bundesbank (rates of 9%). Unemployment of 7%, then 9% in the 1990s, then 11% in 2005. Growth fell like a staircase: 8.2% (1950s), 2.3% (1980s), 1.9% (1990s), 1% (2000s). The three suspects: reunification, the welfare state and globalization.

Reunification

  • Kohl converted wages at 1:1 and assets at 2:1, when a realistic rate was about 5:1, so eastern industry collapsed. The Treuhand sold 8,444 firms, liquidated 3,718, laid off 3 million and ran a DM275bn deficit. 4 million eastern jobs were lost (1990-93) and eastern unemployment was 13.5-20.2% a decade later, against 4-10% in the west. The unemployed were moved into welfare and early retirement. Transfers ran at about 4% of GDP (about €100bn a year), and the total cost was about €1.7tn by 2009. Mitterrand traded French support for reunification for German acceptance of the euro.

A wave of reforms

  • Labour: union membership fell from 35% to 18%, but collective-agreement coverage held. Opening clauses traded concessions (wage limits, flexible hours) for job security (Siemens gave job security to 120,000 in 2010), and company agreements rose from 2,550 to 7,540 (1995-2003). The Mittelstand drifted out of sectoral bargaining. Mini-jobs (up to €400 a month, later €450, exempt from employee contributions, with a 20% employer tax) reached about 7 million workers, 20% of the workforce.
  • Corporate governance: a securities regulator (1994), the Neuer Markt (1997, closed 2003), and the 1998 law curbing bank board power and abolishing capital-gains tax on large stake sales. The effect on how firms actually ran was modest.
  • Welfare: pay-as-you-go. 100 workers supported 50 beneficiaries in 1970 and 69 by the end of the 1990s. Contributions rose from 27% to 40% of gross pay. Federal debt trebled (€536bn to €1.7tn, 1990-2009). The vicious cycle: more beneficiaries mean higher non-wage costs, which mean fewer jobs, which mean more beneficiaries. Agenda 2010 / Hartz (2003, Schröder): unemployment insurance for one year, "activation" (take any job), and a welfare floor.
  • Energy: feed-in tariffs (2000) brought renewables to 28.5% of electricity by 2014. Wholesale prices fell 30% while household rates rose 60%.

Crisis in Europe

  • The euro (2000). Exports surged even as the euro rose. A cumulative trade surplus of €1.4tn (2000-09), 60% of it with the EU. Manufacturing productivity grew 3.2% a year (1990-2008). German banks' exposure to the periphery was $704bn (2009), far above their capital. The Greek bailouts (€110bn, then a €750bn package) in effect also rescued German banks. Merkel backed the stabilization mechanism and the banking union (to break the bank-sovereign doom loop) and the 2012 Fiscal Compact (3%), but refused fiscal union, insisting on liberalization and austerity. At home: working-time accounts and short-time work (Kurzarbeit), with the top-up extended from 12 to 24 months and 1.5 million workers on it by May 2009. Unemployment fell during the crisis. A fiscal stimulus of about 5% of GDP and cash for clunkers (€2,500). A minimum wage of €8.50. Immigration rose (a net inflow of 445,000 in 2013).

A refugee crisis

  • 1.3 million asylum applications in the EU in each of 2015 and 2016, half of them in Germany. Merkel's "no upper limit". The Cologne New Year's Eve assaults shifted the mood. Schengen border controls returned. The EU-Turkey deal ($7bn). The AfD became the third-largest party in 2017 and fell back to fifth in 2021 (Scholz's SPD won).

Crisis from abroad and "where next?"

  • Covid: the ECB's PEPP (eventually €1,850bn), SURE (€100bn in loans), and Germany and the Netherlands objecting to corona bonds. Next Generation EU: €390bn in grants (the first EU grants ever) plus €360bn in loans. German packages of €346bn (10% of GDP) and loan guarantees of €750bn. Kurzarbeit paid 60%, then 70%, then 80% of pay. Russia's invasion brought the Zeitenwende (€100bn for the military), coal and LNG, and the €200bn energy "defence shield", which drew EU anger over market fragmentation. The US IRA ($450bn) and Northvolt hesitating. Habeck's "year of industrial policy" (2023). The irony that Germany now relies on the debt-rule exceptions it opposed. Debt of 69% of GDP (2021). The unresolved flaw: a single currency but multiple fiscal policies. The surplus sustains German demand but weakens partners'. Dehio: Germany is "too big for a balance of power in Europe and too small for hegemony".

What the professor added in class

  • EXAM: GDP is production inside the borders, GNP is output by nationality (hard to measure), and GNI is GDP minus income flowing out plus income flowing in. The World Bank classifies countries by GNI per capita.
  • EXAM: remittances are income, not production. They raise GNI (in Garland's framing), not GDP. El Salvador and Haiti are the examples where GDP per capita understates purchasing power.
  • EXAM: degree of openness = (X+M)/GDP. Germany is about 100%, and large, resource-rich countries (the US) are less open. Trade policy and size and resources are the two main factors. India was about 22-23% before 1991.
  • EXAM: the 1:1 reunification rate (instead of about 5:1) was a political choice that destroyed eastern competitiveness. The East still has lower wages and higher unemployment.
  • EXAM: Weimar hyperinflation explains Germany's inflation strictness (the Bundesbank, and its stance in the ECB).
  • EXAM: the euro trade-off: Germany would have a stronger DM (fewer exports, richer citizens) and Spain a weaker peseta (more exports, poorer citizens).
  • Germany's private consumption is only about 50% of GDP, against two-thirds in the US: an export-led versus a consumption-led economy. This is useful for EU-versus-US and Germany-versus-US comparisons.
  • Undervaluation strategies (Germany, Japan at 360, China at 8.28 from 1995 to 2004) work until the country is big enough to draw pressure. A weak currency raises the cost of external debt.
  • Garland's own opinion on the main reason for the euro: ending competitive devaluations, which is more important than the case's "keep Germany in check" story.

Likely exam angles

  • MCQ: IBM's profits from its Mexican plant count in Mexico's GDP and US GNP.
  • MCQ: for a country receiving large remittances (El Salvador), GNI per capita is above GDP per capita (class version).
  • MCQ: compute openness. With X = 500, M = 500 and GDP = 1,000, openness is 100%.
  • MCQ: which factor tends to make a country less open? Large size and a rich resource endowment (the US), plus protectionist trade policy.
  • MCQ: why did East German industry collapse after 1990? The 1:1 wage conversion, against the roughly 5:1 that productivity justified.
  • MCQ: what is codetermination? Workers elect about half of supervisory-board seats (the 1951 law: 5 + 5 + a chair approved by stockholders).
  • MCQ: how did German firms handle the 2008-09 recession? Fewer hours and lower pay instead of layoffs (working-time accounts and Kurzarbeit with state top-ups), so unemployment fell.
  • MCQ: List's argument is infant-industry protection first, free trade among equals later ("kicking away the ladder").
  • MCQ: what drove German exports under Bretton Woods? An undervalued DM produced by low inflation, plus productivity growth and the EEC.
  • Short answer, growth strategies in the 20th century: Germany shows coordinated capitalism (patient bank capital, codetermination, vocational skills, Mittelstand) plus an undervalued currency and export orientation. Contrast import substitution (India, Latin America) and the Asian export model.
  • Short answer, EU against US: the US is consumption-led (C about two-thirds of GDP) and hires and fires fast. Germany is export-led (C about 50%), adjusts through hours, and resists fiscal union (single currency, multiple fiscal policies).

Traps and look-alikes

  • GDP, GNP and GNI: location against nationality against actual cross-border income. A plant's output is always in the GDP of the country where it stands.
  • Remittances and GNI (conflict with the standard definition): Garland treats remittances received as part of GNI. In standard national accounts (and in the case's Exhibit 3, which separates "net primary income" from "net secondary income"), GNI = GDP + net primary income (wages and profits). Personal remittances are secondary income, which enters gross national disposable income, not GNI. For the exam, answer with Garland's version if the question follows his example. UNSURE how strictly it will be tested.
  • Transcript numbers on GNI per capita are partly garbled (UNSURE): for example, "Germany at $260,000" and Singapore's Atlas figure "$8,760" are obvious mis-transcriptions. Reliable figures from class: India $2,760 to $11,600 at PPP, Singapore $135,715 at PPP (the highest), Qatar $74k to $126k, Switzerland the only one that falls (110 to 109).
  • The "×4" for India applies to both GDP (Session 1: $4tn to $17tn) and GNI per capita (Session 5). The implied Indian price level is about 25% of the US's. China's is about 50%.
  • Openness uses X + M, not X - M. Net exports (X - M) enter the GDP identity, while openness adds them. Germany's 2022 net exports were only about 2% of GDP, yet openness was about 99%.
  • Trades: class said about 400 recognized trades, while the case says 378 formally defined occupations (1990s).
  • The "90%" figure: students said strikes need 90%+ member approval. The case says mine and steel unions voted by more than 90% to strike if necessary to win codetermination (board seats). The case does not state a general 90% strike rule (UNSURE whether class meant the general German rule).
  • Siemens example: Garland put Siemens's hours-and-pay deal in the 2008-09 crisis. The case cites Siemens granting unlimited job security to 120,000 workers in a 2010 opening-clause agreement, and credits Kurzarbeit and working-time accounts for 2008-09.
  • Bismarck's sickness insurance: the case says 1871. The standard historical date is 1883 (my outside knowledge). Use the case or avoid the year.
  • Maastricht thresholds: class said inflation +2 and interest +1.5, while the Spain case (and the treaty) says inflation +1.5 and interest +2. The same conflict appears in Session 2.
  • Why the euro: the case says Mitterrand's quid pro quo for reunification. Garland's view is that ending competitive devaluations was the main reason. If an MCQ asks "according to the case", pick the Mitterrand bargain.
  • The case says Adenauer was "elected president". He was chancellor (a case slip).
  • Unemployment peaked at 11% in 2005, not during reunification. During the 2008-09 crisis it fell (from 7% in 2008 to 5% in 2015) thanks to short-time work.
  • Do not mix up the two exchange-rate stories: undervaluation under Bretton Woods (good for exports) against the overvalued 1:1 eastern conversion (deadly for eastern firms).
Session 6 · 25 Sep 2026 · Allard · complete notes

Session 6: Japan, and deficit vs surplus countries (Prof. Gayle Allard)

Allard opened by finishing US material: global current-account imbalances, the characteristics of current-account deficit and surplus countries, and US federal fiscal transfers vs the EU budget. She then taught her deck "Japan: From the Miracle and the Bubble to a New World": the growth miracle and lost decade, Japan's three key indicators (GDP growth, inflation, current account), deflation, the NAIRU and Phillips curve, Abenomics' three arrows, fiscal deficits and the world's highest debt/GDP ratio, QE, Prime Minister Takaichi, and the carry trade. She ran out of time before the yen, structural reform, demography and immigration slides (Japan returns in Session 10; those slides are summarized below from the deck). There is no case for this session. Exam relevance: the sample MCQ asks for the main driver of Japan's chronic current-account surplus (the savings habits of the Japanese). The deficit vs surplus framework applies to the US, China, Germany and India too.

Slide-by-slide

Slide 2: Japan, the crisis and the future (agenda)

  • The growth miracle and the long crisis. The Japanese current account. Abenomics: QE pioneer, fiscal expansion and the world's largest public debt, juggling growth and fiscal sustainability, the yen, higher inflation and a weaker yen.
  • What Japan shows about the future of countries following the export-led rapid industrialization model (demography, pensions, demand and growth).

Slides 3-4: Real GDP growth, Japan vs China; recent growth

  • Penn World Tables chart labels a "Japanese miracle?" and a "Chinese miracle?", both followed by a downshift.
  • Low Japanese growth has picked up a little recently (OECD comparison with USA, China, India, Spain). Japan is the lowest almost every year after 1974.

Slide 5: GDP growth, inflation and current-account balance (the three indicators)

  • The key Japan dashboard: GDP growth, the inflation rate, and the current-account balance as % GDP.

Slide 6: Growth in real GDP, nominal GDP and GDP per capita

  • Real GDP growth was above nominal GDP growth for long stretches, which is very rare and is caused by deflation (the GDP deflator is negative). Things normalized from 2022 as inflation returned.
  • GDP per capita grows slightly faster than GDP because the population is shrinking.

Slides 7-8: Inflation, headline and core; comparison

  • First deflation in 1995. Since 2000, roughly 12 years of deflation and 12 of inflation. Maximum 2.77%, minimum -1.36% (headline, 21st century).
  • Core inflation was also negative, so it was domestic prices falling, not just oil or food. Japan is an outlier compared with the USA, China and India.

Slides 9-10: Unemployment vs NAIRU; Japan's Phillips curve

  • Japan's unemployment rate is well below the NAIRU, yet inflation did not take off for decades.
  • Japan's Phillips curve shows no normal inverse relationship. In class she said there is effectively no Phillips curve.

Slide 11: Gini coefficient (lower = more equal)

  • Countries: Germany, Japan, UK, India, Spain, US. The US is highest at 41.06, and Japan is quite equal.

Slide 12: Japan's form of capitalism

  • Once post-WWII recovery was over, Japan's form of capitalism and export-oriented growth surged ahead.
  • The US scores high on individualism. Asian nations score higher on paternalism, long-termism and community.
  • Wealth accumulates across generations in family businesses, creating dynastic corporate structures with little need to list on stock markets. Low market capitalization and trading volumes, which the West sees as weaknesses, are seen in Asia as strengths: long-term orientation, stability, immunity from market pressure.

Slide 13: Abenomics (2012+), the three arrows

  • A radical program to end stagnation and deflation. Goals: end deflation, weaken the yen, restart growth.
  • Arrow 1, fiscal expansion: the focus was spending. The sales tax was raised twice (2014 and 2019, to 10%) to help reduce the extremely high debt.
  • Arrow 2, more (and more!) QE.
  • Arrow 3, structural reform: the least talked-about arrow, which met much resistance.
  • Sanae Takaichi (the deck spells it "Sanai") is still committed to Abenomics-style policy.

Slides 14-16: Spending and revenues; fiscal deficits; deficit vs output gap

  • Tax revenue is always below spending, and both are below 40% of GDP (lower than Europe). There was a brief surplus around the late 1980s boom.
  • Fiscal deficit comparison: Japan, UK, US, Euro area, China, India.
  • Chart of the fiscal deficit against the output gap: a long recessionary gap explains years of deficits. The ideal is deficits only in recessionary gaps and surpluses in inflationary gaps.

Slides 17-18: The world's highest government debt as % GDP (2016 vs 2026)

  • Japan tops the OECD ranking of gross financial liabilities as % GDP.

Slide 19: Net government interest payments, % GDP

  • Very low, at times negative, now rising.

Slide 20: Does Japan have a public debt problem? (checklist)

QuestionJapan answer (deck)
How large as % of nominal GDP?The world's highest, but now falling as % GDP
What currency (domestic or foreign)?Yen, a reserve currency. No foreign debt
Who owns it (internal or external)?The Japanese private and public sectors own nearly all of it
How much does it cost to finance?The debt service/GDP ratio is very low
Why did it rise?The lost decade, the Asian crisis, the financial crisis and recession: a long recessionary gap
Sustainable, repayable?Pension liabilities will grow as baby boomers retire, so the fiscal position will worsen. But Japan has massive savings

Slides 21-22: The monetary arrow, rates at or below zero

  • Japanese interest rates were at or below zero for two decades. Inflation and inflation expectations have now risen.

Slides 23-24: QE

  • Japan "invented" quantitative easing (2000, per the Japan notes; the year is missing from the slide text) to fight deflation. Abenomics planned to double the monetary base again through the world's biggest QE program, which has since ended (year missing on the slide; see Traps).
  • The BoJ holds a massive share of government bonds.

Slide 25: How has policy changed under Takaichi?

  • She will continue fiscal expansion and even cut taxes despite the massive debt.
  • Debt has declined to 248% of GDP, and interest rates are at 31-year highs.
  • In August 2026 the US and Japan coordinated an unusual currency intervention to stop the yen's depreciation, after it hit its lowest value in 40 years.

Slide 26: How do these changes affect the world? The carry trade

  • The carry trade is the strategy of borrowing in a low-interest market and investing the funds in a high-interest market.
  • Japan was a perfect funding source: low and even negative rates, a large economy and free capital flows. Typical routes: yen or Swiss franc into the US dollar, Australian dollar or Mexican peso.
  • Since Japanese rates began rising in 2024 the carry trade has been unwinding, which could cause widespread disruption.

Slide 27: Structural reform, the neglected arrow

  • Incentives for capital investment, so companies spend at home rather than save or invest abroad.
  • Raising women's participation: large companies must publish the number of women on their boards, and women get preference in civil-service hiring. This reform has been very successful, and women's participation is now 10 points higher.
  • Reform of the electricity market, possibly breaking up conglomerates, deregulating over-the-counter pharmaceuticals, promoting large commercial farming, cutting rice subsidies, easing firing rules, and allowing immigration.

Slides 28-29: Freeing up the labor market; wages

  • In the high-growth past, talent was scarce and firms held on to workers with lifetime employment, which unions still defend.
  • The result is a dual labor market. Firms hire more and more low-paid "irregular" workers (40% of the employed). High temporary employment lowers the birth rate and consumption and causes social unrest. Proposal: allow layoffs with severance pay.

Slides 30-32: The current account and the yen; savings and investment

  • The current-account surplus gave Japan growth and strengthened the yen.
  • The root of a current-account surplus is a high savings rate, relative to investment. IMF charts compare gross national savings and savings vs investment for the USA and Japan: Japan saves more than it invests, while the US invests more than it saves.
  • Japan may eventually run a current-account deficit as it ages and becomes a net dissaver.

Slide 33: Remember the differences between deficit and surplus countries

CA deficit countries (e.g. US, India, UK)CA surplus countries (e.g. Japan, China, Germany)
Depend on foreign capital to finance their overspendingDepend on foreign demand and on the value of their currencies
Consume more than they produceProduce more than they consume
Can drive their own growth with domestic demandNeed foreign demand to keep GDP as high as it is
Must borrow abroad to sustain high consumptionMust lend to foreign buyers
If the rest of the world disappeared, their GDP would be higher (X-M is negative)If the rest of the world disappeared, their GDP would be lower
Currencies tend to fall as the deficit growsCurrencies tend to rise as the surplus grows
Often experience deflation

Slides 34-35: Currency value is key for a country relying on foreign demand

  • The yen is traditionally strong because of the export surplus. It appreciated about 50% in the mid-1980s under pressure from the US and other developed countries (the Plaza Accord episode, not named on the slide).
  • Many trace Japan's asset bubble, bust, lost decade and deflation to that drastic appreciation.
  • QE aimed to weaken the yen and boost growth through net exports. But Japan imports nearly all its energy, especially since the tsunami, so a weak yen makes energy expensive.
  • Since the pandemic the yen has fallen to historic lows, because Japan kept monetary policy expansive while other central banks raised rates. (Yen/USD chart since 1970.)

Slide 36: Why Japan is especially vulnerable to crises, especially trade conflicts

  • A tendency to save and hoard. It needs outside demand to grow, given its demography, slow productivity, savings and tendency to invest abroad rather than at home.
  • World trade and foreign demand are collapsing, and supply chains are breaking down (Japan is offering incentives to reshore from China). Add the Trump trade war and the importance of the yen exchange rate.
  • Good points: high savings and cash-rich companies, and that cash could support workers through a crisis.

Slides 37-38: Trump 2.0 and tariffs on Japan

  • Charts only: how Trump's tariffs have affected Japan.

Slides 39-42: Demography

  • With a fertility rate of 1.2, Japan's population has shrunk about 0.55% a year since 2010, from a peak of 128.1m.
  • Japan is the fastest-ageing country. World picture: Eastern Europe and Russia are declining, Western Europe may decline, North America is still rising (mainly through immigration), Asia is in rapid demographic transition, and the Middle East and Africa are in a demographic explosion. Some countries are "in danger of extinction."
  • Japan's working-age population has been shrinking for almost 30 years.

Slide 43: Old-age support ratio (OECD)

  • Old-age support ratio = people of working age (20-64) per person aged 65+. The OECD ratio will roughly halve.
  • Japan has the lowest: 2.8 in 2008, falling to 1.2 by 2050. Comparisons for 2008 and 2050: US 4.7 to 2.6, OECD 4.2 to 2.1, Germany and Italy 3.0 to 1.6/1.5, Spain 3.7 to 1.5, Korea 6.3 to 1.5, China 7.9 to 2.4, India 11.2 to 4.5.

Slides 44-45: Immigration could help, but the Japanese are not enthusiastic

  • Registered immigrants in 2025: 3.95 million (a record), or 3.2% of the population (28% Chinese, 16% Korean, 13% Vietnamese), vs an 11.5% average in developed countries. Most are permanent residents.
  • Japan would need about 10 million immigrants by 2050 to keep the ratio of working-age to retired people constant.
  • Cultural resistance: the Nihonjinron literature on Japanese uniqueness; gaijin, the word for foreigner, literally means "outsider." Abe started a limited guest-worker experiment (mainly domestic help). The "gated community" model promotes equality and cultural identity. Japan recently tightened its immigration requirements.

Slide 46: What is the future for Japan?

  • Were the crisis and the lost decade the inevitable fallout of an aggressive export-led strategy and "miracle"? An "oversized" economy, growth driven artificially by a weak currency and government strategy at unsustainable rates, and dependence on foreign demand beyond domestic capacity.
  • Or is it simply the natural drama of an ageing, slowing country?
  • Competition for external demand is intense, so Japan will need to grow from domestic demand by mobilizing its savings. "Do we need to rethink our ideas of constant growth?"

What the professor added in class

  • EXAM: The current account (a country's transactions with the rest of the world, mainly trade) will matter for every country except the EU as a bloc, and especially for India, China, Japan and the US. Always divide it by GDP to compare countries. She showed it in dollars once to make a point.
  • EXAM: Deficits enable surpluses. One country's surplus requires another's deficit, and globally they roughly balance apart from errors and omissions. In 2024 the largest deficits summed to about $1.4trn and the largest surpluses to about $1.38trn (China the biggest). The US is the world's "great hungry consumer," and nobody really wants that to stop.
  • EXAM: Domestic demand determines the current-account balance. The cake analogy: a "hungry" country (the US) eats its whole GDP cake and buys more from abroad. A "not hungry" country (China, Japan, Germany) does not eat everything it produces and must sell the extra slice abroad. An article she read said selling to the US adds about 1.5 points to China's growth rate.
  • India vs China: India "eats it all and still wants more" and has no problem finding buyers for its output. China has exactly that problem.
  • EXAM: Deficit countries such as the US and India are less stressed by trade wars because their growth comes from domestic demand. Surplus countries need foreign demand: imagine China growing 5% without its net exports. Accounting: with GDP = C + I + G + (X - M), removing the negative X - M raises a deficit country's GDP and removing the positive X - M lowers a surplus country's GDP.
  • EXAM: A deficit country's currency tends to fall, which she likened to "divine justice" that corrects the deficit. A surplus country's currency tends to rise. It is supply and demand. Exception: reserve currencies (US and UK). Under her "6 rules for currencies," confidence in a reserve currency offsets the deficit, so it tends to hold its value.
  • EXAM: Surplus countries tend toward deflation, because they must keep prices down to stay export-competitive, and if they are not competitive they do not grow.
  • US vs EU fiscal transfers: the EU budget moves money from richer to poorer members (as % of GDP, the Netherlands is the biggest net contributor, then Germany, Sweden, the UK when it was a member, Denmark, Austria). The maximum receipt is about 4% of GDP. US federal transfers between states are far larger: Delaware, Illinois, New York, Ohio, Texas and California pay in more than they receive, while Puerto Rico and others receive a great deal. Americans pay most taxes to the federal government, which pays unemployment, poverty and food aid, smoothing differences between states all the time. Europeans pay taxes to their national governments and resist contributing to the EU. The EU needs something similar, if smaller, to make a single monetary policy work. (Her exact per-state figures are UNSURE from the transcript, and the US and EU charts use opposite signs.)
  • US states also have their own taxes: California has a sales tax and Oregon does not; Texas and Tennessee have no state income tax. US diversity: maps of the largest immigrant group by state in 1920 vs 2020, where Mexico or another Latin American country dominates almost everywhere in 2020 and India appears in some states.
  • The Japan and China parallel: their growth models (not their politics) are similar. A miracle followed by a downshift. Japan's lost decade came from a real-estate collapse, and China's property sector is collapsing as a government-managed "slow-motion train wreck." She did not want to push the parallel too far.
  • EXAM: The Japanese miracle: growth averaged about 8-9% from 1950 to 1974 and exceeded 12% in some years. It ended with the 1974 oil shock, since Japan imports nearly 100% of its oil. Growth dropped to about 5% in the 1980s, which is still good for a developed country, and fell further in the 1990s lost decade.
  • Decades of fast growth create inflationary pressure. If it does not show up in the CPI it shows up in asset markets (stocks, bonds, housing). In Japan it was property, and the collapse hit the banks.
  • Why US pressure worked on Japan's currency but not on China's: Japan was a loyal US ally with no military after WWII, so when the US "asked" (not very nicely) it complied.
  • Inflation history: a 23% spike after the war and spikes at the 1974 and 1980 oil shocks. These were external supply shocks (aggregate supply shifting), so inflation and unemployment rose together: stagflation. When aggregate demand shifts, they move in opposite directions.
  • EXAM: The current account has always been in surplus except during the oil shocks (1974 and 1979-80), when expensive oil imports offset exports. Japan is therefore driven by external demand and very vulnerable to external shocks. Its domestic demand is better than China's, but a significant part of its growth comes from the current account. After 1974 Japan built many nuclear plants to cut oil dependence. Neither Japan nor the US depended on Russian gas in the 2022 energy crisis, unlike Germany.
  • EXAM: Real GDP growth above nominal GDP growth is very rare. It happens because prices are falling (deflation), so nominal GDP shrinks while real GDP grows. Germany and China have had it briefly, but Japan the longest. It normalized from 2022.
  • EXAM: GDP per capita grows faster than GDP because the population is shrinking, so the Japanese feel their incomes rising even though aggregate growth looks weak, and they do not feel in crisis.
  • EXAM: Why deflation is harmful: (1) people postpone big purchases (cars, TVs, durables, housing) because they expect lower prices, so consumption slows in a vicious downward cycle that is hard to break once expectations are anchored; (2) it is very hard to solve with policy, since rates cannot go far below zero; (3) debt and deflation: the debt ratio is debt divided by nominal GDP, so falling nominal GDP raises the debt ratio. Central banks stop inflation early for the same reason: persistent inflation changes habits (people buy now, expecting higher prices).
  • Deflation tallies: first deflation 1995; in the 21st century, 12 years of deflation and 12 of inflation; this year will make about 14 years of inflation, so inflation is now "beating" deflation. The deepest deflation, -1.36%, is substantial. Core was negative too, so domestic prices were falling.
  • EXAM: NAIRU = the non-accelerating inflation rate of unemployment, the ideal rate that keeps inflation from rising. It is estimated, not observed. Japan's unemployment has always been below its NAIRU, yet inflation did not appear. The Phillips curve does not hold in Japan. Reasons: an old, conservative, saving-loving population that trusts its government; a shrinking labor force (running out of people); culture. A student noted that the theories are built on Western behavior.
  • Gini: lower is more equal. Japan is quite equal and the US quite unequal. "Please don't go out saying we have the highest Gini in the world": the US is highest in her sample and in the developed world, not in the world. Japan's equality comes mainly from homogeneity and very little immigration, not from a large welfare state (it does not have a comprehensive European-style one). Germany works hard to keep its Gini low despite heavy immigration.
  • Immigration evidence (US micro studies using states as natural experiments): immigrants often take lower-wage jobs while natives move up (e.g. to foreman), and prices fall. High-skilled immigrants in health and tech pay as much tax as natives or more. In Germany immigrants are a net budget drain because rules keep them from working for years. In the US they are net contributors, since working requires a Social Security number and even undocumented workers pay in through borrowed numbers.
  • Hofstede's cultural dimensions for Japan: high power distance (respect for authority), very high achievement motivation, very high long-term orientation (forgo consumption today and save), low individualism, high uncertainty avoidance (conservative), low indulgence. The West is the opposite on most.
  • EXAM: Abenomics (Shinzo Abe, 2012; Abe was later assassinated at a political rally) was Keynesian expansion, focused on spending because the Japanese spend even less when taxes rise. Each announced sales-tax rise caused a spending rush and then a slump, inducing a couple of recessions. Structural reform succeeded mainly in bringing women into the labor force, which was needed for lack of workers. Current PM Sanae Takaichi is Japan's first woman prime minister (the transcript garbles the name) and continues Abenomics-style policy.
  • Government spending and taxes are both below 40% of GDP, low compared with Europe. Spending is about 38-39% of GDP because Japan has no huge welfare state (it does have public health), and it was afraid to go further because of the debt. Japan's and the Eurozone's deficits have moved closer to balance, while the US has moved further away. Recent inflation helps by enlarging nominal GDP. There was a brief surplus in the late 1980s boom. Deficit spending rose sharply after 2012 as deliberate stimulus, plus the pandemic.
  • EXAM: Fiscal deficit vs current account: the fiscal deficit is the government only (spending minus revenues). The current account covers all of a country's external transactions. They are separate: China has a large current-account surplus but a looming government debt problem, because domestic demand is so weak. India also has large fiscal deficits. Within that group, Japan looks fairly good on its fiscal deficit.
  • Output gap: the OECD estimates Japan at potential now, with a small inflationary gap projected for next year (2027). Takaichi's expansion is widening the deficit again just when it could have converged.
  • EXAM: Debt: close to 250% of GDP, the highest in the world as a share of GDP. The US has the largest debt in nominal terms, but you must divide by GDP. Japan's debt is now falling for the first time in the 21st century because inflation has returned (a larger nominal GDP). Since 2016 the US had the largest debt increase in her table, while Japan, Greece and Italy came down.
  • Net interest payments are under 1% of GDP and were recently negative, though rising now. She compared the US at about 5% of GDP (UNSURE: the transcript says "we saw the US at about 5%"; the S4 deck said US interest is 11% of the federal budget).
  • EXAM: About 90% of Japanese debt is owned by Japanese institutions (households, corporations, government). It is held for pensions, so holders are unlikely to dump it, which makes it very stable. It is in yen. Japan has massive savings, government and private. Risks: an old country (average age about 46) faces rising pension liabilities and fewer workers paying in. Class vote on whether Japan has a debt problem: split, with "not now, but yes with demography" the common view.
  • Could Japan simply print money? It could if backed to the wall, and it does not have an inflation problem, but "you're not supposed to." Another option is for the central bank to forgive the government's debt, which would be very unconventional, though Japan has done unconventional things before.
  • Interest rates: zero for a long time, then -0.5% for years until 2024, now about 1.25%, "one of the very big changes in the world right now." Deeply negative rates break the financial system, so they could not go further.
  • EXAM: QE = the central bank buys bonds massively from private banks and credits electronic money to their reserves. Buying directly from the government would be monetizing the debt (printing money). It did little for years, because Japan's conservative banks did not lend and people did not want to borrow. QT (quantitative tightening) is the opposite: selling bonds or letting them mature without replacing them, which drains liquidity. The Fed has done the most QT, and Japan a little at the end.
  • EXAM: The BoJ holds more than 50% of JGBs (about 55%, worth more than 100% of GDP), vs about 25% of US Treasuries held by the Fed. The risk: if QT continues and ageing savers cash in bonds to live on, Japan could face a crunch over who buys its bonds.
  • Takaichi will continue fiscal expansion and tax cuts, so the debt improvement may stop. Rising rates are a sign of better health. About a month before the class, the US and Japan coordinated an intervention to rescue the yen. A student said Japan spent about $30bn (UNSURE).
  • EXAM: Carry trade: borrow at about 0% in Japan (or in Swiss francs) and invest in higher-yielding assets in stable currencies (US dollars, Australian dollars, Mexican pesos, and Brazil). As Japanese rates rise and the US-Japan rate gap narrows, investors unwind the trade: they sell US, Brazilian and Mexican bonds and send money back to Japan. These are huge trades, so some fear a crisis or a big blow to US markets. While it runs, the carry trade tends to push the yen down (borrowed yen are sold for other currencies). Unwinding could push it up, though the net effect is hard to tell.

Likely exam angles

  • Main driver of Japan's large, chronic current-account surplus: the savings habits of the Japanese (savings above investment). Not a dollar peg (the yen floats), not very low costs, not the budget deficit (a fiscal deficit is dissaving and works against a surplus).
  • Which describes a surplus country? It produces more than it consumes, needs foreign demand, lends abroad, would have lower GDP without the rest of the world, its currency tends to rise, and it is prone to deflation.
  • Which describes a deficit country? It consumes more than it produces, can grow on domestic demand, borrows abroad, would have higher GDP without the rest of the world, and its currency tends to fall (unless it is a reserve currency).
  • Why Japan's 250% debt is less dangerous than it looks: it is in yen, about 90% domestically held, very cheap to service, backed by massive savings, and now falling as inflation returns. The risk is pension liabilities and ageing savers selling bonds.
  • Why real GDP growth exceeded nominal growth in Japan: deflation (a negative GDP deflator).
  • Why deflation hurts: postponed purchases, a downward spiral, anchored expectations, a zero lower bound on rates, and a rising debt/GDP ratio.
  • Abenomics' three arrows: fiscal expansion, QE and structural reform (the neglected arrow). The most successful reform was raising women's participation.
  • Carry trade definition, and the consequences of the unwind as Japanese rates rise since 2024.
  • QE vs monetizing debt: QE buys from private banks, while buying from the government means printing money to finance it.
  • Fiscal deficit vs current-account deficit: the government's budget vs the whole country's external balance. China shows a surplus on one and a problem on the other.
  • Yen up about 50% in the mid-1980s under US pressure, to which many trace the bubble, bust, lost decade and deflation. Japan's old-age support ratio is the OECD's lowest (2.8 to 1.2). Immigrants are 3.2% of the population vs an 11.5% developed-country average.
  • Paradox of thrift: high savings fund the surplus and the debt, but they also hold back domestic demand, so Japan needs to mobilize savings into domestic demand to grow.

Traps and look-alikes

  • Japan's debt/GDP: about 250% in class (S6), 248% on the deck (under Takaichi), and "200" in S4 class. Use the deck figure.
  • Domestic ownership: "about 90%" (class, figures she looked up) vs "nearly all" (deck).
  • When QE ended: the deck's year is missing from the extracted text; the Japan notes say 2024; in class she said they stopped "last year, I think" and also mentioned 2024. UNSURE between 2024 and 2025. Rates rose from negative in 2024 (deck and class agree).
  • Japan's policy rate: "about 1.25%" (class) vs "31-year highs" (deck). These are consistent and not in conflict.
  • Abenomics aimed to weaken the yen, not strengthen it. The August 2026 US-Japan intervention aimed to stop the yen falling, a reversal of the Abenomics-era goal.
  • A weak yen helps exports but makes energy imports expensive, since Japan imports nearly all its energy.
  • The surplus comes from savings relative to investment, not from cheap costs or a peg. A fiscal deficit lowers national saving and pushes the other way.
  • "Deficit countries' currencies fall" holds only "tend to." The US and UK hold their value as reserve currencies.
  • "If the rest of the world disappeared": a deficit country's GDP would be higher and a surplus country's lower. This is easy to reverse.
  • Japan's unemployment is below the NAIRU without inflation, which breaks the Phillips curve. Do not say it is above the NAIRU.
  • Japan's low Gini comes from homogeneity and low immigration, not from a generous welfare state. The US has the highest Gini in the developed world, not in the whole world.
  • Japan was not involved in the Vietnam War. Do not confuse that with the US pressure on its currency.
  • Prime Minister's name: Sanae Takaichi (the deck spells it "Sanai", the transcript "Takahashi").
  • Old-age support ratio = working age (20-64) per person aged 65+. A higher number is better. Japan's falls from 2.8 to 1.2.
  • Fertility: Japan 1.2 vs the US 1.8 (S4 deck).
Session 7 · 29 Sep 2026 · Allard · complete notes

Session 7: China and India, history and development (Prof. Gayle Allard)

Allard opened with a short wrap-up of Japan (the 1985 yen revaluation), then started her deck "India and China". The class covered the first half of the deck: the two countries' long history as the world's largest economies, how countries develop (the Human Development Index, GDP per capita, the GDP identity), the two 20th-century growth strategies (import-substitution vs export-oriented industrialization), India's path from ISI to the 1991 balance-of-payments crisis and Washington Consensus reforms, China's path from Mao to Deng, and the first data comparisons (growth, inflation, current account, savings vs investment, consumption). This session feeds two of the professors' named short-answer topics directly: the Human Development Index and growth strategies in the 20th century. It is also the source of the sample MCQ "Which comparison between India and China is correct?" (answer: India has a chronic current-account deficit, China a surplus). The second half of the deck (investment, currencies, China shocks, institutions, demography, future) was taught in Session 10.

Slide-by-slide

Slides 1-3: Title, Khanna quote, outline

  • Deck by Gayle Allard. Opening quote from Tarun Khanna (Billions of Entrepreneurs): few shocks to the world system compare with China and India entering the global economy at the same time.
  • Outline: History; Data and development; Institutions; Demography; Future.

Slide 5: China and India were historically large economies

  • Ranking by economic size (Maddison; Haig Bryan): 1625-1825 China, India, France (India first in 1675-1700); 1825-1850 China, UK, India; 1850-1875 China, USA, UK, India; 1875-1900 USA, China, UK, Germany, India; 1900-1925 USA, UK, China, France, Germany, India, USSR; 1925-1950 USA, USSR, UK, China, France, Germany, India.
  • In the 1800s as much as 50% of world GDP was produced by China and India.

Slides 6-8: The last 2000 years in one graph; today's ranking

  • Nominal US$ (IMF 2020): China and India are now the 2nd and 5th largest economies (pie: USA 24.4%, China 16.4%, Japan 5.8%, Germany 4.4%, India 3.3%).
  • At PPP they are 1st and 3rd (China 17.3%, USA 15.8%, India 7.1%). Lesson: in low-price countries PPP GDP is much larger than GDP at market exchange rates.

Slide 9: Some parallels in the past?

  • Rich, ancient histories; first significant unifications around 200 BC (Mauryas in India, Han dynasty in China).
  • Negative experience under the British.
  • Radical political shifts at the same time under visionary leaders: Nehru led independent India from 1947; Mao made China a modern state in 1949.

Slide 11: How do countries develop?

  • Factors of production: labor, capital, land and resources, technology and institutions.
  • Components of growth: GDP = C + I + G + (X - M).
  • Fully endogenous growth may be slow: the UK and US grew about 1% a year in GDP per capita during industrialization.
  • 20th century: strategies to "jump-start" growth: import-substitution industrialization (ISI) and export-oriented industrialization (EOI). Question posed: what is the consequence of choosing one or the other?

Slide 12: What are the forces driving growth?

  • GDP = C + I + G + (X - M). ISI leans on domestic demand (C + I + G); EOI leans on external demand (X - M).

Slide 13: What was India's choice?

  • From independence to 1991: Soviet-style planning and ISI.
  • Nationalizations until 49% of output came from state-owned enterprises; rigid labor laws; the "Permit Raj" (colossal bureaucracy).
  • Result: the "Hindu rate of growth" (3.5% average) and declining exports.
  • From 1980 policies became more business-oriented, boosting entrepreneurship (diaspora, technology entrepreneurs).
  • 1991: balance-of-payments crisis, plus the failure of the USSR model.

Slide 14: Indian balance-of-payments crisis 1991

  • Current-account deficit was 3%+ of GDP; reserves covered only two weeks of imports.
  • India turned to the IMF, which lent on condition of major economic reforms. This was the catalyst toward a market-oriented economy.

Slide 15: "Washington Consensus" strategy for rescue

  • Imposed by the IMF: fiscal discipline; public spending on health and education; tax reform; liberalize interest rates; competitive exchange rate; remove trade barriers (subsidies, tariffs, licenses); free up foreign investment; privatize; deregulate; secure property rights.
  • Outcome: growth accelerated to 6%; reforms seen positively by the population; entrepreneurship took off. The sheer volume of educated English speakers led to an outsourcing boom. But unreformed sectors still made up a large part of the economy.
  • Speaker notes: there is no longer a Washington Consensus in recent World Development Reports; it was a market-oriented 1980s view (macro balance, "getting prices right"), quoting Stiglitz.

Slide 16: What was China's choice? (Mao)

  • The CCP took power in 1949 (People's Republic). Phases: consolidation and reconstruction (1949-1957); Great Leap Forward (1958-1960); recovery (1961-1965); Cultural Revolution (1966-1976).
  • Great Leap Forward: break with the Soviet Union; land given to peasants was reclaimed for rural communes to raise farm output and build rural industry (steel); production figures were falsified, labor moved from farms to industry, farm output collapsed and 20-30 million people starved.
  • Cultural Revolution: "destroy the old and establish the new"; the Four Olds (ideas, culture, customs, habits); keep China in "permanent revolution".

Slide 17: What was China's choice? (Deng)

  • Era of reform under Deng Xiaoping, 1979-1990s. Pragmatic: "Who cares if a cat is black or white, as long as it catches mice?"
  • Starting point: severe damage from the Great Leap and Cultural Revolution; 60%+ lived on less than $1 a day.
  • Reforms: one-child policy; dual price system (part of farm output sold at market prices), which raised farm productivity, savings and demand for consumer goods; more Foreign Trade Corporations, so the number of firms with trade rights soared; FDI liberalized in Special Economic Zones; the yuan steadily lowered, with firms allowed to keep and trade some foreign currency; reform of state-owned enterprises ("Management Responsibility System").
  • These reforms transformed China's links to the world and led to a stunning transformation. (Notes: Deng died in 1997.)

Slide 18: Key differences in the economic frameworks

  • China: "top-down", directed by the Communist Party with little input or dissent; centralized, opaque, orderly (corrupt) hierarchy; uncertain property rights, state-controlled markets; rapid progress with little opposition; but a vibrant private sector is emerging.
  • India: heterogeneous and pluralistic; permits debate, creativity, experimentation; hard to approve and implement big projects; a (corrupt) government that may be dysfunctional but respects individuals; vibrant, decentralized, chaotic but functioning markets.
  • "In China, the government has been the entrepreneur; in India, the entrepreneur has stayed as far away from the government as possible." Khanna: the two are "inverted mirror images".

Slides 19-23: Growth charts (World Bank)

  • Real GDP growth China vs India; China vs Japan; real GDP per capita growth; GDP per capita nominal and at PPP for both.

Slide 24: India's development gaps

  • Much variety in incomes and development across India; inadequate housing, millions without electricity.
  • Common features of both: excessive bureaucracy and trade barriers, inadequate infrastructure for a fast-growing country. Modi wanted India in the top 50 of the World Bank Ease of Doing Business index.

Slides 25-28: Inflation, current account, savings and investment

  • Inflation rate (World Bank) for both; current-account balance % GDP for both; gross savings vs gross capital formation % GDP, first China then India.

Slides 29-31: Forces driving growth; household consumption

  • Repeat of the GDP identity, then household consumption % GDP for India and China, and for India, China and the USA.

What the professor added in class

  • Japan wrap-up (opening): a surplus country's currency should rise naturally; if it does not, something is going on. Japan used to be called a "black hole for dollars" because it bought dollars to keep the yen cheap. In 1985 the US said stop (Japan's cheap yen had killed US TV and appliance industries); manipulating currency is against the IMF rules because it works like a subsidy. Japan, a dependent ally, gave in and the yen rose about 50% in a short time; she linked this to the lost decade.
  • Why China and India were biggest: population. Before industrialization output is a direct function of the number of workers, with no machinery or technology in between.
  • Colonization: the UK passing India around 1825-1850 coincides with the Industrial Revolution and colonization. Part of the capital that fed the Industrial Revolution came from the colonies and the slave trade, which may have pushed India down and the UK up. Similar later declines of India and China in the early 20th century.
  • USSR parallel: the USSR appears in the 20th-century ranking because it mobilized resources from the top (like China) and got a short-run surge; people once feared Russia, then Japan, would overtake the US, so take a long-term view of "China will overtake everyone".
  • EXAM: Human Development Index (not on the slides, taught orally): three indicators, health measured by life expectancy, education measured by the literacy rate, and income measured by GDP per capita. She chose them because they are easy to measure and not subjective; literacy is the weakest (what counts as literate?). Rankings at the top move a little, but the split between developed and developing barely changes.
  • EXAM: GDP per capita must grow, not just GDP: GDP has to grow faster than population for a country to develop; very poor populations generate no surplus to invest.
  • Early developers (UK, US, Germany) grew mostly on domestic demand, with little export market and little foreign capital except colonial capital and the slave trade, so growth per capita was slow.
