  • ISI logic: a Latin American country or India facing imports from advanced economies fears its industry will never grow, so it shuts out imports to keep every dollar or rupee of domestic demand for local industry. Latin America, India and, in an odd way, China all did it. The US did some of it against the UK in its early years.
  • Why ISI slows growth: India became over-regulated (firms waited months or years for permits to import machinery); bottlenecks everywhere; protected firms with no competition get sloppy and inefficient; they cannot produce attractive goods so they do not export; foreign firms do not invest; the state often buys failing firms, which enlarges the state's share of output. A first big push can work in the early years (USSR), then growth falls. Anecdote: a relative in Argentina waited about four years for a car under ISI and got the wrong color.
  • EOI logic: put all effort into the export sector; possibly manipulate the currency so goods look cheap; the government may invest; welcome foreign investment; import technology and machinery to cut costs. X - M drives GDP, GDP per capita rises, and eventually C + I + G should be strong enough to keep momentum. Japan after WWII was probably the first to use the model; China is "living out" it now.
  • Downsides of EOI: to sell abroad without currency manipulation you push down wages and costs, which can bring deflation; the best products go abroad and domestic consumers get the rest (the "can't get a good banana here" complaint in Central America). There are limits to export-led growth; no one can grow at those rates forever.
  • India's "Hindu rate of growth" of about 3.5% was low for a poor country, which should grow nearer 7%. Nothing happened overnight: education and entrepreneurship were rising in the 1980s, but the government kept the basic policy.
  • EXAM: 1991 BoP crisis mechanics: a current-account deficit must be offset by a financial-account surplus (foreign investment, foreign loans) or by running down reserves. India's deficit was just over 3% of GDP, which she called not that much, similar to the US today and not crisis level. The problem was that the financial account did not cover it; reserves were enough for two weeks of imports (India imported nearly all its oil). India asked Switzerland and the UK for loans and had to airlift gold as collateral, then went to the IMF. The collapse of the USSR and Eastern Europe had discredited India's planning model.
  • She said the IMF conditions were "very good for India" given how regulated it was. In her oral list she said "let your currency float" (the slide says "competitive exchange rate"). Indian entrepreneurs welcomed the changes (unlike much of Latin America). Outsiders discovered a large pool of educated English speakers, so India's first big global industry was outsourcing (call centers, tax preparation, health records). 1991 was the watershed.
  • Her view: she does not like to see the world as a competition; a country may choose to sacrifice growth.
  • Great Leap Forward: Mao had absolute power (unlike democratic India) and tried to industrialize in two years. He collectivized farming to free labor for industry that did not exist; steel quotas were met by melting iron gates into unsaleable steel; communes were unproductive. Her estimate: 20 to 38 million deaths in about two and a half years (the slide says 20-30 million).
  • Cultural Revolution: keep everything shaken up so an entrenched party elite would not form; families were broken up. She mentioned that the current leader's father, a party official, was sent to forced labor in this period.
  • Deng: pragmatic communist (the cat may be capitalism or communism; what matters is catching mice, i.e. growing). 60% of the population was under the global poverty line. The one-child policy was "a terrible mistake" that China still pays for. The dual price system: if farmers had to deliver 100 and produced 110, they could sell the extra on the free market, which raised productivity (something Stalin never did).
  • Growth path: China grew around 15% in the first reform years and averaged roughly 10% after about 1976; she sees a Japan-like pattern: growth miracle, then a downshift (she placed the clear slowdown around 2015-2016). There are limits to export-led growth.
  • India's growth has been steadier, with smaller falls; it shifted up around 1980 and again after 1991. Recently India grows faster than China. EXAM: potential GDP growth = productivity growth + labor-force (population) growth; both are falling in China and both are strong in India, so barring a crisis India should grow faster most years.
  • Chinese data: she posted an article (the FT piece) and described a senior Chinese economist saying they no longer know the true GDP; provincial leaders always report growth equal to the government target because nobody wants to be the one who misses it.
  • GDP per capita: India about $11,000 at PPP today (her reading of the chart), much lower in nominal terms. China started at the same level and then grew far faster.
  • A 2011 chart of development indicators: India lags China by decades (she read about 36 years on life expectancy and longer on child mortality; exact figures UNSURE).
  • Inflation: India's is higher. A faster grower should have higher inflation, so China's low inflation means either bad data or the export model forcing prices down (as in Japan). India's inflation is fast but under control, so not a big risk signal for an investor.
  • EXAM: Current accounts are "the biggest, most interesting difference": China is always in surplus and recently rising (reaching for more external demand); India is almost always in deficit (the 1991 deficit was not that big) and near balance now. A deficit country grows on domestic demand.
  • Investor risk view from the discussion: China is very dependent on global growth (trade wars, currency disputes); India's risk is getting enough capital to cover the deficit, but capital has flowed freely recently.
  • EXAM: Savings vs investment: a surplus country is a saver (China: savings always above investment); a deficit country is a spender (India: investment often above savings; now close). These are total national savings, not just government.
  • Consumption: about 60% of GDP in India (it was about 80% in early years; the share fell because GDP rose), about 40% in China, about 70% in the US. Poorer countries usually have higher consumption shares. With consumption at only 40%, China must find the other 60% elsewhere, which makes it very vulnerable to a fall in demand, a financial crisis, a trade war or currency changes. Her comparison: a saver like Germany is roughly 60% C, 20% I, 15% G plus some exports.

Likely exam angles

  • Short answer: "Explain the Human Development Index." Three dimensions: health (life expectancy), education (literacy), income (GDP per capita); it gives a broader view of development than GDP alone.
  • Short answer: "Compare the growth strategies of the 20th century." ISI relies on domestic demand and protection (India before 1991, Latin America) and tends to produce inefficiency, bureaucracy and slow growth; EOI relies on external demand, a cheap currency and FDI (Japan, China) and produces fast growth but dependence on foreign demand, currency conflict and possible deflation.
  • MCQ: correct comparison of India and China: India has a chronic current-account deficit, China a surplus.
  • MCQ: trigger of India's 1991 reforms: a balance-of-payments crisis (reserves for two weeks of imports) and IMF conditionality (Washington Consensus).
  • MCQ: which is NOT a Washington Consensus measure (e.g. "nationalize strategic industries" or "raise tariffs").
  • MCQ: which was NOT one of Deng's reforms (e.g. collectivized communes, which was the Great Leap Forward).
  • MCQ: what is the Hindu rate of growth (about 3.5% under ISI).
  • MCQ: why China and India were the largest economies before 1800 (population: output was a direct function of workers).
  • MCQ: savings and investment: a surplus country saves more than it invests (China); a deficit country invests more than it saves (India).

Traps and look-alikes

  • India is NOT a surplus country and China is NOT a deficit country; do not reverse. India's deficit is "chronic" but small (near balance now).
  • India and China did NOT both follow ISI and then switch to EOI at the same time (sample distractor). India stayed with ISI until 1991; China's reform era began in 1979.
  • Both are not "declining in population": China's working-age population is falling; India's population is still growing (1.2% on the slide).
  • The HDI as taught in class uses literacy; the current UN definition uses years of schooling and GNI per capita. For the exam, give the class version (health, education, income) and you are safe either way.
  • Great Leap deaths: slide 20-30 million, class 20-38 million.
  • Washington Consensus exchange-rate item: slide says "competitive exchange rate", class said "let your currency float".
  • India's 1991 deficit (3%+ of GDP) was not huge; the crisis came from lack of financing and reserves, not the size of the deficit.
  • Rankings: nominal GDP China 2nd, India 5th; PPP China 1st, India 3rd. Do not mix them.
  • Nehru 1947 (India's independence) vs Mao 1949 (PRC); unifications around 200 BC are Mauryas (India) and Han (China), not the reverse.
  • ISI uses domestic demand (C + I + G); EOI uses external demand (X - M). EOI may involve a deliberately weak currency.
Session 8 · 30 Sep 2026 · Garland · complete notes

Session 8: Exchange-rate theory (trade model, pegs, Mundell, twin deficits, dirty floating, dual rates) and Angola and the Resource Curse (Prof. Gonzalo Garland)

Garland called this "the day of the exchange rate". Using his deck "Notes on Exchange Rates" (Aggregate Demand Model in an Open Economy) he recapped the classical trade-based model, then fixed / pegged rates as a price control (China above equilibrium, Argentina below, with the full list of pros and cons), Robert Mundell's addition of capital flows (a trade deficit financed by capital inflows need not depreciate the currency, which explains the US), China financing the US twin deficits, and dirty (managed) floating. He then debriefed the Angola case (the group midterm): which of the five resource-curse elements appear, with the case exhibits (kwanza nominal vs real, volatility in GDP and budget), and finished with the dual exchange rate as "an invitation to corruption". Expect MCQs on the pros and cons of an over- or undervalued peg, dirty floating and dual rates, and a short answer on the resource curse.

Slide-by-slide

Slide 1: Title — "Notes on Exchange Rates: Aggregate Demand Model in an Open Economy"

  • IE Business School, Prof. Gonzalo Garland with Anthony Moore, Antonio Razetto and Alessandro Procaccini.

Slide 2: Exchange rate and degree of openness

  • Exchange rate = the relation between two currencies. If 1 US$ = 10 pesos you can say the rate is 10 (pesos per dollar) or 0.1 (ten cents of a dollar per peso).
  • Degree of openness = (X + M) / GDP. It falls when a country is protectionist, and it is related to size: very large countries produce most of what they need and trade less relative to GDP; small countries are much more open.

Slide 3: Currency market, classical theory (variable / floating / free)

  • Vertical axis: pesos per dollar; horizontal: dollars. D (imports) slopes down, S (exports) slopes up.
  • D depends on how much Mexicans want to import US goods and services; S depends on how much Americans import from Mexico (Mexican exports).
  • Above equilibrium: excess supply of dollars (X greater than M) → dollar depreciates, peso appreciates. Below equilibrium: excess demand for dollars → dollar appreciates, peso depreciates.
  • Constant trade deficits are a fundamental reason for a currency to depreciate; constant surpluses a reason to appreciate.

Slide 4: More recent additions — Robert Mundell

  • Classical theory assumed people exchange currency mainly to trade goods and services.
  • Mundell said in the 60s (deck) that capital would move a lot among countries, driven mainly by interest-rate differentials and associated risk.

Slide 5: 1st example — r€ greater than r$

  • Capital flows out of the US into the Eurozone; supply of dollars in the euro market becomes S1 = X + K inflows. The euro appreciates (E → E1).
  • Before: M = 1,000, X = 1,000. After: M = 1,200, X = 800, K inflows = 400 (total 1,200). A trade deficit financed by capital inflows. Exporters complain they are no longer competitive.
  • Note on the slide: with a fixed rate currencies revalue or devalue (they do not appreciate or depreciate). Above the equilibrium level → trade surplus; below → trade deficit.

Slide 6: 2nd example — r€ less than r$

  • Capital flows from the Eurozone to the US: demand for dollars becomes D1 = M + K outflows; the rate moves from er1 to er2, the euro depreciates.
  • X = 1,200, M = 800; capital outflows 400. A trade surplus compensated by capital outflows.

Slide 7: Fixed exchange rate — why use one? Pros and cons

Peg level (axis = local currency per $)Positive aspectsNegative aspects
Below equilibrium = overvalued local currency (Argentina 1:1)1. Cheaper external debt. 2. Cheaper imports (helps fight inflation). 3. Positive wealth effect (citizens relatively rich).1. Exports go down, imports go up. 2. Trade deficit.
Above equilibrium = undervalued local currency (China 8.28)1. Exports cheap, imports expensive. 2. Trade surplus and economic growth.1. Expensive imports. 2. Expensive external debt. 3. Negative wealth effect (citizens relatively poor).

Slide 8: Dirty floating — "going against the market", Example 1: capital inflows

  • S → S1: appreciation because of an interest-rate differential. You want to avoid it to help production and exports.
  • The central bank demands dollars (D → D2), selling local currency, which depreciates it against the original appreciation.

Slide 9: Example 2 — the Lula effect: capital outflows

  • Lula was going to be elected and markets got nervous; capital outflow increased demand for dollars.
  • The central bank started to sell dollars (S → S2) to weaken the dollar and strengthen the real.

Slide 10: Inflation and depreciation

  • 1 peso = 1 US$; a product costs 100 pesos = $100. Mexican inflation 100%, US 0% → after a year it costs 200 pesos = $200 at an unchanged rate and is not competitive.
  • To remain competitive the rate must change by the inflation differential: 2 pesos = 1 US$.

Slide 11: Balance of payments

  • Records all transactions between one country and the rest of the world. Three main accounts: current, capital, financial. "Net errors and omissions" is the balancing item so the total is zero.
  • Current account: trade in goods and services (visible and invisible balance), primary income (dividends), secondary income (remittances, transfers).
  • Financial account: mainly long-term FDI and portfolio investment.
  • Capital account: a narrow set of special transactions (debt forgiveness, patents, assets changing country); usually very small.

The case, section by section: "Angola and the Resource Curse" (HBS 711-016, 2010; the group midterm)

Opening (early 2010)

  • Eight years after the civil war, President Dos Santos (de facto president since 1979, MPLA) wonders whether to continue IMF-loan reforms (transparency, spending cuts to lower inflation).
  • 2002: the IMF demanded Sonangol's books be opened ($8.5bn of public money unaccounted for 1997–2001). In 2004 Angola broke off talks, calling the conditions "humiliating", and took a $2bn China Eximbank credit line (12 years, LIBOR + 1.5%), repaid with oil. Chinese loans brought Chinese firms and imported Chinese labor, little technology transfer, and undercut Western reform pressure.
  • 2008–09: growth had averaged 10% a year (1994–2008), but the oil-price collapse and an OPEC quota cut (1.9 → 1.656 million barrels a day) sent exports from $65bn to $39bn and real GDP growth from almost 12% to −0.5%. The central bank (BNA) sold reserves to buy kwanza and rationed foreign exchange; by June 2010 reserves were about $10bn, barely three months of imports. The BNA raised rates and Angola took the IMF loan.

History and geography; the Portuguese legacy

  • 18.5 million people, fertile land and water; world's 4th diamond producer by the 1970s; oil found in the 1950s (production from 1955), offshore from the 1970s; oil exports over $70bn in 2008 = 96% of exports.
  • Slave trade (about 2 million exported 1600–1850s), then forced contract labor until 1961. Postwar Portuguese investment: coffee from 14,000 tons (1940) to 220,000 tons (1974), world's 4th coffee producer; food self-sufficient and a maize exporter; diverse manufacturing (food, textiles, soap, paper, plastics, cars and buses, cement, steel, aluminum); GDP growth 7% a year 1960–74. Natives got almost no education.

Independence and civil war

  • MPLA (Soviet/Cuban-backed), FNLA and UNITA (backed by South Africa, the US and others). Independence 11 Nov 1975; civil war for 27 years; MPLA financed by Cabinda oil, UNITA by diamonds. Ended when Savimbi was killed in Feb 2002.
  • 300,000 Portuguese fled with the skills; Marxist central planning and price controls; coffee fell to about 1,000 tons by 2002; Angola could not feed itself. Only offshore oil survived intact. Over a million dead; 80% of factories and schools destroyed; 7 million land mines.

State and society after the war

  • Dos Santos at the center of a patronage network; MPLA won 82% in 2008; 2010 constitution kept him in power potentially until 2022. EIU democracy index 3.35 (authoritarian). Contracts take 1,011 days to enforce.
  • HDI rank 143/182; Gini 0.59; GDP per capita $3,626 (2007) yet 70% under $2 a day. Oil = about 60% of GDP but 0.2% of workers; agriculture = 70% of workers, 10% of GDP. Treated as middle-income, so no foreign aid.

Sonangol: the Midas touch

  • State oil company and sole owner of deposits; became a holding with banks, airline, real estate. Deepwater success rate 60% vs 30% worldwide; proven reserves 13.5bn barrels (2009). China became the top buyer of Angolan oil.
  • Opaque because of five roles: allocates concessions, collects and passes on government revenue, raises secret oil-backed loans for the presidency, runs unrelated subsidiaries, and provides quasi-fiscal services (public debt, subsidized fuel). Audited only once by 2008.

The resource curse (Sachs, Stiglitz, Humphreys): five consequences

  • Dutch disease: a rise in resource exports appreciates the real exchange rate; other exports and import-competing producers are driven out; labor and materials shift to the resource sector (resource pull effect); weaker incentive to invest in education; inequality if returns are concentrated; slower productivity growth.
  • Volatility: front-loaded extraction, varying tax revenue (early exemptions), and commodity-price cycles.
  • Asset depletion mentality: politicians with an uncertain hold on power spend sooner rather than leave wealth to opponents, and use it to stay in power (campaigns or militias).
  • Spoliation / corruption: windfalls create chances to steal or favor cronies in procurement.
  • Political resource curse: states living on resource revenue tax citizens lightly, engage less with society, invest in repression (oil states spend 2–10x more on the military) and are more prone to civil war because capturing the state means capturing the rents.

Rebuilding Angola

  • War-time money printing: three digits cut from the currency in 1995 and six in 1999; deficit 35% of GDP in 1999, surpluses after 2005.
  • The government bought kwanza to stabilize the rate: lower inflation via stable import prices but an overvalued exchange rate; the EIU estimated the real rate appreciated more than 100% from 2002 to 2009.
  • Investment only about 10% of GDP; under 20% have electricity.
  • Agriculture: devastated by war and now facing cheap imports "due to overvaluation of the currency"; ports exist mainly to import. Aldeia Nova / Quiminha settlement projects.
  • Manufacturing: about 7% of GDP, hurt by the war and the strong exchange rate; it mostly makes bulky goods expensive to import (soft drinks, cement).
  • Education: Wright and Czelusta — resource economies can thrive by building a knowledge economy around extraction (California). Literacy 15% (1975) to 67% (2007); only 44% of primary-age children in school; skills scarce.
  • Corruption: Transparency International rank 162/180. Patronage tools: official and parallel exchange rates differing by a factor of 2.9; connected people got dollars at the official rate, a "license" to make 190% profit by selling at the parallel rate; credit at negative real rates from state banks; cheap privatizations.
  • Red tape: 328 days for a construction permit, 65 days to export.

China in Africa: friend or foe? and a new dawn

  • China overtook the US as top buyer (33% of exports, 2008). Sinopec–Sonangol (SSI) needed Western operators; the Lobito refinery failed over where products would go. Chinese firms prefer Chinese workers in enclaves; they are not bound by the FCPA or OECD anti-bribery rules.
  • 2010 IMF mission saw recovery and eased FX pressure. The question: keep IMF conditions, or stand alone / lean on China?
  • Appendix (IMF conditions): publish audited Sonangol and BNA accounts, quarterly budget reports, tax reform, report SOE quasi-fiscal operations, and submit a sovereign wealth fund to cabinet.

What the professor added in class

  • Trade-based recap with India as the example; Germany would have a much stronger mark given its surpluses, so it benefits from the weaker euro (while contributing a lot to the EU).
  • A fixed rate is a price control in the market for dollars. Few countries fix today; the hard case is Saudi Arabia (40 years of peg, explained with Norway).
  • Argentina pegged 1:1 for about 11 years from 1990 after hyperinflation and debt rescheduling; China 8.28 renminbi per dollar for 9 years (1995–2004).
  • China's choice explained with Y = C + I + G + X − M: boosting X and cutting M boosts GDP. China's priority in the 1990s–2000s was growth (9–10%). Expensive external debt did not matter (China had little); inflation was occasionally a problem (oil shocks) and was handled with monetary policy; citizens' negative wealth effect did not matter. So the negatives were cheap for China.
  • Argentina: cheap dollar meant Argentines bought Miami real estate and found Europe very cheap (positive wealth effect).
  • Mechanics of China's peg: excess dollars in a mostly state-owned banking system were all bought by the central bank, paid with renminbi. Two results: trillions in reserves and a lot of renminbi pumped into the economy. A Chinese leader answered US pressure by saying change would come "soon", adding that in a country with 5,000 years of history soon is relative (speaker not named; UNSURE). Fully freeing the rate might have taken it to 5 per dollar and bankrupted thousands of factories, so China opened a band instead.
  • Capital controls: India and China lack full convertibility (you cannot freely move $2m to New York). Full convertibility: US, Eurozone, Japan, Switzerland, Australia, Canada. The renminbi cannot be a world currency until it is fully convertible.
  • Mundell: Nobel prize, Canadian, "father of the euro" (transcript: "father of Europe"), died recently. Garland said he developed the idea in the 70s; the deck says the 60s. With free capital, money moves to higher returns at equal risk. Example: US bond 5–5.5% vs a hypothetical German 9% → sell dollars, buy euros → euro appreciates → exports down, imports up → trade deficit that does not depreciate the currency because capital inflows finance it. "That is the US": the world's appetite for dollar assets keeps a deficit country's currency strong. Reverse case (today's reality: US about 5.5%, Germany about 3-point) → euro depreciates and runs a surplus compensated by outflows. Risk matters: Brazil paying 7% would not attract money if the US pays 5.5%.
  • Carry trade is different: borrow where rates are low (yen) and invest where they are high.
  • Twin deficits: China financed both US deficits — buying dollars supported the dollar and financed the trade deficit; recycling those dollars into Treasuries financed the fiscal deficit. The "nuclear option" (China dumping US debt and dollars) is unlikely because China's own wealth is in dollars: interdependence. Some gradual diversification into euros, but China moves slowly.
  • Tariffs can protect a specific sector but cannot offset a whole exchange rate.
  • Dirty / managed floating: the currency floats but the central bank intervenes. A band is semi-fixed, intermediate between float and fix. Oil exporter with rising oil prices → dollar supply up → appreciation that would hurt other industries (Dutch disease) → central bank buys dollars and adds them to reserves. Capital flight (political nerves) → demand for dollars up → depreciation that would raise the cost of external debt and inflation → central bank sells dollars.
  • Angola 2009 as dirty floating: the oil collapse cut dollar supply, the kwanza came under depreciation pressure, and the BNA sold reserves and rationed FX (page 2 of the case). Garland said they spent about $10bn; the case says reserves were left at about $10bn (see traps). The whole economy was hit through one industry.
  • Class consensus on Angola's curse: Dutch disease clearly present; volatility clearly present; corruption and the political curse present; asset depletion not clear.
  • Dutch disease evidence: Exhibit 6 nominal kwanza per dollar 10.0 (2000), 22.1, 43.5, 74.6, 83.5, about 87 (2005) — a huge nominal depreciation — but CPI inflation was 325% (2000), 153%, 109%, 98%, so the kwanza did not depreciate fast enough and appreciated in real terms. This is why the real/nominal lesson came before the case. A student summed up the farmer's position: cannot export and domestic prices still high.
  • Colonial era was mixed, not only negative: 4th largest coffee producer, food self-sufficient, some manufacturing. The civil war is a huge confounder ("we cannot rerun history without the war"), but the case explicitly blames the overvalued currency too: agriculture faces cheap imports, manufacturing is reduced to cement and soft drinks — the bulky, quasi non-tradable goods. The well-off in oil enjoy a strong currency abroad; Luanda was at one point the world's most expensive city.
  • Volatility evidence: real GDP growth 3.1, 14.5, 3.3, 11.2, 20.6, 18.6, 21.1, 11.7 then −0.5 (2009); exports surging then falling; Exhibit 10 revenue 46.4, 46.7, 50.5 then 32.5% of GDP while expenditure stayed at 41.6% → overall balance from +8.8% (2008) to −9.1% of GDP (2009). Spending rose in the boom and did not come down. Of 48.2% of GDP tax revenue in 2008, only about 7 points were non-oil.
  • Asset depletion: unclear — reserves kept being discovered, OPEC quotas limit extraction, and the regime seemed to expect to stay in power (long horizon). A student (Jordan) noted they seemed to think long term.
  • Corruption: Sonangol, one of the most corrupt; avoiding IMF/World Bank audits to keep control.
  • Political resource curse: civil war tied to the Cold War (USSR backed MPLA, US backed UNITA); after 1990 the US no longer cared and the MPLA kept power. Little non-oil taxation → strange citizen–state relationship (as in Saudi Arabia and the UAE, only now discussing income tax).
  • Extractive institutions inherited from Portugal and captured by the new elite (like Brazil). Dos Santos left in 2017; João Lourenço (transcript: "Joel Lorenzo") says he fights corruption, but Garland sees uncertainty. Dos Santos's daughter was mentioned (name not recalled; UNSURE).
  • Policy ideas from students: rebuild institutions, invest in human capital and education, stop defending the kwanza / deal with the appreciation; elites took cheap dollars to Switzerland. How to avoid the Dutch disease is the bridge to Norway (a resource-rich counter-example with similarities).
  • Dual exchange rate (case p.10). Logic with 1 peso = $1 and 3 pesos = $1: resource exporters get the bad rate (1) because they are profitable anyway; manufactured exporters get the good rate (3) to encourage diversification. Imports: luxuries and foreign travel pay 3; essentials (medicines, basic food, education) pay 1 as a subsidy. China used it for Special Economic Zones in the 1980s then dropped it; Venezuela for education (Venezuelans were once IE's largest nationality, paying a third of the price).
  • Why it fails: an invitation to corruption. With connections you fake medicine-import papers, get $1m at the official rate, sell it round the corner at the parallel rate and turn 1m pesos into about 2.8m in days, then repeat. Venezuela: a study estimated about 15% of GDP stolen over 4–5 years. Angola: factor 2.9, 190% profit.
  • Argentina after 1:1 ended: the peso went to almost 4; dollar mortgages became unpayable (3,000 pesos became 9,000); Argentina asked the IMF for a dual rate, the IMF and World Bank refused, so it subsidized mortgages directly instead.
  • Calendar: Norway next session; then India and UK/Brexit with Allard, back to the US, Mexico with Garland in session 12.

Likely exam angles

  • Effect of a fixed rate below equilibrium (overvalued local currency) → exports less competitive and imports cheaper, cheaper external debt, citizens "richer" abroad (all of these); negatives: trade deficit.
  • Effect of a fixed rate above equilibrium → trade surplus and growth, but expensive imports, expensive external debt, negative wealth effect.
  • Short answer "dirty floating": currency floats but the central bank goes against the market — buys dollars to stop excess appreciation (resource boom or capital inflows), sells dollars to stop excess depreciation (capital flight, oil collapse; Lula effect, Angola 2009). Cost: buying dollars injects local money (inflation); selling drains reserves.
  • Mundell: why the US can run persistent trade deficits without the dollar collapsing → deficits financed by capital inflows driven by interest differentials and demand for dollar assets.
  • Twin deficits: China's dollar purchases financed both the US trade deficit and (via Treasuries) the fiscal deficit.
  • Dual rate: who gets which rate and why it breeds corruption (arbitrage between official and parallel rate).
  • Angola's nominal depreciation but real appreciation → inflation far above the nominal depreciation.
  • Resource curse short answer: list the five elements and say which appear in Angola (Dutch disease, volatility, corruption, political curse; asset depletion unclear).
  • Degree of openness formula and why large countries are less open.
  • BoP: which account holds FDI (financial), remittances (current, secondary income), debt forgiveness (capital).

Traps and look-alikes

  • Axis convention: pesos per dollar. "Below equilibrium" = overvalued local currency (cheap dollar). The posted sample question uses exactly this. A peg "below" is NOT a weak currency.
  • Under a below-equilibrium peg there is excess DEMAND for dollars (black market risk); under an above-equilibrium peg there is excess SUPPLY of dollars (central bank piles up reserves).
  • Dirty floating vs fixed: under dirty floating the central bank only leans against moves; under a fixed rate it must defend one level. Both require buying/selling dollars and both carry inflation/reserve costs.
  • Angola's case exhibit uses a real exchange rate index where a HIGHER number means APPRECIATION (73.3 in 2000 to 192.7 in 2009), the opposite of the nominal kwanza-per-dollar convention. Footnote 91 says so.
  • Angola reserves 2009–10: case says reserves had fallen to about $10bn (three months of imports) by June 2010; Garland said the BNA spent about $10bn. Use the case wording.
  • Mundell's dating: deck "in the 60s", class "in the 70s".
  • Dual-rate profit: factor 2.9 means 190% profit (2.9 − 1 = 1.9), not 290%.
  • Do not call the Angolan 2009 intervention "buying dollars": it SOLD reserves to buy kwanza (stop depreciation).
  • Capital account (tiny, special transfers) vs financial account (FDI, portfolio): easy to swap.
  • Resource pull effect (labor/materials move into the resource sector) is part of the Dutch disease in the case, not a separate element.
Session 9 · 1 Oct 2026 · Garland · complete notes

Session 9: Norway: The Embarrassment of Riches, the cure for Dutch disease, institutions and labor markets (Prof. Gonzalo Garland)

Norway is the counter-example to Angola: also resource-rich, but with strong institutions before oil was found, no corruption, no civil war and no asset-depletion mentality; the one element of the resource curse it does show is volatility. Garland used the HBS case "Norway: The Embarrassment of Riches" (Reinert, rev. 2018, set in 2012–13) and his deck "Dutch Disease II: the cure" (dirty floating, fixed rate with the Saudi example, and sterilization through a sovereign wealth fund). He also covered corruption measurement (Transparency International's CPI), why Norway stays out of the EU, the welfare state and incentives, and an extended detour on labor-market flexibility, South Africa, flexicurity and pension systems. This was his last session before the final exam apart from Mexico.

Slide-by-slide (Dutch Disease II: "The Cure", as presented in this class)

Slide: Cause of Dutch disease

  • The main cause of the negative effects is strong appreciation of the local currency from large inflows of foreign currency, so the main cure is controlling that appreciation.

Slide: Exchange rate

  • A new natural resource shifts dollar supply to S1; the local currency appreciates.

Slide: Dirty / managed floating

  • Resource sales shift S → S2 (appreciation). The central bank buys dollars (D1 → D2), selling local currency and depreciating it against the original appreciation. Risk: pumping local currency into the economy for a long time → inflation.

Slide: Fixed exchange rate

  • At S1/D1 nothing to do. If oil prices rise (S2) the central bank must buy dollars; if supply moves left it must sell dollars and remove local currency.
  • Class example: Saudi Arabia. SAMA (the central bank) absorbs excess dollars and injects riyals when oil is high; when oil falls it spends the reserves built in good times. It has held about 3.75 riyals per dollar for about 40 years and it seems to work (Garland trusts their monetary management). Open question: is 3.75 already too strong, creating a Dutch disease that discourages non-oil industry? A fixed rate at least stops any further appreciation.

Slide: Pros and cons

  • Dirty floating with heavy inflows → the central bank constantly buys dollars and injects local currency → inflation.
  • Fixed rate → same when oil is high; when oil falls the central bank spends reserves and drains liquidity.
  • Therefore, if large resource inflows are expected, it is better to "sterilize" them: keep the foreign currency outside the country, typically in a sovereign wealth fund. That is Norway's solution.

The case, section by section: "Norway: The Embarrassment of Riches" (HBS 713-061)

Introduction (Oct 2012 budget)

  • Finance minister Sigbjørn Johnsen, "the world's luckiest Minister of Finance", did not even use the full Action Rule quota — up to 4% of the fund's capital a year (its assumed long-term yield). Europe was cutting; Norway's biggest budget complaint was the price of Kobe steak.
  • HDI first in 9 of the last 11 years (Iceland interrupted in 2005–06 at its bubble peak).
  • Doubts: a child psychologist warned of spoiled children; some say Norwegians were happier in the 1950s; is Norway planning for life after oil?

Country overview

  • 5 million people (2012); immigrants over 28% of Oslo; most democratic country (EIU, 2011); PM Jens Stoltenberg (Labor, red–green coalition). From the 1990s a liberal turn to "industry neutrality".
  • Economy: oil, gas, lumber, minerals, fish, shipping, high-tech, arms (Kongsberg). 5th largest oil exporter and 3rd largest gas exporter, not in OPEC; oil production peaked in 2000. Hydropower supplies nearly 99% of domestic energy.
  • Krone about 18 US cents (early 2013), fluctuating with oil prices and seen as a safe haven. GDP per capita at PPP $54,479 (2012), 4th overall. Gini 5th lowest. Topped the Big Mac index ($7.84); Oslo world's most expensive city.
  • WEF competitiveness 15th (Switzerland 1st): 1st for prosperity, low inflation and credit rating, but 27th infrastructure, 47th railroads, 84th roads (between Jamaica and Ghana), 57th math and science. Some farm tariffs above 1,000% while the non-farm tariff average is 0.4%.

Country background

  • Never feudal; early division of powers; independence from Sweden in 1905 (368,200 to 184 in the referendum).
  • Historic exports: dried cod, timber, minerals; early industrialization with cheap hydropower (Norsk Hydro, 1905), much of it foreign-owned, leading to ownership limits.
  • An earlier sovereign fund (about 1905–1910, NOK 40m, a third of the budget, in British, French and German bonds) was dismantled after WWI with heavy losses.
  • WWII occupation (Quisling); postwar nation-building and industrial policy until the 1990s.

Norway, Europe and the world

  • Rejected EU membership by referendum in 1972 and 1994 but is in the EEA ("de facto membership"; only Malta complies better with EU rules). Agriculture still protected (a 344% beef tariff proposed in 2012).
  • Footloose shipping relocates to Cyprus and the Caymans; a 1.1% wealth tax and inheritance tax push some owners abroad. Over 1% of GDP given in aid; Nobel Peace Prize awarded in Oslo.

Black gold or devil's excrement? Hydrocarbons

  • Phillips Petroleum hit Ekofisk in late 1969 on its last scheduled well.
  • The "ten petroleum commandments" (1970–71 white paper, from Iraqi-born engineer Farouk Al-Kasim): national guidance and control of all activity on the shelf. Jens Evensen shaped the 200-nautical-mile Exclusive Economic Zone. The state took 51% of Hydro and created 100% state-owned Statoil; Saga for domestic competition.
  • Key point vs the resource curse: Norway already had robust institutions when Ekofisk came on line. After a 1962 Svalbard mining accident (21 deaths), civil servants were barred from sitting on state-company boards.
  • A 1973 white paper feared Dutch disease, expected people to want shorter hours, and called for research and active industrial policy to prepare for oil's end. Higher costs were tolerated to build domestic know-how ("the world's technology laboratory", Troll A platform). Statoil could claim 80% of discoveries; foreign share of fields fell from 91% (1965) to about 20% (2001).
  • Statoil partly privatized in 2001 (former chairman Finn Lied called it treason and "the greatest economic blunder"); merged with Hydro's oil arm in 2007.

Government Pension Fund – Global

  • Created in 1990 as the Government Petroleum Fund to absorb all oil and gas profits; renamed Government Pension Fund – Global in 2006 with no change to its strategy. About NOK 4 trillion in early 2013, roughly 140% of GDP, the world's largest SWF. Lost 23.3% in 2008 and recovered (counter-cyclical mandate).
  • "For fear of Dutch Disease" it may not invest in NOK-denominated assets, so it is both a stabilization fund and a savings fund.
  • Action Rule (since 2001): non-oil budget deficits can be covered up to the expected real return, about 4% a year; it does not distinguish investment from consumption; it is voluntary (unlike Alaska's constitutional limit). 2013: drawing 3.3% of the fund to cover nearly 12% of the budget.
  • Portfolio 60% equity / 40% fixed income, real estate up to 5%; may own up to 10% of a company, average under 1%.
  • Debate: Sachs — intergenerational sharing is best done fiscally; critics — "building a country vs building a piggy-bank" (Mediaas); Frisch — society can only save through productive investment; "Why buy luxury shopping districts in Paris while our roads collapse?" Could Norway without oil have had Nokia/Ericsson-type firms (GSM was invented by Norwegians)?

Responsible capitalism

  • Norfund and SN Power invest in developing countries; East Timor copied the petroleum fund. Ethical guidelines from 2004; blacklisting of tobacco, some weapons makers, Rio Tinto (environment) and Wal-Mart (2006, labor rights). Estimated cost NOK 10.6bn in returns since 2005.

Homo Petrolicus: welfare state, internationalization, homogeneity

  • "The market is a good servant but a bad master" (Stoltenberg). Flexicurity: lifelong retraining and a safety net meant to encourage risk-taking and entrepreneurship.
  • Critics: benefits at 66% of past income; twice Sweden's disability rate; by some estimates 20% of the working-age population on unemployment benefits; a 1% rise in benefits raises claimants by more than 1%; Norwegians work 23% less than before oil; workweek cut from 45 hours (1959) to 37.5 (1987). "Incentive trap" (Sætre coined "Homo Petrolicus"). Social security about 35% of the 2013 budget.
  • But: unemployment a steady 3.1%, employment ratio 75.8% (third in OECD), unionization about 84%.
  • Education: weak PISA, University of Oslo 111th (60 places behind Copenhagen); no engineer in government.
  • Immigration: of 315,000 new jobs since 2004, almost 200,000 went to foreigners; integration tensions; the July 2011 Breivik attacks (Oslo bomb and Utøya); trust actually rose afterwards.

Future prospects

  • Seland's early-1970s warning about "Kuwaitization". Non-oil industries finding it harder to compete; SAS pay cuts as a possible "canary". Has Norway put too many eggs in one basket? Election year 2013.

What the professor added in class

  • History: Norway was the poorer, more rural, agricultural and fishing Scandinavian country; Sweden and Denmark had higher incomes and more sophisticated economies. Oil reversed this.
  • Numbers he read out: GNI per capita $97,310 (2025), 3rd in the world after Bermuda and Switzerland; Iceland $89,000, Denmark $77,000, Sweden $63,000. A family of three around $300,000. Oslo's expense is an indirect indicator of currency strength.
  • Australia, Canada and Norway are sometimes called commodity (mineral) currencies; a student linked Australia's mining boom to a two-speed economy.
  • Institutions: The Narrow Corridor again — rule of law, education and culture build strong institutions, but no country is guaranteed to stay; this is a live debate in the US today. Garland avoided saying some cultures are prone to corruption; Scandinavian history has been built around equality and inclusion.
  • Corruption Perception Index (CPI here is not consumer prices): by Transparency International, built from surveys of individuals and businesses (have you had to bribe anyone?). It is perception-based, so biased, but probably reflects patterns. Top: Denmark and Norway about 88, Singapore about 85 (with New Zealand, Sweden near the top). Singapore pays public servants more than the private sector to attract talent and deter corruption.
  • Scale of Nordic scandals: the head of the oil fund (Garland first said Equinor, then corrected to the fund) nearly lost the job for accepting a ride on a banker's private plane from a Wharton conference to London, and apologized. Swedish state alcohol-monopoly buyers were taken on cruises by suppliers. Compare Angola's Sonangol, with sums in the tens of billions of dollars mentioned in class (a figure of $22–28bn; UNSURE).
  • Political resource curse absent: no civil wars, citizens pay high taxes so the state engages with them. Contrast the Mexican perception "why pay taxes if they will steal it", possibly similar in India.
  • Case problems discussed: unemployment-benefit use (a student cited about 20%), sick leave, comfort and weak incentives for start-ups; half the country wants lower taxes, half does not (Swedish elections also near 50–50). Swedish parental leave was described as around 15 months fully paid (transcript also says "almost 2 years"; UNSURE).
  • Infrastructure is poor partly because of geography (fjords, mountains): Garland recalled the Oslo–Bergen train taking about 10 hours (his recollection; UNSURE), and partly because the fund does not invest at home. University of Oslo below Copenhagen.
  • Why the fund does not invest in Norway (student answer endorsed): bringing the dollars home means buying kroner, which appreciates the currency and destabilizes the economy (Dutch disease). Angola instead spent its surplus domestically, causing inflation and unsustainable growth.
  • Fund facts from class: renamed from Petroleum Fund to Pension Fund because Norway is aging and will need it for pensions after oil — a state-owned "own capital" system invested abroad in stocks. Garland put it at about $4 trillion, about half a million dollars per Norwegian, the single largest SWF (China has more in total across several funds; Middle East and Singapore have funds too). Criticized for buying parking lots in France. It holds small minority stakes but uses pressure (Walmart).
  • Garland's version of the Action Rule: in a genuine emergency about 4% can be taken out (used in the pandemic; he mentioned a figure of about 8% at one point; UNSURE); money must come back slowly or it would destabilize the economy.
  • Volatility is the curse element Norway does show: Exhibit 2 growth 3.3, 3.9, 2.7 ... then −1.4% in 2009 when oil collapsed. Diversification is weak: non-oil sectors not growing strongly; mining/manufacturing/oil share broadly unchanged.
  • Paradox: Norway is first or second in the world in electric vehicles per capita and has many electric ships; hydro covers domestic power.
  • Government outlays (Exhibit 7, 2010) about 55% of GDP, among the highest; France has since overtaken (about 56%); usual suspects Denmark, Finland, Sweden, Austria, Belgium, Netherlands. Spending is funded mainly by taxes (including oil taxes), not the fund. Fiscal pressure = taxes / GDP, which can differ from spending / GDP by the deficit.
  • Incentives: if a degree pays only 10–20% more than waiting tables, why study? Immigration is becoming contentious in an aging country; the US absorbs immigrants into one large culture more easily than small European nations with their own languages. Breivik keeps suing the state over prison conditions — an illustration of how strong rights institutions are.
  • EU status: Norway is not in the EU; rejected it twice; the last vote close (Garland: about 52–48). He also said the most recent vote was only about starting discussions (UNSURE; the case gives 1972 and 1994 referendums). Iceland, Norway and Switzerland have an open-door invitation, are in Schengen and get many benefits without membership. Reasons to stay out: protect fish and cheese from EU quotas and rules, already among the richest countries, and (student point) keep an independent monetary policy to manage oil money. Counter-argument: more bargaining power inside the EU (e.g. with US tariffs) and Norway already pays without full say. Norway is the most likely of the three to join one day; Switzerland is happy with its cantonal direct democracy (about 70% against). Iceland's recent vote was described as narrowly against (UNSURE).
  • Labor markets (classical): labor supply comes from people, demand from firms (reverse of goods markets); price = real wage (no money illusion). Flexible markets move to equilibrium, i.e. the natural rate of unemployment (full employment is not zero unemployment; people out of the labor force are not counted). US example: firms fire millions in a recession, real wages fall, they rehire in the recovery (and people complain the new jobs are bad and badly paid).
  • Europe is rigid: Spain's severance of 45 days per year worked (favoring older workers, discouraging hiring); too-high minimum wages create excess labor supply = unemployment; France makes firing very hard and courts can order reinstatement; Germany once had "professional unemployed" abusing benefits.
  • South Africa: COSATU, the union confederation historically allied to the ANC, acts like a labor monopoly and pushes very high wages; youth unemployment around 60% (class estimate). Insiders–outsiders dilemma: unionized insiders protected, young outsiders locked out. Firms in tradable sectors cannot compete with high labor costs; non-tradables (electricity) can pass costs on.
  • Flexicurity: Europe will not abandon the welfare state but wants to add flexibility while keeping security. Austrian backpack: worker and employer pay monthly into a government-run insurance; in a crisis the firm can fire without paying severance because the insurance pays. Something similar in Sweden.
  • Pensions: pay-as-you-go (Bismarck; Europe, Japan, partly the US) is an intergenerational transfer under strain from aging; own capital (created in Chile; Latin America, some Eastern Europe) puts mandatory contributions in individual accounts run by private pension administrators and is not hit by demographics. Europe does not switch because the transition cost (paying current pensioners while contributions move) is estimated at 15–20% of GDP. Indian students described the Provident Fund (12% employee + 12% employer). Norway's fund is effectively state-owned own capital invested abroad, which makes Norway the envy of Europe.

Likely exam angles

  • Why Norway's fund invests only abroad → to sterilize oil revenue and avoid real appreciation (Dutch disease).
  • Three ways to fight Dutch-disease appreciation → dirty floating (buy dollars; risk inflation), fixed rate (Saudi; inflation in booms, reserve loss in busts), sterilization via SWF (Norway, preferred).
  • Which resource-curse element does Norway still show? → volatility (2009 GDP −1.4%).
  • Why Norway escaped the curse → strong institutions before discovery, no corruption, state control and domestic know-how, fiscal rule.
  • What the CPI (corruption) measures and its weakness → perception surveys by Transparency International; possible bias.
  • Why Norway is not in the EU → protect fish/agriculture, already rich, keep monetary independence; it is in the EEA and Schengen.
  • Short answer on labor markets: rigidities (severance, minimum wage, strong unions) → unemployment above the natural rate; insiders–outsiders; flexicurity / Austrian backpack as the European answer.
  • Action Rule → spending of oil money limited to about 4% (the fund's expected real return).
  • Saudi peg 3.75 → central bank buys dollars in oil booms, sells in busts; question whether it is too strong.

Traps and look-alikes

  • Fund size: class "about $4 trillion" (about $500,000 per person); case "about NOK 4 trillion" in early 2013 (about 140% of GDP; at 18 US cents per krone that is roughly $700bn). Do not mix currencies.
  • Action Rule: case = annual draw up to the expected real return (4%) to cover the non-oil deficit (3.3% drawn in 2013); class = up to about 4% in emergencies such as the pandemic. Know the case definition.
  • Name change: case says 1990 Petroleum Fund renamed GPF-G in 2006 "with no change to strategy"; class linked the name to aging and future pensions.
  • Income figures: class GNI per capita $97,310 (2025); case GDP per capita at PPP $54,479 (2012). Different measure and year.
  • Unemployment: case 3.1% total; the "20%" is the share on unemployment benefits by some estimates, not the unemployment rate.
  • CPI = Corruption Perception Index in this session, not consumer price index.
  • Norway: in EEA and Schengen, NOT in the EU and NOT in OPEC (Angola is in OPEC).
  • Sterilization here means keeping resource dollars out of the domestic FX market (in a fund abroad), not only the central-bank bond operation.
  • Dirty floating cure vs fixed cure: both inject local currency when buying dollars → inflation risk; only sterilization avoids it.
  • Oslo–Bergen "10 hours" and Swedish leave "15 months / 2 years" are classroom recollections; do not treat as data.
Session 10 · 8 Oct 2026 · Allard · complete notes

Session 10: Japan's surplus and demography, then China and India growth models (Prof. Gayle Allard)

Allard finished Japan and then the second half of the "India and China" deck. On Japan she explained why a surplus country's currency tends to rise, how Japan held the yen down, the 1985 Plaza Accord (yen up about 50% in two years), the paradox of the world's highest public debt in a country of savers, the life-cycle theory of saving and how ageing could flip Japan into a net dissaver, and the demography and pensions problem. On China and India she went through investment, government spending, sources of growth, The Economist's view of India's non-mercantilist growth, expected currency trends (rupee down, yuan should be up but is held down), India's services-based model, the risk/opportunity table for an investor, and the China shocks 1.0 and 2.0 with the shift of manufacturing jobs to other countries. Two articles go with this session: the FT on China's unreliable statistics and the NYT on the hidden policies behind China's export boom. This session feeds the named short-answer topic indicators of risk or opportunity for an investor and the sample MCQs on India vs China and on the driver of Japan's surplus (savings).

Slide-by-slide

Japan deck, slide 34: The current account and the yen

  • The current-account surplus gave Japan growth and strengthened the yen.
  • The root of a current-account surplus is a high savings rate relative to investment (charts: gross national savings and savings vs investment, US vs Japan).
  • Japan may eventually develop a current-account deficit as it ages and becomes a net dissaver.

Japan deck, slide 38: Differences between deficit and surplus countries

CA deficit countries (depend on foreign capital)CA surplus countries (depend on foreign demand and on their currency)
Consume more than they produceProduce more than they consume
Can drive their own growth with domestic demandNeed foreign demand to keep GDP as high as it is
Must borrow abroad to keep consumption highMust lend to foreign buyers
If the rest of the world disappeared, GDP would be higherIf the rest of the world disappeared, GDP would be lower
Currencies tend to fall as the deficit growsCurrencies tend to rise as the surplus grows; often experience deflation

Japan deck, slides 39-40: Currency value is key for a country relying on foreign demand

  • The yen is traditionally strong because of the export surplus; it appreciated by 50% in the mid-1980s under pressure from the US and other developed countries. Many trace Japan's asset bubble, bust, lost decade and deflation to this.
  • QE aimed to weaken the yen and boost net exports; but Japan imports nearly all its energy (especially since the tsunami). Since the pandemic the yen has hit historic lows as Japan kept expansive monetary policy while others raised rates.
  • Slide 40: yen/US dollar rate since 1970 (OECD).

Japan deck, slides 41-43: Vulnerability to crises and trade conflicts

  • Tendency to save and hoard; need for outside demand given demography, slow productivity, savings and a tendency to invest abroad; collapsing world trade; broken supply chains (Japan incentivizing reshoring from China); Trump's trade war; importance of the yen.
  • Good macro points: high savings and cash-rich companies, which could support workers in a crisis.
  • Slides 42-43: Trump 2.0 tariffs and their effect on Japan.

Japan deck, slides 44-53: Demography and pensions

  • Fertility rate 1.2; population shrinking about 0.55% a year since 2010 (peak 128.1m).
  • World: Japan the fastest-ageing country; Eastern Europe and Russia declining; Western Europe may shrink; North America still rising mainly through immigration; Asia in rapid demographic transition; Middle East and Africa still exploding. Some countries "in danger of extinction".
  • Japan's working-age population has been shrinking for almost 30 years; demographic change will strain welfare systems, especially pensions.

Japan deck, slides 54-55: Immigration

  • 3.95 million registered immigrants in 2025 (a record), 3.2% of the population (28% Chinese, 16% Korean, 13% Vietnamese), against 11.5% on average in developed countries. Japan would need 10 million immigrants by 2050 to keep the worker-retiree ratio constant.
  • But the Japanese are not enthusiastic: Nihonjinron (uniqueness literature); "gaijin" means outsider; only limited guest-worker experiments; requirements recently tightened.

India-China deck, slides 32-38: Exports, investment, government, sources of growth

  • Exports % GDP (chart marks China's WTO entry and the financial crisis); net exports (Penn World Tables); investment (GFCF) % GDP; government spending % GDP; sources of growth (C, I, G, X-M) for China, India and Japan.

Slide 39: The Economist on India

  • "India's membership of Asia remains primarily cartographic." India's growth (around 6% that year) is below China's but impressive amid global turmoil; government stimulus plays only a small part; capital and infrastructure investment compare favorably with China's; the hot story is domestic demand.
  • India is no mercantilist adding to global imbalances: it imports more than it exports, creating global demand. Its long-run growth will overtake China's.

Slides 40-42: Expected currency trends

  • Indian rupee against the US$: expected trend is depreciation.
  • Chinese yuan: expected appreciation, but the Council on Foreign Relations estimated it undervalued by 20-35% against the dollar.

Slides 43-44: Growth models

  • India's growth relied most heavily on services; Indian exports tend to be in lower value-added sectors.
  • India is 3% of global manufacturing output, China 28-30%. In commercial services the shares are closer: India 4.3%, China 5.4-6.7% and rising.
  • In services China specializes in high-tech value manufacturing and digital commerce; India in software and IT-enabled services.

Slide 45: Risk and opportunity (first version)

Sources of riskSources of opportunity
India: international borrowing to cover external deficitChina: controlled inflation
India: institutional risks?China: impressive investment process; competitiveness and leadership in many key industries
India: tendency for currency to depreciateIndia: strong domestic demand to drive growth
China: dependence on foreign demandIndia: attractive import market to serve consumer demand
China: reliance on C and I to replace X-MIndia: tendency for currency to depreciate; free float
China: tendency for currency to appreciate; currency manipulationIndia: excellent demographics
China: excess capacity, global reactions; deflation

Slides 46-49: FDI, Make in India, India's IT sector

  • FDI net inflows % GDP chart; "Make in India" campaign to attract manufacturing FDI.
  • Knowledge-based services (Bangalore, Chennai, Mumbai) grew 50% a year since 1991 with "benign neglect" from government; English speakers attracted customer-facing services; abundant engineers; services always internationally oriented. Manufacturing is rising (electronics, auto components) despite bureaucracy and infrastructure obstacles.
  • India: 2.7% of world service exports (40% computer services, 31% other business services); BPO and IT 5.6% of the economy ($71.7bn), $47bn of exports, 2.23m direct and 8m indirect jobs; cost advantage of 60-70% over source markets.

Slide 50: Rising labor costs and the new industrial revolution

  • These are eroding the manufacturing competitiveness of both countries.

Slides 51-52: China shocks 1.0 and 2.0 (and 3.0?)

  • China shock 1.0 (after WTO entry): surging surpluses aimed mainly at the US. Not all the decline was due to Chinese imports; probably some 2-2.4m US jobs. The US resisted protectionism and did not accuse China of currency manipulation. Trump and Brexit (2016) had roots here. In 2008-2009 China hit a plateau as export demand fell.
  • China shock 2.0: another export surge from 2020, aimed mainly at Europe but also the US, driven by industrial policy to move up value chains and win technological leadership; China raised its surplus through import limits and subsidies. Protectionism in the US was now strong (empowering Trump), and also in the EU.

Slides 53-56: Market share, subsidies, jobs

  • China has gained market share massively in many high-tech industries; subsidies exist everywhere but China's have become a problem.
  • China shock 3.0? World factory employment has risen by 4m. China's export-supported factory jobs are down 16% since 2013, and its share of the world total fell from 37% to 30%. China still employs 134m factory workers, more than twice the advanced economies combined, and its share of world manufactured exports keeps rising; its factory employment is shrinking as productivity rises and labor-intensive stages move to lower-wage competitors.
  • Advanced economies lost manufacturing jobs from 75m (1995) to 60m (2010), then stabilized (60.1m to 60.3m in 2013-2022). Other emerging economies added 22m after 2013 (to 186m); Vietnam, Indonesia, Nigeria, Mexico and Pakistan together added 22m.

Slides 57-58: India's growth future and the "four horsemen" (not reached in class)

  • Potential growth used to be lower than China's; now both potential and actual growth are above China's (6-7%). Policy-induced stagflation had kept growth low; hopes of a "Modi effect"; fiscal and structural reform could raise it. Concerns over autocracy and "ethnic democracy" under Modi.
  • Four horsemen: backlash against globalization (limits export-led growth); climate change (a 1-degree rise in a 25C country cuts per-capita output up to 1.5%; an average low-income country could lose about a tenth of output by 2100, IMF); automation and "premature deindustrialization" (robots replacing low-skilled workers; growth miracles like Japan's and China's may become impossible); India must invest in its people.

Slides 59-61: China's housing bubble, Evergrande, overcapacity (not reached in class)

  • China used more cement in 2011-2013 than the US in the whole 20th century (Roubini).
  • Evergrande: the world's most indebted developer, $300bn liabilities, defaulted on offshore debt; mortgage boycotts over unfinished homes; property was up to one-third of China's growth.
  • "Brute force capital accumulation": overcapacity is capacity domestic demand cannot absorb, which pushes (often subsidized) exports. China produces about 30% of world manufactures but consumes about 18%. Manufacturing output $625bn (2004) to $4.658trn (2023); exports $607bn to $3.513trn.

Slides 66-67: When the bubble bursts; China's growth future (not reached in class)

  • 45% of household assets in housing; real estate 29% of GDP; about 60% of bank loan books tied to property. Risk of cascading defaults and growth below 4%. But China is different: huge government cash and savings, strict control over lending, capital flows and currency, state-owned banks, "three red lines" for indebted developers. A slow-motion train wreck.
  • Growth driven by monetary and fiscal expansion, credit, investment and exports: 10.5% average 1991-2011, 6.4% under Xi; potential may slow to 4% or less. Private debt bigger than the US in 2007. As Japan shows, the transition from a long boom is hard. Can China shift from investment-led to consumption-led growth?

Slides 72-86: Institutions (not reached in class)

  • Inglehart: mass democracy has risen only in market economies; a strong middle class is a necessary but not sufficient condition. All developed countries are democracies. Is China a new model?
  • India: largest democracy, free press, but corruption, bureaucracy, caste and "reservations", nationalist populism. Rajiv Gandhi estimated 85% of development spending went to bureaucrats. Both bureaucratic; which is more stable?
  • Indian bureaucratic revolutions: biometric ID for 850m people (food-ration theft used to take about 40%); 150m new bank accounts for cash transfers. GST (1 July 2017) replaced 12 tax rates, created a single market; demonetization targeted the black market.
  • Investor question: democracy or autocracy? Xi as leader for life may reduce innovation; concerns about Modi.

Slides 87-99: Demography (not reached in class)

  • Fertility: India 1.9, China "now below 1?". Population growth: India 1.2%, China 0.4% (US Census charts). China's working-age population falling since 2011.
  • Population growth: labor-force growth is part of potential growth (otherwise you need immigration); the dependency ratio in either direction is a drag; a young population tends to be more dynamic.
  • The demographic dividend was 15-25% of China's growth 1980-2000; India is in the midst of its own.
  • Second risk/opportunity table adds: China's demographic crisis (slower growth, fiscal pressure), authoritarian model magnifying errors, possible institutional crisis; India's ethnic nationalism; India's growing population and democracy as opportunities; both have large internal markets.

Slides 101-106: Future (not reached in class)

  • China scores above India on openness, macro stability, education and infrastructure, government efficiency; perhaps 10-15 years ahead in reforms.
  • India's advantages: young, growing population (bigger than China by 2030?), English (spoken by 2%), a highly educated elite, democracy since 1947, high-tech services, more self-sustained growth without bubbles or debt overhang, Modi's reform and infrastructure push.
  • Summary table: opportunities are large markets, rising incomes, middle classes; current account: exports for China, imports for India. Risks: ageing (China); China's international lending, foreign-demand dependence and rising currency; India's international borrowing and declining currency; regulations and corruption.
  • Can the world sustain more giants? India as big as the US by 2045, China by 2032; world output six- to seven-fold by 2050; energy use at US levels would be 13x the US; 3bn cars.

The articles, section by section

FT Big Read: "The growing problem with China's unreliable numbers" (Thomas Hale, Nov 2025)

  • Economist Gao Shanwen (then at SDIC Securities in Hong Kong) said nobody knows China's true growth; his guess was about 2% a year in the past two to three years against an official figure near 5%. He then lost his position and went silent for almost a year.
  • China has increased opacity: discontinued data series and restricted researchers. Eswar Prasad: opaque collection, unclear definitions, no sampling transparency.
  • China is the only big economy that does not publish a quarterly GDP breakdown by expenditure (consumption, investment, net exports). The IMF's 2024 grade for its national accounts is C on an A-D scale, the same as India and below Vietnam. Beijing disagreed, saying production-side GDP is the "appropriate primary approach".
  • Official GDP has almost always hit or beaten the target (about 5% now), except during Covid.
  • Three GDP approaches: expenditure (C + I + NX), production (output minus inputs) and income (earnings and taxes), which should match. Until 1993 China used the Soviet-style material product system.
  • Risk of local officials over-reporting; Li Keqiang (2007, via WikiLeaks) called GDP "man-made" and watched electricity use, rail cargo and bank lending instead.
  • "Broken Abacus?" (Rosen and Bao, 2014) found China's 2008 9% growth was, if anything, an underestimate; transparency has since gone backwards.
  • Fixed asset investment: over Rmb50tn in 2023 (40% of GDP); in 2025 it is down 1.7% and its property part down 14.7%, yet official GDP investment shows no decline. In 2018 the NBS stopped publishing sectoral values; in 2021 it dropped a price series; in 2023 land-sales data and (temporarily) youth unemployment were suspended. Holz sees a pattern of removing data that would reveal slowdowns.
  • Hsieh (Chicago) used tax data (2019) to show GDP was overstated; NBS downward adjustments averaged about 5% of GDP a year from the mid-2000s but were not enough. He links the clampdown to a political shift from 2012-2013.

NYT: "The Hidden Policies That Power China's Export Boom" (Keith Bradsher, 6 Oct 2026)

  • China sustains exports with export tax rebates and a weak currency. The EU's trade deficit with China exceeds $1.1bn a day; Brussels complains of subsidies and cheap loans.
  • The renminbi is down more than 14% in inflation-adjusted terms from early 2022 against a broad basket; over the same period China's trade surplus nearly doubled. Its long slide after the March 2022 Shanghai lockdown took it down about 20% (BIS); it has strengthened somewhat in the past 15 months but is far below pre-Covid levels.
  • VAT rebates to exporters reached $318.2bn last year, 1.5% of GDP; they were about a tenth of tax revenue before Covid and nearly an eighth now. China plans to end rebates for battery exporters next year.
  • Last year's record trade surplus: $1.19 trillion. Growth goal 4.5-5%.
  • Costs at home: a weak renminbi raises the cost of oil and imports and squeezes households; rebates drain government revenue already hit by the housing slump (existing-home prices down about 40% since summer 2021). Abroad: resentment and protectionism, especially in Europe.
  • Chinese economists (Shen Guobing, Huang Qifan) argue a stronger currency may help and that rebates, effectively export subsidies, should be cut. Bert Hofman: cutting rebates would free more revenue for health and education than appreciation would.
  • Not only currency: innovation in batteries, EVs and solar and fierce domestic competition also explain export strength; Zhao Jian says low prices rest on suppressed wages and margins.

What the professor added in class

  • EXAM: Surplus countries and their currency: a surplus country depends on foreign demand and the value of its currency is critical; if the currency rises, exports fall and imports become attractive, so the growth engine weakens. Market forces automatically push a surplus country's currency up.
  • EXAM: How to hold a currency down: buy foreign currency and foreign assets (e.g. US bonds), which floods the market with your own currency. Keeping a currency down is easy (just build reserves) and can last a long time; keeping it up is hard because you run out of reserves and must stop.
  • Size matters: small countries (Colombia, Mexico in the past, Norway) can do it without anyone caring; when a big country does it (Japan then, China now), partners feel the competition and protest.
  • Plaza Accord, 1985: Japan flooded the US with manufactured goods (first toys, then refrigerators, TVs, cars). The US, with partners including Germany, told Japan its cheap yen was unfair and against the IMF agreement (rarely enforced), and threatened it (e.g. tariffs). Japan agreed to let the yen float and it rose about 50% in two years, a terrible blow to exporters used to a cheap yen. She stressed this as the start of the asset-bubble burst, the lost decade and the slowdown. Rule of the game: losing to a floating currency is fair; losing to a suppressed one is not.
  • Germany had the same problem with an ever-rising Deutsche Mark (the first EMS crises were about the DM rising); the euro solved it because its main partners now share its currency.
  • Abenomics wanted a weaker yen but could not intervene openly any more, so it sought inflation to bring the yen down. The yen chart shows the 1985 jump and a recently weak (but not ultra-weak) yen.
  • Lessons from Japan to carry to China: a tendency to save and a need for outside demand under an export-oriented strategy. Trump's tariffs hit Japan and China; Japan's export value and volume fell.
  • EXAM: Debt and savings paradox: Japan has the highest public debt to GDP and also the highest saving. Different agents: households and corporations save; the government borrows, a Keynesian response because the private sector will not spend. An idea to "kill two birds with one stone": a (higher) tax on savings, which would cut the incentive to save and raise revenue, but would be very unpopular.
  • EXAM: Life-cycle of saving: young people dissave (debt for house, MBA, furniture); middle-aged people pay off debt and save until retirement; retirees run down savings. Japan's average age is about 48, still in the saving phase; if it moves toward 60, Japan becomes a dissaver, sells bonds, Japanese bonds find fewer buyers, and the current account could even flip to deficit. Raising the retirement age (60 to 65 or 70) delays the flip.
  • Japan's population shrinks about half a percent a year since 2010; the world fertility average was 5 in 1950. The US survives demographically through immigration (otherwise fertility around 1.8).
  • An Economist calculation of the year each country's population would disappear: Hong Kong first, then Portugal; Spain is on the list; China disappears before Japan; India and the US are not on the chart.
  • Fertility rate = births per woman over her lifetime; she noted it may be underestimated because statisticians stop counting around age 40. In most poorer countries women have fewer children than they say they want; India is the exception that wants fewer.
  • Class counterpoint (a student): a shrinking population eases pressure on resources and AI/robots may offset labor shortages; Allard agreed growth will be slower and that we may all be heading where Japan is.
  • EXAM: Old-age dependency ratio (working-age people per pension-age person): OECD about 4 in 2008, Turkey almost 10 (pensions cheap), Japan 2.8 then and 1.2 projected for 2050, meaning each worker supports almost a whole pension. Even at 2.1 each worker pays half a pension. She called this probably the biggest economic problem.
  • Pension solutions she likes: lift or remove the mandatory retirement age; basic low state pensions plus incentivized private saving (US, Switzerland, UK); a means-tested income top-up for workers like the US Earned Income Tax Credit. She is against universal basic income as unaffordable. Japan still largely rejects immigration.
  • Shocks: big countries joining global trade deliver big shocks; India and China have delivered powerful shocks since the 1990s, especially since 2000.
  • Investment: India's investment rate is very impressive, about 30% of GDP, 1.5 times the developed-country norm of about 20%. China's is about 45%, a level "we never see anywhere".
  • What can go wrong with China's investment: it went into property, which collapsed slowly (a "slow-motion train wreck") because the state controls banks, capital flows and lending. From about 2012 investment shifted to infrastructure and green energy (solar). Overinvesting creates overcapacity (producing more than you can sell), and with consumption at 40% of GDP the domestic demand is not there. After the financial crisis China raised investment by about 5 points of GDP or more to keep growth going. Students mentioned China's giant dam on the Brahmaputra (Yarlung Tsangpo) affecting India and Bangladesh.
  • Government spending: close to 50% of GDP in Europe, below 40% in the US and Japan; India about 10% (few welfare programs); China about 16% but rising since the financial crisis, again to keep growth going. Investment and government spending are borrowed money.
  • Sources of growth: China's consumption share fell over time while investment rose; net exports up a little; India's consumption fell then rose as it got richer (the normal pattern), investment well above 20%, and net exports subtract from growth.
  • EXAM: Where is the risk for an investor in C + I + G + (X - M)? Class answer: China, because it is extremely dependent on external demand and getting more so, must keep exports going at any cost and may provoke a reaction; India is more self-reliant on domestic demand.
  • Rupee: should fall against the dollar; good for exports; if your inflation is higher than partners' your currency falls by roughly the difference; risk of a vicious circle (depreciation raises inflation, which pushes the currency down further). The rupee was worth about a dollar in 1913 and 1947. Before 1990 India intervened to support the rupee, using reserves.
  • An Indian student described a recent scheme inviting non-resident Indians to make long-term US dollar fixed deposits to boost reserves and steady the rupee amid FDI outflows; Allard tied it to the BoP: inflows on the financial account offset the current-account deficit. (Student's figures for the size of the scheme: UNSURE.)
  • Yuan: she first said it "should appreciate" with the surplus, but the chart shows depreciation, which tells you it is artificial: the government intervenes to keep it cheap (devalued in the early 1990s, then held flat; allowed to rise a little after WTO entry in 2001 when partners complained). Possible because the government owns the banks and controls capital flows. Recent estimate: undervalued by 20-35% against the dollar. If it floated, competitiveness would suffer badly. Comparing exchange-rate levels (6 yuan vs about 95 rupees per dollar) says nothing; look at trends.
  • Manipulation only works if few countries do it; if many did, the system would be a mess. That is why Bretton Woods fixed rates after WWII, and later the US moved to floating so the market sets competitive levels. The cost of manipulation falls on trade partners through job displacement.
  • Services model: India is unusual: countries normally go agriculture, then manufacturing, then services; India went heavily into services early (already in 1970). India is about 3% of global manufacturing, China almost 30%; closer in services. Students noted engineering services for US manufacturers and call centers moving to the Philippines.
  • Risk/opportunity discussion: India's external deficit means it must borrow all the time; institutional risks raised by Indian students (civil unrest, media control, voter-roll purges, platform censorship); a weak rupee is a risk for asset holders but an advantage for export manufacturing. An Indian manufacturer said Indian manufacturing depends on foreign demand because it cannot beat Chinese prices at home; clothing and auto exports have suffered (economies of scale and overcapacity in China). China: excess capacity (if the world reacts you cannot sell), flirting with deflation (India "doesn't even know what it is"). Opportunities: India's domestic demand and excellent demographics. She said she finds China "a little scary"; a student countered that China is far more stable internally. She deliberately did not list institutional risk for China because the system looks set to last.
  • Minimum wages have risen a lot in China and little in India, which should make it hard for China to sell into India, but currency manipulation can offset it.
  • China shock 1.0: import penetration rose and US manufacturing employment fell; not all trade (automation too); economists think 2 to 2.5 million jobs; nobody reacted; part of where Trump comes from. In 2008-2009 China could not keep raising exports.
  • China shock 2.0: from 2020, currency manipulation and huge subsidies; China wanted to move up the technology chain and escape competition from India and others. Since Trump's first trade war (2018) China imports less and exports much more. Nobody complains about Indian imports because there is no manipulation behind them.
  • China's market share: solar panels from about 6% to 80%, plus shipbuilding and steel; massive overinvestment. Subsidized credit: Chinese firms borrow well below the base rate because the state owns the financial sector; India gets no such subsidy. Trump's weakening of the WTO removed the channel to complain.
