  • Manufacturing jobs: advanced economies stable (even slightly up) after the early period; China peaked around 2006-2007 and has lost about 20-22 million; others gained: India about 1.4 million (small for its size), Vietnam, Nigeria, Indonesia, Mexico, Pakistan, Turkey. Indian students said India was not ready (infrastructure) and lost opportunities, e.g. clothing to Bangladesh. A Turkish student suggested Turkey may be the biggest winner relative to population, helped by a booming defense industry.
  • She ran out of time before India's and China's growth futures, institutions and demography (slides 57 onward).

Likely exam angles

  • Sample MCQ: the main driver of Japan's chronic surplus is the savings habits of the Japanese (not a dollar peg, low costs or the budget deficit).
  • Short answer: "Indicators of risk or opportunity for an investor in India vs China." Use the C + I + G + (X - M) breakdown, the current account (China surplus, India deficit), currency trend (yuan held down, rupee depreciating), inflation (China near deflation, India controlled), demography (India's dividend vs China's ageing), institutions (India's democracy and fragility vs China's stability and opacity), data quality (FT).
  • MCQ: why it is easier to keep a currency undervalued than overvalued (you accumulate reserves rather than run them down).
  • MCQ: what the Plaza Accord did (yen rose about 50% in two years, 1985; seen as triggering the bubble and lost decade).
  • MCQ: characteristics of a surplus country (produces more than it consumes, lends abroad, currency tends to rise, deflation risk) vs a deficit country.
  • MCQ: what is overcapacity (production capacity domestic demand cannot absorb, exported with subsidies).
  • MCQ: which is NOT a tool China uses to support exports (e.g. "a freely floating currency").
  • MCQ: FT article: which GDP breakdown China does not publish quarterly (expenditure); IMF grade C.
  • MCQ: China shock 1.0 vs 2.0 (1.0: post-WTO, aimed at US, no reaction; 2.0: from 2020, aimed mainly at Europe, high-tech, subsidies, protectionist backlash).
  • MCQ: life-cycle saving: an ageing country eventually dissaves and its surplus can shrink or reverse.

Traps and look-alikes

  • Japan's surplus driver is savings, not the government deficit (the government is the dissaver) and not a dollar peg.
  • Plaza year: 1985 (deck says "mid-1980s"; a raw class note says 1980, which is wrong).
  • The yuan "should" appreciate (surplus) but is held down; the rupee depreciates naturally (deficit, higher inflation). On the opportunity side the slide lists the rupee's depreciation and free float as an opportunity (cheaper entry, export competitiveness) and also as a risk (asset values).
  • China shock US job losses: deck 2-2.4m; class 2-2.5m.
  • China's manufacturing jobs: deck says export-supported factory jobs down 16% since 2013 (share 37% to 30%) and 134m still employed; class read the chart as a peak around 2006-2007 and a loss of about 20-22 million. The 22m on the slide is jobs gained by other emerging economies after 2013.
  • Government spending figures (India about 10%, China about 16%) are class readings of a chart; Europe close to 50%, US and Japan below 40%.
  • Investment: India about 30% of GDP, China about 45%; developed countries about 20%.
  • The FT's economist (Gao Shanwen) worked at a state-linked brokerage (SDIC Securities), not in the government statistics office as described loosely in class; his estimate was about 2% vs an official 5%.
  • NYT renminbi figures: "down more than 14%" (real, broad basket, since early 2022) vs "about 20%" (the long slide after the 2022 lockdown). Different windows, not a contradiction.
  • Rebates: $318.2bn, 1.5% of GDP (not 15%). Trade surplus $1.19 trillion; EU deficit $1.1 billion a day.
  • Dependency ratio is working-age people per retiree (higher is better for pensions): Turkey 10 good, Japan 1.2 in 2050 bad.
  • Japan's public debt: slide says it has declined to 248% of GDP, and debt is in yen and owned domestically, which limits the risk.
Session 11 · 13 Oct 2026 · Allard · complete notes

Session 11: The European economy, EU vs US, and Brexit (Prof. Gayle Allard)

Allard taught from her deck "The European Economy" (EU, Spain, UK). In class she covered why Europe unified (peace), the levels of economic integration (free trade area, customs union, common market, economic union), deepening and widening (enlargements and candidates), the EU vs the eurozone, a structural comparison of the EU and the US (population, GDP, income, sectors, unemployment, current account, demography, productivity, regulation, R&D, trust, the welfare state, inequality, the middle class), and Brexit (history, the vote, Article 50, the menu of options and their costs, consequences, and the Rodrik trilemma and populism). She ran out of time before the macro, fiscal, eurozone-policy (optimal currency area, NextGen EU, Greece, Spain's internal devaluation) and climate slides; those are summarized below from the deck because they are exam material (several overlap with the Spain and Germany sessions). This session feeds the named short-answer topic EU vs US comparison and the sample MCQ "Which comparison between the EU and the United States is correct?" (answer on the slide: the EU's debt risk is similar to the US).

Slide-by-slide

Slide 1: Outline

  • History; structure and indicators; macro trends; economic policymaking in the eurozone; Brexit; the eurozone crisis; climate and outlook.

Slide 3: What do countries gain from integrating?

  • Free trade: efficiency and consumer surplus from comparative advantage (more variety, better and cheaper goods, higher real incomes, resources used efficiently).
  • Free capital flows: capital used more efficiently (more capital for some, more return for others).
  • Free movement of people: home countries alleviate poverty (but brain drain); host countries see aggregate supply shift out (more GDP, less unemployment and inflation; possibly lower native wages).
  • Single currency: lower transaction costs, intensified four freedoms, a world-class currency with psychological impact.
  • Win-win overall, but some groups lose; government should compensate the "losers".

Slides 4-5: The biggest reason: peace; integration as a "Trojan horse"

  • Peace after the devastating 20th-century wars was the biggest reason.
  • Continuum: free trade area (NAFTA/USMCA, EFTA); customs union (1951 ECSC with 6 members; 1957 Treaty of Rome creating the EEC/Common Market); common market (1993 European Union); economic and monetary union (euro 1999-2002).

Slide 6: "Deepening": ever stronger union

  • ECSC 1952 (6 states, 2 industries); customs union 1958 (Common Market); European Monetary System 1979; Schengen 1985 (26 states; Ireland and the UK stayed out); common market 1993 (EU, Treaty on European Union); single currency 1999-2002.

Slides 7-9: Widening: enlargements and candidates

  • 1957 Germany, France, Italy, Benelux; 1973 UK, Denmark, Ireland; 1981 Greece; 1986 Spain, Portugal; (1990 East Germany); 1995 Austria, Finland, Sweden; 2004 the A10 (Cyprus, Czechia, Estonia, Hungary, Latvia, Lithuania, Malta, Poland, Slovakia, Slovenia); 2007 Bulgaria, Romania; 2013 Croatia; 2021 first exit (UK), leaving 27. "Soon: 33 states?"
  • Balkans: hoped to bring in six by 2025; issues are corruption, bilateral disputes, refugee transit, Russian influence.
  • Candidates: Albania, Montenegro, North Macedonia, Serbia, Turkey, Ukraine, Moldova. Potential candidates: Bosnia and Herzegovina, Kosovo, Georgia.

Slide 10: Why a single currency?

  • Lower transaction costs (no exchange fees, no hedging); an EU estimate in 1990 put the savings at 0.1% of European GDP.
  • Less currency risk boosts trade and investment; comparable prices boost trade and help control inflation.
  • Historically weak-currency countries (Italy, Spain, Greece) gain inflation control and lower borrowing rates.
  • Psychological symbol of unity, a step toward political union.

Slide 11: The quest for exchange-rate stability

  • Bretton Woods to 1971; the 1972 "snake" (no more than 2.25% fluctuation); March 1979 EMS and the ECU, with bands of +/-2.25% (narrow) and +/-6% (wide).
  • The Deutsche Mark became the anchor; others had to follow German low-inflation policy, which bred dissatisfaction and helped drive the push to monetary union.
  • Early 1990s strain from reunified Germany; Black Wednesday 16 Sep 1992 (the UK, a member since 1990, left for good); 1993 attacks on the franc led to +/-15% bands. New objective: a single currency.

Slide 12: Maastricht criteria

  • Deficit no more than 3% of GDP; debt no more than 60% (or falling toward it in the final year); inflation no more than 1.5 points above the three lowest-inflation members; interbank rate no more than 2 points above those three; two years of stability in the EMS exchange-rate mechanism.

Slides 13-15: Eurozone of 21; support; Eichengreen

  • The eurozone now has 21 countries; Europeans are generally favorable to the EU and the euro.
  • Barry Eichengreen: within about five years of the deadliest war, enemies ceded control of coal and steel to a transnational body, and five years later gave up national trade policy; nothing analogous had happened before.

Slides 16-23: Comparing the US and the EU: size, income, structure

  • Topics: population, GDP, trade, middle class, demography, productivity, institutions, welfare state, inequality.
  • Charts: population and GDP (EU, euro area, USA, Japan, UK); GDP per capita at PPP (World Bank 2024); sectors as % of GDP (agriculture, manufacturing, services); trade as % GDP; unemployment (World Bank 2024); current-account balance (UK, USA, euro area).

Slides 24-27: Demography

  • UN projected population decline 2015-2050: Bulgaria -27.9%, Romania -22.1%, Latvia -19.1%, Lithuania -17.5%, Croatia -16.2%, Hungary -15.6%, Poland -14.2%, Germany -7.7%, Italy -5.5%, Spain -2.8%.
  • Europe's population will fall, leaving it only 7% of the world. All European fertility rates are below the 2.1 replacement rate.

Slides 28-36: Productivity, regulation, R&D, finance, GDP gap

  • EU productivity growth has fallen behind the US. Higher US productivity may stem from greater institutional and market efficiency (Ease of Doing Business score 2019: Singapore, USA, UK lead; then Germany, Russia, Japan, Spain, China, France, Mexico, India...).
  • Employment Protection Legislation (OECD): higher means more protected, more rigid; the US is the least protected.
  • Gross domestic spending on R&D lags in the EU (World Bank 2023); EU companies rely on more traditional (bank) finance.
  • Slower EU productivity and GDP growth has left EU GDP at only 68% of the US (nominal $, EU minus UK).

Slides 37-46: Trust, welfare state, inequality, middle class, measures of success

  • Trust in government is generally higher in Europe.
  • Welfare state definition: social security through compulsory contributions; usually public education, health and housing (sometimes free or cheap); antipoverty programs and progressive taxes; benefits usually universal, without means testing.
  • Sweden, one of the most comprehensive welfare states, has high female employment, gender equality, low Gini, innovation leadership and high R&D, large multinationals, high growth and incomes, low unemployment, low public debt, high birth rates and life expectancy: welfare costs do not appear to reduce competitiveness. Question: will record immigration since 2015 strain it?
  • Gini (World Bank) chart for UK, US, Germany, Denmark, Spain, France, Italy, Sweden; Eurostat Gini for the EU 30.7, eurozone 30.4. "The shrinking middle class?" charts.
  • What should be our measure of success? High incomes, growth, productivity, dynamism, big companies, opportunity, lower taxes... vs free time, life satisfaction, equality, security, long healthy lives, sustainability, a thriving middle class, a strong safety net, free public services.

Slides 47-55: Basic macro indicators (not covered in class)

  • GDP growth, output gap, headline inflation (European inflation was back at the 2% target before the US attack on Iran), policy rates (rose to combat inflation), unemployment (OECD).

Slides 56-66: Fiscal issues (not covered in class)

  • The US used fiscal policy more aggressively in the Covid crisis.
  • Government spending (and taxes) are much higher in Europe than in the US (OECD receipts and disbursements, social spending charts).
  • Ageing will worsen fiscal imbalances: the over-65 share rises from 20% to 30% by 2050 (European Commission); rating agencies warn of downgrades; a one-point rise in borrowing costs adds about 40-60 points to debt/GDP for Japan, Italy, the UK and the US by 2060 (S&P); half the largest economies could be junk by 2060.
  • Does the EU have a debt problem? Risk is similar to the United States: debt in own currency ("really their own?"), low but rising interest rates, large as % of GDP. Differences: falling in the eurozone as it recovers, unlike the US; an explicit deficit-control objective; lower eurozone inflation and growth make debt harder; high European taxes make it harder to repay by raising them; "sovereign risk" differs by country and can create a doom loop with banks; many external holders make some markets less stable.

Slides 67-70: Policy tools and optimal currency areas (not covered in class)

  • Fiscal policy is national but limited; monetary and exchange-rate policy belong to the ECB in the eurozone; structural policy is national with some limits.
  • Eurozone members give up monetary and exchange-rate policy; fiscal rules and market fear restrict fiscal policy; if internal demand fails, turn to external demand (as Japan did).
  • Optimal currency union theory: a currency union can only work with (1) fiscal transfers across countries to offset asymmetric shocks and (2) free flow of labor to relieve unemployment. A banking union would also need a common deposit-insurance scheme and integrated capital markets. Without them: protracted crises, bailouts, "internal devaluation", export-led recovery, and widening income gaps.

Slides 71-83: Fiscal transfers, NextGen EU, labor mobility (not covered in class)

  • EU net contributors and beneficiaries are small compared with US federal transfers between states ("what Germans might call a permanent bailout, we just call Missouri").
  • July 2020: a €750bn recovery package (4.7% of EU GDP), part loans and part grants, borrowed jointly by the EU; mostly to the hardest-hit south; the "Frugal Four" won a brake for slow reformers. A great step toward fiscal transfers.
  • Results: pre-pandemic growth regained in under two years; public investment 3% to 3.2% of GDP (2019-2022); but only about a third disbursed, and the GDP boost was 0.4% against an estimated 1.9% for 2022. Italy €191.5bn (11% of GDP), Spain €69.5bn (5.8% of GDP); Italy struggled to find projects by the June 2026 deadline.
  • Labor is more mobile in the US than in the EU.

Slides 84-88: Ukraine energy shock (not covered in class)

  • Recession more likely in Europe than the US (gas exposure, weaker recovery, war next door). The shock was milder than the 1970s for oil (oil 32% of energy vs 48% in 1973; prices up 56% at peak) but severe for gas (prices up 700%).

Slides 89-90: The UK's conflicted relationship with the EU

  • Present at the first talks but chose its own way; founded EFTA in 1960 (remaining members Iceland, Norway, Liechtenstein, which pay into the EU budget and accept free movement of workers for market access).
  • De Gaulle vetoed British entry twice (1963, 1967); the UK joined in 1973; Thatcher won a structural budget rebate in 1985; stayed out of Schengen and the euro (1999). Britain was a liberalizing, free-trade influence inside the EU.

Slides 91-98: The vote, Article 50, negotiations, the Irish border

  • June 2016: the UK voted narrowly to leave. Where the foreign-born population grew more than 200% in 2001-2014, a Leave vote was almost guaranteed.
  • Article 50 of the Lisbon Treaty: two years to leave after notification. Theresa May triggered it at the end of March 2017; talks from June 2017; May's deals were rejected by Parliament, bringing her down; Boris Johnson promised to fast-track exit; the slide says the UK left on 31 January 2021.
  • First issues: EU citizens in the UK, the UK's debt of up to €100bn, the Northern Irish border. A transition keeping the four freedoms to 31 Dec 2020; a temporary customs "backstop" to keep the Irish border open.
  • The Irish border (310 miles): neither side wants a hard border; a "frictionless" border is only possible inside a single market with the EU, so the UK cannot keep that pledge. Northern Ireland voted against Brexit and is split on leaving the UK if a border appeared.

Slide 99: Menu of options

  • 1. Cancel Article 50 and stay ("Regrexit").
  • Soft Brexit: 2. Join the EEA like Iceland, Norway, Liechtenstein (full single market except agriculture and fisheries; four freedoms including people with an "emergency brake"; follow EU law; pay into the budget). 3. The Turkish deal: customs union for non-agricultural goods (no customs procedures, no free movement, no payments; but no services and no independent trade deals because of the common external tariff).
  • Hard Brexit: 4. A "deep and comprehensive" FTA like Canada or Japan (tariff-free goods, not services; no free movement, payments or EU laws; customs controls and non-tariff barriers). 5. No deal: WTO terms (tariffs, no services trade, non-tariff barriers, most-favored-nation rules).

Slide 100: A parenthesis: the options for economic integration

  • Free trade area: remove tariffs among members, usually on goods only. Customs union: FTA plus a common external tariff. Common market: customs union plus free movement of capital and people (the EU's four freedoms: goods, services, capital, people). Economic union: common market plus harmonized economic policy, possibly a single currency.
  • Moving down the continuum, nations give up more sovereignty and gain more from trade, investment and migration.

Slides 101-103: Projected costs, and what Leave voters wanted

OptionTypeUK economy after 15 years vs staying
No deal (WTO)Hard9.1% smaller; sharp slowdown, higher inflation, migrant inflow down 80%, debt up at least 12% of GDP a year
FTA (Canada)Hard6.3% smaller (and impossible with a frictionless Irish border)
Customs union (Turkey)Soft2.2% smaller
Single market (Norway)SoftNo significant cost, but no control of migration or regulation
Stay (Regrexit)Least damaging
  • Slide 103 grid: no option gives new trade deals, an end to immigration, no budget payments, no EU rules, "take back control" and an open Irish border all at once. Canada: yes to the first four and control, no to the open border. Turkey: no new deals, yes to stopping immigration and payments, no on rules, mixed on control, no open border. Norway: no to everything except the open border. The Johnson government chose a Canada-style FTA (hard Brexit).

Slides 104-112: Effects of Brexit

  • Slower growth after the financial crisis and Brexit; had post-2010 trends held, real income and consumption per capita would be 8-9% and 11-12% higher. Brexit's negative impact reaches about 5-6% of GDP (about £2,300 per head) by 2035, mostly via lower terms of trade and lower productivity (each over 2.5 points).
  • Charts: GDP per capita, inflation and stock market; more bureaucracy for exporters to the EU, hitting small firms hardest; UK universities now recruit more in Asia than in the EU; most British would now like to rejoin, though support for Reform UK has risen.

Slides 113-117: Why did Brexit occur?

  • Johnson: after Brexit there will be no one to blame but ourselves. A quote on naive English nationalism defining "us" as "not them".
  • Two decades of development left part of the electorate behind; Leave was partly a backlash against unshared gains.
  • Kevin O'Rourke (Oxford): too much market and too little state invites a backlash; markets and states are complements, not substitutes.
  • Dani Rodrik (Harvard): democracy, national sovereignty and global economic integration are mutually incompatible; you can combine any two but never all three in full. Immigration acts as a "lightning rod" for this trilemma.
  • Many Leave voters were unhappy about public spending cuts. Where are populist movements coming from?

Slides 119-149: Spain in the eurozone (not covered in this class; see the Spain session)

  • Spain's transition timeline and 2002-2019 data table (e.g. current account from -9.7% of GDP in 2007 to surpluses from 2013; unemployment 26.1% in 2013; debt 29.4% in 2007 to over 100%).
  • Greece: no fiscal stimulus, no monetary or exchange-rate policy, only austerity; growth returned but per-capita income much lower and debt very high; debt came from bank bailouts and high borrowing costs. Bond prices and yields move inversely.
  • How a eurozone country recovers: structural reform plus exports via "internal devaluation" (lower wages, layoffs to raise productivity, aggressive marketing abroad), which further cuts consumption. Spain went from big spender to big saver. Dual labor market examples; "flexicurity" (flexible hiring and firing, active labor-market policies, comprehensive social protection) as the northern European solution. The cost: high unemployment, stagnant incomes, emigration of the young.

Slides 150-162: Climate, outlook, investor pros and cons (not covered in class)

  • The EU ranks high on environmental policy; the EU ETS (2005) is a cap-and-trade system covering over 11,000 installations and 40% of emissions; long oversupply and low prices, then prices up almost 70% in 2021; still short of Paris-compatible levels. Why the EU leads: no oil lobbies, citizen education, collective values, depoliticized issues, decisions by non-democratic bodies.
  • Outlook: Brexit galvanized reform rather than division; scars of internal devaluation are deep; Europe looks stable and serious next to Trump's US; "economically, Europe may be more like Japan than the United States".
  • Investor view: advantages are access to a large EU market (550m on the slide), a shared currency (no currency risk, no exchange cost, no hedging), an important global currency, low rates and easy borrowing. Drawbacks: fewer or constrained policy tools for each country, so problems may not be addressed quickly; countries retain much structural policy.

What the professor added in class

  • Framing: the course aims to make students familiar with the biggest economies and to think about risk; Europe is a fairly safe area, but Brexit shows specific risks. Economic integration is a big part of Europe's attraction for investors.
  • EXAM: Why Europe unified: war. A chart of 20th-century battle deaths; less than a generation between the World Wars. Economic integration was a Trojan horse: a technical, unthreatening project with political integration and peace hidden in its belly, because French and Germans could not simply declare friendship. It worked: no wars among EU members; trading, investing and working together made enmity hard.
  • EXAM: Levels of integration, each including the previous one and adding something: (1) free trade area (NAFTA/USMCA before Trump's tariffs, which broke its rules and the WTO's; EFTA, founded by Britain); (2) customs union = FTA + common external tariff, so members must agree a common trade policy toward India, the US, China (Turkey has a customs union with the EU; Europe started here with coal and steel in 1951, the commanding heights of industry, 6 members); (3) common market = customs union + free movement of people and capital (live and work anywhere without permits; immigrants who get status in Spain can move anywhere; imagine the US doing this with Mexico); (4) economic (and monetary) union = common market + possibly a single currency + coordinated fiscal and monetary policy, handing policy to a supranational level. The deeper you go, the more sovereignty you give up, which the UK did not want.
  • Quirk: Europe always names itself after its next step (called itself the Common Market while a customs union, the European Union while a common market).
  • Why integrate besides peace: bargaining power (the EU speaks with one voice, so small Belgium carries weight); efficiency and growth from bigger markets, no tariffs, removal of non-tariff barriers (e.g. rules on tractor steering-wheel width), and a single currency.
  • Enlargement story: the original six (France, Italy, Germany plus Benelux; two war losers and France seeing itself as victor, so France dominated without the UK). 1973 UK, Denmark, Ireland (the UK saw Europe doing well). The EU uses membership to consolidate democracy: Greece 1981 after the military dictatorship; Spain and Portugal 1986 after almost 40 years of right-wing dictatorship (slow because Spain is big). 1990 East Germany via reunification. 1995 Austria, Finland, Sweden; Norway (oil, generous welfare state, fisheries, did not want interference) and Switzerland (financial secrecy, rules to harmonize) voted no twice; both are in Schengen but not the EU. 2004: ten former Soviet-bloc countries brought in fast to protect their transition; millions of Poles and Hungarians moved to the UK, one reason for Brexit. 2007 Bulgaria and Romania (Bulgaria only adopted the euro in 2026, almost 20 years later). 2013 Croatia; 2021 the UK left (28 to 27).
  • Accession requires democratic reforms: independent courts, no death penalty, minority rights. Turkey has been in talks about 50 years and it has become "a joke"; obstacles discussed were population size (bigger than Germany), being Muslim-majority, and Cyprus/Greece. Allard thinks Turkey should have been brought in; a Turkish student agreed (and noted Turkey holds millions of Syrian refugees). Ukraine is a candidate but has 40 million people and much to reform. Canada and Japan have free trade agreements with the EU.
  • EXAM: EU vs eurozone: two circles: 27 EU members, and an inner circle of 21 sharing the euro and monetary policy. Non-euro members include Poland, Romania, Hungary, Sweden (never wants to join) and Denmark (apparently would like to); the UK kept the pound.
  • Support (Pew 2025): most Europeans favorable; Greece not, France "doesn't like anything"; 74% in the EU and 83% in the eurozone support the euro; support dipped during the 2011-2014 eurozone crisis.
  • Eichengreen (UC Berkeley economic historian): going from warring states to this unity is unprecedented. Economics has a special power to overcome political obstacles.
  • EXAM: EU vs US numbers: EU population 450 million (third largest in the world), eurozone 351 million (bigger than the US), but GDP much smaller than the US. GDP per capita (2024, PPP chart): US almost $86,000, eurozone about $66,000, EU about $63,000, UK a bit behind (after almost 10 years of Brexit).
  • Her view: Europe chose a different lifestyle (more free time, less work); she does not take "competitiveness" as the metric; countries should choose how their people want to live. Europe is behind in science and technology.
  • Health spending: about 17% of GDP in the US vs about 8% in Europe, a policy difference (profits to pharma and the health industry). Home ownership: US about two-thirds, Europe about 71-72%.
  • Structure: manufacturing is not very big anywhere; the UK is about 80% services; the EU has more manufacturing than the US because of Italy and Germany. She dislikes trade/GDP for the EU because intra-EU trade (Italy to Spain) counts, unlike California to Nevada.
  • EXAM: Unemployment is structurally higher in the EU: welfare states make it easier not to work; the US and UK administer welfare strictly; ageing may play a part.
  • EXAM: Current account: the UK and US are deficit countries; the euro area is a surplus region, mainly because of Germany, but it would still be in surplus without Germany (Spain and Italy run surpluses). The surplus grew after the eurozone crisis as consumption fell and countries "had to export to live", putting Europe beside China and Japan.
  • Demography: Europe's population is shrinking like China's and Japan's; many losers (e.g. Poland) lost people to emigration, often to the UK. Working-age population to 2060 falls about 40% in Poland and 30% in Germany; Spain may beat projections because immigration is far higher than modeled. Europe will be only 7% of world population; Africa rises to 25%. Fertility: France (decades of family support), Ireland and, surprisingly, Sweden close to 2; then the UK and Denmark; Italy, Spain, Greece and Cyprus lowest.
  • EXAM: Potential growth = labor-force growth + productivity growth. Both are weak in Europe. Output per hour (inflation-adjusted index) rose in the US over two decades and fell in the eurozone; there is a big debate about measurement (PPP etc.), but Europe does not look brilliant; no major tech innovations come from Europe. Trend GDP per person is falling everywhere and is especially low in the eurozone; Japan is lower still. Bottom line: Europe cannot grow as much, a consequence of its choices (few children, heavy regulation, a comfortable welfare state), but not the end of Europe.
  • Why is US productivity higher? Nobody knows for sure; maybe regulation. Ease of Doing Business (a World Bank index dropped because China cheated): Singapore, US, UK easiest; Germany and Spain not far behind; China and India more bureaucratic. Regulation was one reason the UK was uncomfortable in the EU. Hiring and firing is easy in the US, harder in Europe, harder still in China and India (hence informality).
  • R&D: the EU lags, but Sweden (ahead of the US), Belgium, Austria, Switzerland and Germany are high; Korea leads; Japan similar to the US. EU GDP is about 68% of the US; a student asked whether healthcare explains it: healthcare is only about 9 points of the 32-point gap; tech is a big part.
  • Trust: Europeans trust their governments more; the Nordics most; the US has become distrustful; Spain distrusts but less than the US.
  • EXAM: Welfare state: many countries (including the US and India) have welfare elements such as pensions; pensions were invented by the Germans in the 19th century to end old-age poverty. The biggest difference is that European benefits are universal: everybody, rich or poor, gets them; in the US mainly the poor and the old get free healthcare (i.e. targeted programs). She has come round to the European view: universality creates consensus, so each administration does not try to take benefits away (unlike the US back-and-forth over Obamacare).
  • Sweden: cradle-to-grave state, spending a little over 50% of GDP now (used to be 60%); public debt about 30% of GDP; innovative for its size, many multinationals, high R&D, high birth rates and life expectancy; welfare does not seem to hurt competitiveness, perhaps because it is small and well run. Immigrant countries (e.g. Latin America) find less consensus for a welfare state; corruption perceptions make people reluctant to pay into funds (a Kenyan student's housing-levy example).
  • EXAM: Inequality: the US is the most unequal developed country. Gini: Sweden 0.27 (she thinks the lowest in the world), Denmark 0.29, Germany 0.30 (impressive for a big country). Before taxes and transfers the US is more equal than Italy or Germany; Europe ends up far more equal because it taxes and transfers much more. India's Gini is the same before and after taxes and transfers.
  • Middle class (Pew definition: disposable income from two-thirds to twice the national median): Europe 69-80% (Spain and Italy lower); the US has fallen to about 59%. Why it matters: it drives the market, underpins stability, and means a better-prepared labor force.
  • Measures of success: per-capita income and GDP size are not everything; cultures choose differently.
  • Brexit history: Britain was invited to the first talks and declined; founded EFTA (now only Iceland, Norway, Liechtenstein remain). Applied in 1963 and 1967; de Gaulle (anti-Anglo) vetoed both, the second time threatening France would leave if the other five let Britain in. Joined in 1973 after de Gaulle. Paid in far more than it received (little farming); Thatcher demanded "my money back" and won a structural rebate until exit. Stayed out of Schengen (only the UK and Ireland stayed out) and the euro (the pound is a strong reserve currency). A good free-trade influence against subsidies.
  • EXAM: The 2016 vote: 51.9% leave vs 48.1% remain, very close for such a big decision; she thinks Parliament should have decided, or a two-thirds threshold used. Scotland, Northern Ireland and London voted remain; London has the most immigrants, so it was not simply anti-immigrant; mainly rural, less developed areas voted leave. More educated and younger voters chose remain; older voters chose leave. Leave was near certain where the foreign-born population grew more than 200% (growth, not level; similar pattern in eastern Germany). David Cameron called the referendum to settle splits in both parties.
  • Article 50: send a letter, then two years to leave. May triggered it in March 2017, talks from June 2017; she hoped Parliament would treat the referendum as non-binding. The EU offered Turkey-, Norway- and Canada-style options; May's deal was rejected and her government fell; Johnson promised to get it done even without a deal; the UK left "in 2021".
  • Options in class: stay ("Re-Brexit"); Norway/EEA (almost a common market, free movement of people, no economic cost; offered to May and refused because "Brexit is Brexit"); Turkey (customs union for non-agricultural goods, about 2.2% cost); Canada FTA (about 6.3%); no deal (about 9.1%). The cost figures mean the economy is that much smaller than it would have been, i.e. slower growth, not a fall. Uncertainty itself hits investment.
  • Leave voters wanted something they could not have: each option fails at least one goal; the Irish border is the impossible issue.
  • Consequences: slower growth (worse over time), higher inflation (less trade), bureaucracy that hurts small exporters most, universities recruiting in Asia, the EU financial center for EU business partly moved to Frankfurt and Paris (London stayed strong), immigration from the EU fell but from elsewhere rose. Polls: 22% + 43% favor a closer relationship and 36% + 20% (more than half) favor rejoining; yet support for the party that pushed Brexit is also high. Scotland has said it would like to leave the UK and join the EU.
  • EXAM: Populism: people left behind as the middle class shrank; Brexit and Trump as backlash. O'Rourke: too much market and too little state invites backlash; states catch the people markets leave behind.
  • EXAM: Rodrik trilemma: democracy (democratic politics), national sovereignty and global economic integration (hyperglobalization): pick any two, never all three. Joining the EU means handing sovereignty to the union, so democratic politics shrinks (people resent EU rules such as labelling and food inspections). Immigration becomes the symbol of lost sovereignty for people who do not know much about it.
  • She did not reach economic policymaking, the eurozone crisis or climate. Next: Session 12 (Garland), then Session 13 Africa with two short optional exercises (a policy simulator to raise incomes, or an investor risk analysis).

Likely exam angles

  • Short answer: "Compare the EU and the US." US: higher GDP and per-capita income (about $86k vs $63-66k PPP), higher productivity growth, more R&D, lighter regulation, lower unemployment, smaller government and more targeted (means-tested) welfare, higher inequality (shrinking middle class, about 59%), current-account deficit. EU: larger population (450m), GDP about 68% of the US, higher spending and taxes, universal welfare states, more equality and trust, a bigger middle class (69-80%), surplus, weaker demography; public debt risk similar to the US.
  • Sample MCQ: correct EU vs US comparison: average public debt (risk) roughly the same; NOT similar spending, NOT similar per-capita income, NOT "the US has no welfare programs".
  • MCQ: order of integration: free trade area, customs union, common market, economic (and monetary) union.
  • MCQ: what a customs union adds to a free trade area (a common external tariff); what a common market adds (free movement of people and capital).
  • MCQ: optimal currency area conditions: fiscal transfers and labor mobility (plus deposit insurance and integrated capital markets for a banking union).
  • MCQ: Rodrik trilemma: democracy, national sovereignty, global economic integration (not Mundell's fixed exchange rate, free capital flows, independent monetary policy).
  • MCQ: which Brexit option is soft (Norway/EEA, Turkey customs union) vs hard (Canada FTA, no deal); which one the UK chose (Canada-style FTA).
  • MCQ: why the euro area runs a surplus (Germany plus post-crisis compression of demand in the south).
  • MCQ: why EU unemployment is higher (generous, less strictly administered welfare states; rigid labor markets).
  • MCQ: what characterizes a European welfare state (universal benefits without means testing).

Traps and look-alikes

  • Rodrik's trilemma (democracy, sovereignty, global integration) vs Mundell's impossible trinity (fixed exchange rate, free capital mobility, independent monetary policy). Both are "pick two of three".
  • The US does have welfare programs (Medicare, Medicaid, EITC, pensions); they are targeted, while Europe's are universal. "The US has no welfare" is wrong.
  • EU population is larger than the US (450m; eurozone 351m) but GDP and per-capita income are lower. The investor slide says "large EU market (550m)", which conflicts with the 450m in class.
  • EU vs eurozone: 27 vs 21 members. The UK, Sweden, Denmark, Poland, Hungary, Romania are/were EU but not euro.
  • Norway and Switzerland are in Schengen but not the EU; the UK and Ireland were in the EU but not Schengen.
  • Customs union = Turkey model; single market/EEA = Norway model; FTA = Canada model. Turkey cannot sign its own trade deals (common external tariff).
  • Brexit costs are "smaller than if it had stayed" after 15 years (slower growth), not a one-off fall: 9.1% no deal, 6.3% Canada, 2.2% Turkey, about 0 Norway.
  • Dates: ECSC treaty 1951 (slides 5 and class) vs "1952" on slide 6 (entry into force). Treaty of Rome 1957, customs union 1958. The deck and class say the UK left in 2021; the formal exit was 31 Jan 2020, with the transition ending 31 Dec 2020 (slide 96 itself gives the 31 Dec 2020 transition end).
  • Euro area surplus is not only Germany: it persists without Germany.
  • Before taxes and transfers the US is more equal than Italy or Germany; Europe's equality comes from redistribution.
  • Sweden: big welfare state and low public debt (about 30%) and high R&D; welfare is not shown to hurt competitiveness there.
  • Welfare state spending in Sweden: class said a little over 50% now (formerly 60%); the summary rounded to 50-60%.
Session 12 · 15 Oct 2026 · Garland (Allard absent) · complete notes

Session 12: Mexico, reform and crisis 1987-95, and the risks of capital flows (Prof. Gonzalo Garland)

Garland's last session. He opened with the final-exam format and rules (below, marked EXAM). He then took the class through the HBS case "Mexico (C): Reform and Crisis, 1987-1995" (Huw Pill, case 797-050). The story runs: debt crisis and lost decade, the heterodox Pacto, Washington Consensus reforms and NAFTA, the Brady-bond restructuring, a flood of portfolio capital, an overvalued peso inside a band, the 1994 political shocks plus Fed rate hikes, tesobonos and the reserve drain, the December 1994 devaluation and float, overshooting, the $50bn rescue, the Tequila effect, and the wider lesson about "hot money" and sudden stops (Chile's deposit, capital controls in India and China, dollarization). Garland said the case is old but is still the best illustration of a crisis pattern that keeps repeating. For the exam it ties together Mundell (capital inflows financing a trade deficit), fixed/band exchange rates and overvaluation, debt restructuring, and FDI vs portfolio flows.

Exam format and logistics (EXAM)

  • EXAM: Final exam is Wednesday 11 November, in the classroom, on your own computer, closed book. You may have blank paper and a pen or pencil only. No calculations needed.
  • EXAM: 10 multiple-choice questions x 6 points = 60 points, plus 4 open questions x 10 points = 40 points. Total 100. Both professors contribute questions to both parts.
  • EXAM: Nothing asks you to memorize numbers or names from the cases. It tests concepts.
  • EXAM: Open questions are short paragraphs, not essays. Graders look for specific elements. Garland's example: "briefly describe Malthusian theory". Full credit for one paragraph covering population growing geometrically, food growing arithmetically, and the checks (famine, wars, disease) that bring population back down. His second example: "what is the basic idea of dirty (managed) floating?"
  • EXAM: Questions can come from class discussion and notes, not only slides. He gave dirty floating as an example: it was discussed in class but is not really on the slides.
  • The MCQs are graded automatically. Both profs grade the open answers. The full grade breakdown comes after the exam. The exam software (Respondus) has failed for some groups before; Garland said he would check with IT. Allard would not be present on exam day; Garland would supervise. Questions before the exam go by email (copy both profs) or a meeting.

The case, section by section

Opening puzzle

  • After 1987 Mexico stabilized and reformed: trade liberalized, finance deregulated, FDI rules relaxed, state firms privatized. It went from the "basket case" of the debt crisis to the "darling" of the IMF, the World Bank and emerging-market fund managers. Capital flooded in from 1990, and NAFTA started in January 1994.
  • By end-1995: the December 1994 devaluation ended in chaos, capital fled, output fell more than 6% in 1995, inflation peaked at 52%, the banks nearly collapsed, and the US led an international bailout.
  • Banco de México's question frames the case: how could extensive, well-executed fiscal, supply-side and trade reforms end so badly?

Mexico in 1987: orthodox austerity fails, heterodox Pacto

  • After 1982 Mexico ran an orthodox stabilization: tight public finances, on the view that fiscal deficits drive inflation. The economy stagnated (the 1980s "lost decade") and income went abroad to service the external debt.
  • In 1987 the stock market crashed, capital fled, and another deep devaluation pushed inflation higher. The peso was then pegged at the new rate, but capital flight continued.
  • The de la Madrid government (from 1982) switched to a heterodox program. The Pacto was an agreement among government, business, unions and agriculture to coordinate wage bargaining and price setting.
  • Heterodox logic: fiscal deficits are as much a result of inflation as a cause. Inflation has its own momentum through expectations. Unions demand higher wages, costs and prices rise, and the expectation is confirmed (wage-price spiral). Coordination breaks the spiral.
  • Under the Pacto the exchange rate was put on a slow downward crawl. Public finances moved into surplus (privatization proceeds, better tax collection). Inflation fell slowly as expectations came down. Exhibit 5a: CPI inflation 105.7% (1986), 159.2% (1987), 51.7% (1988), 19.7% (1989), 29.9% (1990), 18.8% (1991), 11.9% (1992), 8.0% (1993), 7.0% (1994).

Structural reform (Washington Consensus)

  • De la Madrid promised to open Mexico to market forces, the "Washington consensus" of the IMF and World Bank. Salinas (from December 1988) pushed harder.
  • GATT entry 1986, tariffs slashed, free-trade deals with Central America, then NAFTA with the US and Canada (1994).
  • Exhibit 2: deregulation of more than 3,000 sectors (FDI law of 1991, trucking, finance). About 1,000 public firms privatized for $25bn, used to cut government debt. Ejido land reform. Tax and pension reform. 4,000 miles of private toll roads. Banco de México made independent. In 1994 Mexico became the first new OECD member in 25 years.

Financial liberalization

  • In 1982 President López Portillo had nationalized the banks to stop capital flight. During the 1980s banks were an arm of government: credit was directed by politicians, not market criteria.
  • From 1988 interest-rate controls were removed. Peso convertibility on current and capital account was gradually freed. In 1989 forced lending to government and favored sectors ended, deposit-rate ceilings went, and reserve requirements were cut. Between June 1991 and July 1992, 18 banks were privatized by auction to the highest bidder. Ownership was capped at 5% for individuals and 10% for institutions, and foreign entry was restricted.

Debt restructuring: Baker Plan and Brady bonds

  • The debt overhang from the late-1970s fiscal expansion was the "only brake" on revival. The 1985 Baker Plan tried restructuring alone and was not enough.
  • The initiative of US Treasury Secretary Nicholas Brady (case dates the initiative to 1988; agreement with creditors signed February 1990) brought some debt forgiveness and new lending. Its main feature was converting bank loans into Brady bonds, guaranteed by the US Treasury on the collateral of Mexican oil reserves.
  • Effect: a new time profile of debt service. Payments were much lower in the early years, freeing resources for the domestic economy in the hope it would later be strong enough to repay in full.

Mexico rediscovered: the capital inflow boom

  • Confidence surged. Consumption rose in anticipation of higher future incomes, and investment rose. Liberalization also opened access to two things denied for decades: bank credit and foreign goods. The result was fast credit growth (much of it funded abroad) and a vast trade and current account deficit.
  • The world capital market had changed since the 1970s. Banks were no longer the main lenders; portfolio flows from emerging-market funds (mutual, pension, insurance funds) were. These securities were marketable. They lacked the lender-borrower relationship of bank loans and were not "locked in" like FDI.
  • US rates fell in the early-1990s recession, so investors hunted yield in emerging markets. Mexico ("so far from God, so close to the United States") got a disproportionate share. Fund managers were quoted as unambiguously optimistic.
  • A virtuous circle: reform, then confidence, then inflows, then more lending and spending, then more tax revenue and a better fiscal position, then more confidence.
  • Skeptics: the fiscal surpluses relied on creative accounting (spending moved off balance sheet to development banks). Low inflation was partly administrative: Pacto parties set many prices, and the government alone set prices (gasoline, tortillas) covering more than 10% of the index. Krugman later called the emerging-market enthusiasm a classic speculative bubble.
  • Inflows were larger than needed to fund lending and the deficit. So Banco de México built reserves and the exchange rate appreciated. Since 1991 the peso had traded in a band whose lower limit (the depreciation side) moved a little each day. Inflows kept the peso at the strong top of the band. It was remarkably stable from late 1992, which the authorities read as proof of success.

Overvaluation?

  • From early 1993 critics argued the strong peso showed mismanagement. The Pacto cut inflation, but not to US levels. A stable nominal rate with higher Mexican inflation means real appreciation (Exhibit 9, CPI-based real rate vs the US: 100 in 1990, 75.0 in 1993; a fall means appreciation). Mexico was pricing itself out of world markets, and the current account deficit showed falling competitiveness.
  • Rudiger Dornbusch (MIT) led the critics. He argued Mexico should devalue and accept the inflation cost, because otherwise only recession or unsustainable fiscal expansion could fix the external problem.
  • The authorities' reply: the current account deficit was just the accounting counterpart of capital inflows, and a sign of confidence. Exports were growing (especially in 1994). Imports were capital goods to re-equip industry. Banco de México's labor-cost-based real exchange rate showed less appreciation, and some appreciation could be an equilibrium result of rising productivity.

Social polarization, financial fragility, falling savings

  • Two Mexicos: a modern, US-linked elite that got credit and imports, and an agrarian south (many peasants did not speak Spanish) that gained little. The authorities believed in trickle-down.
  • Banks: after years of nationalization, credit-assessment skills had withered. The new owners came from stockbroking, with a "gambling" approach to lending. There was no credit bureau. Deposit insurance covered all deposits at a flat, risk-unrelated fee, which subsidized risk-taking. Supervision was understaffed and laissez-faire. Bad loans mounted from 1992 (hidden by weak accounting standards). Owners with little equity left had an incentive to "gamble for resurrection". Connected lending and fraud added to it.
  • Private saving collapsed as consumption outran income. Private gross domestic saving fell to 12% of GDP by 1994. Accounting identity: current account deficit = domestic investment minus domestic saving. Low saving made growth dependent on continued foreign inflows, so Mexico was vulnerable to a reversal of sentiment.

End-1993 and the political shocks of 1994

  • OECD entry and NAFTA seemed to lock in the reforms. Inflows peaked in late November 1993 when the US Congress passed NAFTA.
  • February 1994: the Fed began raising rates as the US recovered. Mexico's yield advantage shrank.
  • 1 January 1994: NAFTA took effect, and the same day the Chiapas peasant uprising began (rebels seized six towns). It reminded investors that Mexico lacked the social and political infrastructure of a developed country. Mexican crises followed the six-year electoral rhythm (1976, 1982, 1987).
  • 23 March 1994: PRI candidate Luis Donaldo Colosio (Salinas's chosen successor and virtual president-elect) was assassinated. Inflows dried up and reversed, savings moved to dollar accounts offshore, reserves started falling, and the peso dropped from the top to the bottom of its band.
  • Exhibit 1 Fed moves in 1994: +25bp (4 Feb), +25 (22 Mar), +25 (18 Apr), +50 (17 May), +50 (16 Aug), +75 (15 Nov), from 3% where the rate had stood since September 1992.

Policy response: why not raise rates or impose controls

  • The exchange-rate band was the centerpiece of anti-inflation policy and the anchor of the Pacto, so it "had to be defended at all costs".
  • Higher interest rates were rejected because the banking system was fragile and loaded with non-performing loans. Higher rates could push borrowers into default and banks over the edge, and the payments system had to be protected.
  • Exchange controls were rejected: they would signal abandoning liberalism and could trigger the crisis they aimed to prevent. The long, porous US border and sophisticated markets would also make them hard to enforce.
  • So reserves were used as a buffer against outflows, and the government borrowed dollars to keep gross reserves up.
  • Tesobonos: short-term Mexican government debt indexed to the dollar. Through summer and autumn 1994 the government shifted borrowing from cetes (peso T-bills) and longer peso debt into tesobonos. The government thereby took on the exchange-rate risk that foreign investors had carried before. Tesobonos had to be rolled over every three months, which created liquidity-crisis risk: if they could not be rolled, there would not be enough dollars for creditors and the current account deficit.

New administration and crisis

  • Zedillo won a relatively clean election on 21 August 1994. Confidence still did not recover. On 28 September PRI Secretary General Ruiz Massieu was assassinated, and on 23 November the Deputy Attorney General (his brother) resigned alleging a cover-up.
  • Zedillo took office on 1 December. His plan did not show how the current account and bank problems would be fixed. The finance minister ran a "charm offensive" in New York, but reserves kept draining.
  • 20 December: the band was widened, an effective devaluation of about 15%. Fund managers felt betrayed and pulled out; hedge funds bet on further falls. The virtuous circle became a faster vicious spiral.
  • 22 December: Banco de México withdrew from the market and the peso floated, and it "dropped like a stone". Exhibit 5b: 3.43 pesos/$ on 2 December 1994, 4.94 on 30 December, about 6.97 on 17 March 1995. Cetes rates rose from about 15% to above 80% in March 1995.
  • Liquidity squeeze: loanable funds dried up, rates soared, borrowers with floating-rate debt could not pay, and the hidden banking crisis became real. Output fell and inflation surged.
  • January 1995: Clinton offered a US loan, but it stalled in Congress. On 31 January a $50bn package was announced: $20bn US, $18bn IMF (including a $7.8bn IMF loan with conditionality), $10bn BIS, and $3bn private banks. The depreciation was arrested only in March. Banpaís was taken over on 3 March.
  • Exhibit 7 balance of payments: trade balance +405m (1989), -7.3bn (1991), -15.9bn (1992), -13.5bn (1993), -18.5bn (1994), +7.1bn (1995). Current account -29.4bn in 1994. Portfolio liabilities 3.4bn (1990), 12.7bn (1991), 18.0bn (1992), 28.9bn (1993), then -10.1bn in 1995. Financial account 8.4bn (1990), 25.1bn, 27.0bn, 33.8bn (1993), 15.8bn (1994), -11.8bn (1995).

What the professor taught in class, in order

The 1982 debt crisis and the lost decade

  • Garland linked it to the Brazil case. In the 1970s, petrodollars from the oil shocks flooded banks, which pushed loans onto emerging markets (Mexico, Brazil, also Korea) building infrastructure. Most of the debt was at variable interest rates.
  • Oil shocks caused stagflation in the rich world (US inflation near 20%). The UK and then the US fought it with very restrictive monetary policy. Rates went "through the roof" in 1982, the world fell into recession, and the debtors could not pay.
  • Mexico defaulted first, and the rest of Latin America followed (a domino effect).
  • To stop capital flight, the government nationalized the banks. That made Mexico an outcast from the world financial system. The IMF and World Bank tell a defaulter to negotiate with its banks first; citizens will not buy bonds from a government that does not pay. The only way left to finance deficits was printing money, hence inflation of 105% (1986) and 159% (1987). That was high (some define hyperinflation as above 100%) but nothing like Brazil's 2,000%+.
  • "Lost decade": per capita incomes in most Latin American countries were 25-40% lower in the 1990s than in the 1950s (as said in class). The label was later applied to Japan.
  • Nobody wanted to invest in a country that did not pay its debt and had 100-150% inflation, except perhaps producers of non-tradables for the local market (cement).

Mexican politics

  • After the revolution, one party, the PRI, ruled for about 80 years, until 2000. Presidents served one six-year term and picked their successor, who was certain to win. De la Madrid (1982-88) chose Salinas (1988-94). Power changed hands in December. Garland said names are not exam material.

The Pacto (heterodox policy)

  • With 100% inflation, wages rise 100%, costs rise 100%, and the spiral continues. The Pacto had business, unions, farmers and government agree on smaller increases (his example: 60% instead of 100%) to lower expectations. Garland said it helped somewhat but did not really work well, which is why the whole decade is called lost.

Washington Consensus and Brady bonds

  • Context: the Berlin Wall fell in 1989 and the Cold War ended. Fukuyama's "end of history": no more debate about the model. The Washington Consensus (IMF, World Bank) meant free trade, openness to investment and capital movements, privatization and infrastructure. India's 1991 reform belongs to the same moment (the Soviet collapse removed its trade partner and caused a balance-of-payments crisis).
  • Salinas could not rejoin the world financial system without fixing the debt. The US was the most interested party: Mexico is its neighbor, and a collapse means migration pressure.
  • Baker and Brady were US Treasury Secretaries. The Baker Plan (1985) did not work; the Brady plan did (Garland dated the Brady bonds to 1990).
  • EXAM: Three elements of any debt restructuring: a haircut (cut the principal a little), a longer maturity, and a lower interest rate. What was special about Brady bonds: the bonds were backed by the US Treasury and collateralized by Mexican oil. If Mexico stopped paying, the guarantee covered creditors with the oil. Garland called this crucial.

Capital inflows, the band and overvaluation

  • After opening (1991-92), a consumption boom: Mexicans could finally buy US refrigerators, cars, clothes.
  • FDI rose ("red carpet"). But short-term portfolio flows "went crazy". Exhibit 10: Mexican stocks boomed while US stocks rose slowly. Short-term rates were about 30% in Mexico vs 6-7% in the US. A 1993 fund manager who had not invested in Mexico "would have been fired".
  • Salinas was a world hero. It was expected he would become the first head of the WTO when GATT became the WTO.
  • FX diagram: before liberalization supply (exporters) and demand (importers) set the rate at, say, 5 pesos/$. Capital inflows shift dollar supply out, so the dollar gets cheap and the peso appreciates (say toward 3). Imports get cheaper and exports dearer. Within the band, the central bank bought the excess dollars, building reserves.
  • EXAM: Why Mexico was happy with an overvalued peso: (1) it had just restructured its dollar debt, and a cheap dollar means fewer pesos to repay each dollar; (2) cheap imports helped fight inflation. Coming from 150% inflation and default, these were its two priorities.
  • EXAM: This is Mundell in action: a trade deficit financed by capital inflows. Classical theory says a trade deficit should depreciate the currency. It did not, because everyone wanted pesos to buy Mexican bonds and stocks.
  • FDI vs portfolio ("hot money"): FDI is more stable because you cannot take a factory out. Portfolio money comes in and goes out easily, as it did in 1995.

1994: why it went wrong

  • Many Mexicans blame political instability: Chiapas on NAFTA day, Colosio's assassination, the Ruiz Massieu murder and the Deputy Attorney General's resignation (Garland said it seemed the president's brother was somehow involved), more violence in Chiapas.
  • The second reading of the chronology: the Fed kept raising rates. Garland said the US was not targeting Mexico; a large economy sets monetary policy for its own inflation, and flows to emerging markets are secondary to it. In fact after the crisis the US was desperate to rescue Mexico.
  • Diagram: inflows dry up and reverse, so the equilibrium rate moves back toward 5. To defend the band, Mexico tried something desperate.
  • Tesobonos (class version): very short-term debt paying high interest in dollars, issued to attract dollars. It did not work: the US-Mexico differential had narrowed and the instability scared investors. EXAM: Tesobonos vs Brady bonds: Brady bonds were long-term restructured debt guaranteed by the US Treasury; tesobonos were short-term, dollar-linked, and had no US guarantee.
  • Possible motive for not devaluing: Salinas was leaving in December and may have wanted to leave the decision to his successor, or to leave office in glory.
  • Reserves (Exhibit 14): about $27bn in January rising to nearly $30bn, then collapsing to about $5bn by December. Garland added (not in the case) that Banco de México stopped publishing reserve data mid-year to avoid panic.
  • The "December Mistake" (not in the case, "legend says"): the outgoing team told the incoming one that reserves were about $5bn, they were losing about $3bn a month, and about $15bn was due over the next three months. The mistake was that this information was shared with the 15-20 richest Mexicans and bankers, who pulled their money out immediately. Panic spread and everyone rushed to buy dollars at 3 while it lasted.
  • 20 December: the band was widened 15% (Garland thought it had been 5% before; UNSURE). 22 December: float.
  • EXAM: Overshooting: the peso should have gone from about 3 to about 5, but panic pushed it to about 7. The market overreacts, then the rate drifts back toward 5.
  • Contrast in crisis communication: some argue Argentina handled such moments better by announcing at night that banks would close for three days and then setting out all measures, so there was no time to speculate. India's demonetization (Modi on TV at 8pm, the largest notes invalid from midnight) followed the same logic. Rich countries can announce openly because their citizens do not panic.
  • Aftermath: people with dollar credit-card debt burned their cards in the street. The $50bn rescue ($20bn US + $18bn IMF + $10bn BIS, as listed in class). The trade balance swung from about -$18bn (1994) to a +$7bn surplus (1995) as the cheap peso boosted exports and cut imports, roughly offsetting capital outflows of about $11bn plus reserve changes.

Contagion, sudden stops and the policy debate

  • An economist called Mexico "the first financial crisis of the 21st century".
  • Tequila effect: investors pulled money from all of Latin America (Brazil, Argentina, Peru). It was mostly regional.
  • About 2.5 years later, the 1997 Asian crisis (Korea, Thailand, Indonesia, the best performers of the previous 30 years) was worse and global. Then Russia 1998, Brazil 1999, and later Turkey and Argentina: the same pattern.
  • The name: financial crises caused by capital reversals (sudden stops). Capital flows in fast for a while, then out desperately. Rich countries rarely see this, though Soros's bet against the pound in the EMS was similar.
  • Old consensus: capital freedom is not the problem; if capital panics, something is wrong in your country (no one panics out of Switzerland). Newer IMF and World Bank papers question that: short-term "hot money" portfolio flows can themselves create instability and crises, even with weak fundamental causes, as in Asia.
  • EXAM: This is, in Garland's view, the main reason India and China keep capital controls and do not fully liberalize the capital account: they fear sudden panics and sharp depreciations. In India, moving large sums in or out needs authorizations.
  • EXAM: Chile's model: FDI gets the red carpet (including tax terms guaranteed for 10 years). Short-term portfolio capital (under one year) is welcome but must leave a 20% deposit at the central bank. If you stay one year you get it back; if you leave earlier you lose it. That deters speculative short-term money that drives appreciation (Chile wanted to protect other exports). (The transcript sometimes says "China" here, which is a garble for Chile.)
  • A US capital reversal would take something catastrophic. World demand for dollars is changing only slowly (slight rises in other currencies in transactions and reserves).

Dollarization

  • Fully dollarized in Latin America: Ecuador, El Salvador, Panama. Many others are partly dollarized by custom (prices or houses quoted in dollars, as in Peru), not by law.
  • EXAM: Trade-off: dollarizing removes exchange-rate risk and depreciation crises. But you give up monetary policy and become subject to US monetary policy. A Mexican finance minister compared it to amputating an arm. Small countries may do it; large ones (Mexico, Brazil) will not. Argentina is the possible exception among larger economies because of its inefficient central bank and chronic money printing.
  • Remaining challenge in a dollarized economy: inflation must stay very low. With no devaluation possible, any inflation above partners' steadily erodes competitiveness. You must export to earn dollars.
  • Eurozone analogy: Spain does not control its monetary policy (the ECB does). A student noted the EU is a more equal partnership, which the US would never offer Mexico. Theoretically one world currency would be best, but someone must control issuance. Mundell said strong countries have strong currencies.

What the professor added in class

  • EXAM: All format points in the first section. Concepts, not numbers or names. Short paragraphs with specific elements. Material from class notes counts.
  • Details not in the case: the December Mistake; Banco de México stopping publication of reserve data; Salinas tipped as first WTO head; the minister's "amputating an arm" line; Argentina/India night-time announcements; per capita incomes 25-40% below 1950s levels.
  • EXAM: Three restructuring elements (haircut, longer maturity, lower rate) plus the Brady extras (US Treasury guarantee, oil collateral).
  • EXAM: Two reasons for tolerating an overvalued peso: cheaper dollar debt service and cheaper imports against inflation.
  • EXAM: Hot money vs FDI; sudden stops; Chile's 20% one-year deposit; why India and China keep capital controls; dollarization pros and cons.
  • Overshooting defined as the exchange rate overreacting past its new equilibrium (3 to 7 instead of 3 to 5).

Likely exam angles

  • Short answer: "What is a sudden stop / capital reversal?" Fast short-term inflows, then an abrupt outflow, causing a currency collapse and financial crisis (Mexico 1994, Asia 1997, Russia 1998, Brazil 1999).
  • MCQ: why Mexico kept an overvalued peso: cheaper external debt repayment and cheaper imports (lower inflation).
  • MCQ: features of Brady bonds: debt converted into bonds, US Treasury guarantee, oil collateral, lower early debt service (haircut, longer maturity, lower rate).
  • MCQ: why Banco de México did not raise rates in 1994: fragile banks full of bad loans; higher rates could cause defaults and a banking collapse.
  • MCQ: what tesobonos did: short-term dollar-indexed government debt that moved exchange-rate risk from foreign investors onto the Mexican government and created rollover (liquidity) risk.
  • Cause and effect: Fed hikes plus political shocks lead to reversed inflows, reserve losses, devaluation, float, overshooting, a trade surplus in 1995.
  • Short answer: "Why is FDI safer than portfolio investment?" FDI is long-term and physically locked in (you cannot move a factory); portfolio money is marketable and leaves in a panic.
  • Short answer: dollarization trade-off: no FX risk or devaluation crises vs loss of monetary policy and the need for very low inflation.
  • Mundell link: capital inflows financing a current account deficit and appreciating the currency.
  • Orthodox vs heterodox stabilization: deficits cause inflation vs inflation momentum from expectations, broken by wage-price coordination (Pacto).

Traps and look-alikes

  • Tesobonos vs cetes vs Brady bonds: cetes are peso T-bills; tesobonos are short-term and dollar-indexed; Brady bonds are long-term restructured debt with a US guarantee.
  • Tequila effect (1994-95, mainly Latin America) vs Asian crisis (1997, global). Neither is about Japan; a student asked and Garland said Japan was different.
  • The Mexican authorities rejected both higher interest rates and capital controls. They chose reserves plus tesobonos.
  • Band direction: the case calls the strong-peso side the "top" of the band. A fall in the case's real-exchange-rate index means appreciation.
  • Devaluation (20 December, band widened about 15%) came before the float (22 December). Don't swap them.
  • Numbers that differ: class said the Brady bonds were "1990"; the case dates Brady's initiative to 1988 and the agreement to February 1990. Class listed Fed hikes only to August (+175bp); the case also has +75bp on 15 November (250bp in all for 1994). Class read inflows of "8, 25, 27, 33bn" (1990-93); those are financial-account totals, while portfolio liabilities alone were 3.4, 12.7, 18.0, 28.9bn. Class gave the 1994 trade deficit as about -16bn at one point and -18bn later; the case says -18.5bn. The rescue: class $50bn = 20+18+10; the case adds $3bn from private banks.
  • Chile, not China, has the 20% deposit (transcript garble).
  • Dollarized: Ecuador, El Salvador, Panama. Not Argentina (it used a currency board in the 1990s, not covered in this class).
Session 13 · 26 Oct 2026 · Allard · complete notes

Session 13: Africa, the last development frontier (Prof. Gayle Allard)

Allard's introduction to Africa, using her deck "Africa: The Last Development Frontier" (2025 version). She covered the scale and diversity of the continent, how much African GDP is understated, growth drivers and fragility (commodities, COVID, war, supply shocks), aid vs FDI and trade, demographics and fertility, institutions and conflict (DRC, Ethiopia, Sudan, East Africa and the Bab-el-Mandeb), and continental integration (AfCFTA). She ran short of time and skipped the later slides (governance indices, entrepreneurship, middle class, manufacturing). Those are summarized below from the deck because the exam can draw on slides. Exam relevance: GDP growth vs population growth and GDP per capita, nominal vs PPP GDP, GDP measurement, aid vs FDI and trade, resource curse, and growth strategy. It also sets up the risk/opportunity exercise of Session 14. She also promised to post sample exam questions after class.

Slide-by-slide

Slides 1-2: Title; some macro facts (growth)

  • 54 countries, 2,000 languages, great regional diversity (World Bank data).
  • Growth 3.2% in 2024 and 3.0% in 2023. African potential growth rates are high.
  • GDP growth must outpace population growth (2.7%) for GDP per capita to rise. It has lagged population growth in most years since 2016.
  • 2020: GDP contracted 2.1%, the worst recession in half a century.
  • Many of the fastest-growing economies before the pandemic were sub-Saharan (Angola, Nigeria, Ethiopia, Djibouti, Tanzania...).

Slide 3: Some macro facts (size)

  • Sub-Saharan nominal GDP $1.98trn, 1.8% of the world total, a little smaller than Canada. At PPP it is 3.6%.
  • Africa's economy was about 7% of the world before the Industrial Revolution.
  • GDP in many African economies is severely understated. Many countries are rebasing (Nigeria's GDP rose 89% in 2014). The informal and subsistence economy is hard to measure, and currency changes also play a role.

Slides 4-6: Largest economies, fastest growers, richest per head

  • Highest nominal GDP: Nigeria, South Africa, Egypt, Algeria, Morocco, Ethiopia, Kenya, Ghana, Ivory Coast, Tanzania.
  • Highest GDP at PPP: Egypt first, then Nigeria, South Africa, Algeria, Morocco, Ethiopia, Kenya, Angola, Ghana, Sudan.
  • Some African countries were the fastest-growing on the globe, ahead of Asia.
  • Highest GDP per capita: Seychelles, Mauritius, Gabon, Equatorial Guinea, South Africa, Botswana, Libya (the slide's "Lbyra" is a typo she corrected in class), Namibia, Eswatini, Tunisia.

Slide 7: Other indicators and population

  • Population 1.2bn, 13% of the world total, the youngest and fastest-growing of any region. It may double (to 2bn+) by 2050.
  • Headline: by 2100 only 6 countries (3 African) will have a birth rate at or above replacement (2.1). See Traps: in class she said "at or above 2.5".

Slide 8: Regions (World Bank 2021 or latest)

RegionPopulationGDP per capita
North Africa214m$3,281
West Africa414m$1,750
Central Africa168m$975
East Africa405m$1,024
Southern Africa190m$3,172
  • The richest regions (north and south) have sea access and ports, and a long history of trade and investment exposure.

Slide 9: Oil prices help some and harm others

  • Commodity exporters' outlook moves with resource prices. Higher prices fuel growth, as in the long commodity boom. Eight SSA countries get 90%+ of export revenues from oil.
  • The deck line reads that 36 of 48 SSA countries "see their terms of trade improve" when oil prices rise, and that they hold 80% of SSA population and 70% of GDP. The text is garbled by the two-column layout, and the surrounding argument (commodity importers hit hard) suggests these are the oil-importing countries whose terms of trade worsen. UNSURE: do not rely on the direction.
  • Net commodity importers are more stable but are hurt by high import prices. The Ukraine crisis hit food importers especially hard.
  • Opportunity: better macro conditions have opened Africa's potential. It needs technology, capital and expertise. Key industries: energy supply, financial services, retail, transport and telecoms, public services. A huge population with rising incomes is the great opportunity (and risk).

Slide 10: COVID and the Ukraine invasion left many African nations with much more debt

Slide 11: Optimists or pessimists?

  • Plus: demographic growth is a powerful underlying force for GDP growth.
  • Minus: history shows sustaining fast growth is hard. Looking beyond 5-10 years is difficult, and prospects rise and fall over cycles. Fast-growth periods contain the seeds of their own destruction: exuberant overinvestment (Minsky) and political complacency. A slump leads to clean-up and reform, and the cycle restarts (Ruchir Sharma, The Rise and Fall of Nations, 2016).
  • Do GDP figures reflect reality in Africa?

Slide 12: Absolute poverty is still widespread; only growth can reduce it

  • One in two people in SSA live on under $1/day. About 33% suffer malnutrition. Under 50% have access to hospitals or doctors. Only 57% of children are enrolled in primary school, and only one in three complete it. One in six children die before age 5, 25 times the OECD rate. Children are half of all civilian casualties in Africa's wars. Fewer than one in five people have electricity.

Slide 13: Ranking and evaluating African countries (class exercise)

  • Which indicators best reflect wellbeing and development? Which best reflect risks and opportunities, and are they the same? What weights did you give and why? Top five and bottom five?

Slides 14-17: Aid, trade and prosperity

  • "It is no secret that aid has failed to bring people out of poverty." Dambisa Moyo's vicious aid cycle: aid leads to corruption, which leads to weaker accountability and more poverty, which leads to more aid. Moyo's prescription is market-driven development funded by trade, FDI, capital markets, remittances and microfinance (slide by a former IMBA student).
  • Why trade beats aid as a development tool: it rewards efficiency, entrepreneurship, innovation, saving, investment and productivity; it stimulates all other sectors, including non-traded goods; it generates self-sustaining growth; it sidesteps governance problems; it builds a growing middle class of active, aware citizens. Exception: trade in natural resources may break all these rules.
  • Why trade has been unhelpful for many African countries: (1) inadequate infrastructure, (2) trade barriers in developed countries, (3) protectionism among African nations. Result: trade lost as an engine of growth and productivity.
  • Infrastructure deficiencies inhibit trade.

Slides 18-19: Continental integration and AfCFTA

  • Many schemes exist: CEN-SAD (29, mainly north), COMESA (19, east and central, customs union), EAC (5 East African, customs union), ECCAS (11), ECOWAS (15, West Africa, customs union), IGAD (8), SADC (15, southern, FTA), UMA (5, North Africa). Countries can belong to several. EAC is the most integrated in trade; ECOWAS has the most financial and macro integration.
  • In 2018 African countries agreed to move to a continent-wide FTA (AfCFTA). The ambition: continent-wide free trade, free movement of people, even a single currency some day.
  • Signed by every African nation except Eritrea. Deck: 27 states ratified, to start operating 1 July 2020. Tariffs removed on 90% of traded goods. The UN Economic Commission for Africa says it could raise trade by as much as 52%.

Slides 20-25: Governance and resource wealth

  • Most African nations are not free (freedom map).
  • Corruption is chronic: in the latest Transparency International CPI, six of the most corrupt countries in the world were African.
  • Fragile States Index: seven of the top 10 were African (Somalia, South Sudan, DRC, CAR, Chad, Sudan, Zimbabwe). Another slide: six of the 10 most fragile in 2021 were African.
  • Better governance could solve many problems and make development more inclusive. Elections can help democracy and transparency, but they are often a charade or inflame ethnic and religious divides: "elections determine who is in power, but not how power is used".
  • Governance decides how natural-resource wealth is used.

Slides 26-29: Investment, innovation, entrepreneurship, the middle class

  • On the competitiveness ranking, Mauritius, South Africa, Rwanda, Kenya, Zambia and Botswana are in the top 100, some ahead of India, Mexico, Brazil and Greece. On innovation, South Africa is just behind Spain. But successful ventures beyond micro size are rare.
  • Entrepreneurship needs demand. Is the middle class big enough? The AfDB says 350m people (34%), but that counts incomes of $2-4/day. At $5,500 a year or more, it is only 14%. Standard Bank says the $8,500-42,000/year middle class will triple to 22m by 2030. Expected consumer demand has not materialized; Cadbury, Nestlé, Diageo and many South African firms cut African investment. South Africa is the outlier.
  • Entrepreneurs need capital and institutions. Capital comes from FDI, microfinance, export earnings. Much of Africa is unbanked: savings clubs (South African stokvels), peer-to-peer lending, mobile lending (M-Pesa, M-Kopa). Interest rates are 20-45%. About 40% of wealth may sit outside Africa, which better conditions could bring home. Institutions: dense bureaucracy, widespread corruption, slow permits. In Ease of Doing Business only Mauritius (13th) and Rwanda (38th) were in the top 50; Kenya, South Africa, Zambia, Botswana and Seychelles in the top 100.

Slides 30-33: Manufacturing is shrinking

  • Africa's share of world manufacturing fell from 3% to 2% since 1970, and manufacturing's share of output fell in most SSA countries over 25 years. Nigeria 10% of GDP, South Africa 13% (Thailand 28%). Manufacturing gives 6% of jobs vs 12% in Asia. Exceptions: Ethiopia (manufacturing up 20% a year since 2006 by courting FDI), Tanzania, Rwanda. Is Africa deindustrializing while still poor?
  • High costs: poor energy and transport infrastructure (moving goods inland from a West African port costs four times the US cost; electricity costs about 3x South Asia's), corruption, powerful South African unions, resource curse and Dutch disease.
  • Other culprits: weak trade links (intra-African trade abnormally small; most trade is commodities with former colonial powers; AfCFTA should help), low FDI (corruption, instability), although sectors like flowers in Kenya and Ethiopia boom and China's FDI is the fastest-growing. Robotization may close the export-led path for the poorest (Ethiopia most vulnerable): premature deindustrialization.
  • What replaces manufacturing? Agriculture is shrinking too (down 11 percentage points since 2000). Workers go into informal services rather than factories. Normally labor moves from low-productivity traditional sectors to high-productivity modern ones (Lewis, 1954); manufacturing productivity in Africa is five times agriculture's. Alternatives: tradable services (cut flowers, call centers, tourism) or local services (transport, health, education, telecoms, power).
