Slides 34-35: Dynamic African companies

  • FT list of Africa's fastest-growing companies 2025: by sector, fintech and financial services lead (7); by country, Nigeria (9) and South Africa (5) lead. Final section heading: growth and environmental protection.

What the professor added in class

  • She includes Africa because it is close, little known, and potentially the fastest-growing region after India, if trade and institutions are fixed. Treating "Africa" as one thing is a vast oversimplification.
  • EXAM: Potential growth should be high (population and labor-force growth, plus large room for productivity gains via infrastructure, education and technology). But high population growth means GDP per capita rises only if GDP grows faster than population.
  • COVID hit less hard than feared. Arguments: a young population (COVID was deadliest among the old, e.g. Italy); less urbanization; possible stronger immunity from exposure to serious diseases (a student's view). It still caused a recession because tourism went to zero in countries where it was 5-7% of GDP.
  • Fragility (a student from Ethiopia): supply chains are fragile, so any shock hits fast. Oil, sugar and wheat prices suddenly tripled; after the Iran war escalation there was no fuel and cars were left in the street. The Ethiopian civil war, Sudan's war and the Ukraine war (some countries almost 100% dependent on Russian and Ukrainian wheat) compounded the downturn.
  • The pre-2015 boom was partly the commodity supercycle (oil peaked in 2005-06 above today's levels). But Ethiopia, with no resources, also grew fast.
  • Nominal GDP (2025) almost $2trn, under 2% of the world, about the size of Canada.
  • EXAM: Why GDP is understated: good statistics are expensive (richer countries can afford better data); a large informal and subsistence economy (a family growing its own food and coffee, fetching water and fuel: real value, not in GDP); corruption and smuggling (illegal gold and rare-earth exports from the DRC); capital flight (money spent locally still shows up, but money that leaves is gone). In Ethiopia under 10% of trade is invoiced, and double invoicing is common (one for the importer, one for local offices).
  • EXAM: Rebasing mechanics: GDP growth is computed by weighting sector growth by sector weights from an infrequent detailed survey. Nigeria's weights were over 10 years old, so booming sectors (Nollywood, finance, tech) were underweighted. Rebasing in 2014 raised GDP 89%, and Nigeria briefly passed South Africa. The most conservative estimate she has seen is that African GDP is about 30% underestimated.
  • Before the Industrial Revolution population drove GDP, so Africa was about 7% of the world. The slave trade kept its population far below India's or China's.
  • GDP per capita 1950 vs 2016: Niger, DRC, CAR, Nigeria, Sierra Leone, Zimbabwe fell below the line (negative per capita growth). Many have serious institutional problems or illegal mineral exports.
  • DRC: King Leopold's personal colony with horrific abuse (rubber quotas, hands cut off). Patrice Lumumba, the young independence leader, said he did not care whether the US or USSR helped; he was kidnapped and killed with CIA and Belgian involvement. Mobutu then ruled for about 25 years, ruthless and corrupt, and the West kept giving him aid as an anti-communist bulwark. Most of it ended up in his Swiss bank account. Hopes that Kabila's son would end corruption were dashed.
  • EXAM: Aid vs FDI: the coefficients in her simulation came from a real econometric study in which aid had a negative coefficient every time, however the data was sliced: aid reduced growth. Reason: it induces corruption (people gather round the "money barrel"). Jeffrey Sachs's The End of Poverty inspired the Millennium Development Goals, but the evidence shows aid has not worked. A student added that aid and scholarships can discourage local work. FDI does much more for growth: it creates jobs, brings technology, builds links with foreign buyers and suppliers, and raises productivity. Aid could help only if very carefully directed.
  • Richest per head: Seychelles and Mauritius (small tourist islands), Gabon, Equatorial Guinea (former Spanish colony, tiny population, all oil, a "classic" resource curse), South Africa (the first large balanced economy), Botswana ("a little Switzerland", much better institutions), Libya (high per capita income under Gaddafi, but terrible Gini).
  • Resource-intensive countries can grow faster, but in boom-bust cycles (as in Angola). Non-resource countries (Ethiopia, Kenya) tend to have steadier growth, though they suffer when oil import prices are high.
  • Geography: Africa is far larger than maps suggest (China, India, the US, Europe and Japan fit inside).
  • Demography: Nigeria (now about France plus Germany) will match the US in population by 2050 (UN). Expect emigration or political stress. DRC rises above Ethiopia. Japan, Mexico and Russia drop out of the largest-country list.
  • EXAM: Fertility: SSA held near 7 children per woman into the 1980s and is still well above 2.1 by 2050 in UN projections. Southern Nigeria (Christian, more educated) is already near 2.1. Northern Nigeria (Muslim): in some areas men cannot become voting tribal members until they have 10 children. A student said both Muslim and Christian beliefs in places treat non-reproductive sex as sinful. Birth control is unavailable to many women. Birth rates are falling faster than projected; linear projections tend to be wrong ("the economy is not linear").
  • Infant mortality in the class dataset averaged about 38 per 1,000.
  • Regional story: the north was connected for millennia (Romans, Phoenicians, Greeks, the Mediterranean, the Nile). The south had Portuguese and Dutch ports and settlement. Trade and sea access always help. Being landlocked hurts; landlocked with bad neighbors is worse.
  • Heavy intra-African migration: South Africa receives many from Zimbabwe (hyperinflation) and Somalia.
  • Ethiopia: about 10% a year growth on a Chinese-style investment model until about 2016, then civil war. A student cited about 600,000 killed in fighting and about 600,000 more from famine (student estimates). PM Abiy won the 2019 Nobel Peace Prize for peace with Eritrea; war with Eritrea now looms. "Conflict is the worst kind of institution."
  • Sudan: long civil war; South Sudan seceded after the 2011 vote and then fell into inter-tribal fighting.
  • East African conflicts are tied to Middle East geopolitics: control of the Bab-el-Mandeb chokepoint draws in Israel, the UAE, Saudi Arabia, Turkey, the US, China, Russia, France and the UK, all backing different groups (a student's account).
  • Commodity classification: resource-rich countries are classified by metals and oil as a share of exports (25% threshold mentioned, World Bank data). Many East African countries are not resource-rich (tea, coffee, flowers).
  • Prospects: demography is the big plus, but only if young people get jobs, infrastructure and education (as with India). No country can grow 10% for 30-40 years; even China could not.
  • EXAM: The only proven way to reduce poverty is GDP growth. Education, infrastructure and FDI matter because they raise growth; aid does not work.
  • EXAM: AfCFTA (her updated version): signed by every country except Eritrea, ratified by more than 27, operating since 2019 (she said the slide was out of date). It removes tariffs on 90% of goods. Ambition: common market, single currency, free movement of people (which largely exists de facto; Uganda hosts one of the largest refugee camps). China is building cross-border roads that both sides use even in war zones (a student's point). Internal trade gives economies of scale and specialization, rewards innovation and technology, and lets Africa keep more of the gains from trade, which leak away when you export resources and import manufactures. Resource trade with former colonial powers is volatile and employs few people. The EAC has worked well. Her view: currency integration is feasible because central banks are small and weak, so there would be little resistance. She called AfCFTA one of Africa's best hopes.
  • Indicators students used for risk and opportunity: public and external debt (risk), GDP per capita (opportunity), governance and political stability, ease of doing business, corruption, inflation (a proxy for the overall effectiveness of economic policy). Students' top picks included Ivory Coast, Botswana, Tanzania, Mauritius, Senegal and Rwanda. Rwanda: authoritarian but cracked down on corruption, improved infrastructure, invited FDI, highly digitalized. Bottom: Equatorial Guinea (terribly governed), Congo. No data for Somalia.
  • Homework: do the country investment simulator before Session 14 (10 renamed countries; reveal in class). Allard said she would post sample questions, covering topics like dirty floating that are not fully on slides.

Likely exam angles

  • MCQ: if GDP grows 3% and population 2.7%... per capita barely rises; if GDP grows less than population, GDP per capita falls even though GDP grows.
  • Short answer: why African GDP is understated (informal and subsistence economy, costly statistics, outdated base-year weights, smuggling and capital flight). Nigeria's 2014 rebasing added 89%.
  • MCQ: Africa's share of world GDP is about 2% nominal but 3.6% at PPP. PPP raises it because prices are low.
  • Short answer: why FDI helps development more than aid (jobs, technology, links, productivity vs aid fueling corruption; negative coefficient).
  • MCQ: which is NOT an argument for trade over aid? (e.g. "trade in natural resources always spreads gains", which is false: it is the exception).
  • MCQ: AfCFTA: continent-wide FTA, all except Eritrea signed, 90% tariff removal, ambition of free movement and a single currency.
  • Resource curse links: Equatorial Guinea, Angola, Nigeria; boom-bust growth vs steadier non-resource economies.
  • Lewis model and premature deindustrialization: labor moving from agriculture into informal services, not manufacturing.
  • Growth strategy link: Ethiopia followed a Chinese-style investment model until war broke it.

Traps and look-alikes

  • Fertility headline: the slide says by 2100 only 6 countries (3 African) will be at or above replacement (2.1); in class Allard said 6 countries at or above 2.5 (Global Burden of Disease estimates), naming Chad, Niger and Somalia.
  • AfCFTA start date: the slide says it will begin operating on 1 July 2020 with 27 ratifications; in class she said it has operated since 2019 with more than 27 and called the slide outdated. Summary notes also say "since 2019".
  • GDP scope: the slide's $1.98trn and 1.8% are for sub-Saharan Africa; in class she said "almost $2trn, under 2%" for Africa generally.
  • 2020 recession: the slide says the worst in half a century; the transcript garbles this as "haven't had a recession in half a century".
  • Nominal vs PPP ranking: Nigeria tops nominal GDP; Egypt tops PPP GDP.
  • Middle class: 34% (AfDB, includes $2-4/day) vs 14% (at $5,500/year). Same continent, very different answers depending on the definition.
  • Ease of Doing Business top 50: only Mauritius (13th) and Rwanda (38th).
  • Most fragile states list: "seven of the top 10" on one slide, "six of the 10 in 2021" on another.
  • Terms-of-trade line ("36 of 48... improve") is garbled in the deck; direction UNSURE.
  • "Aid has a negative coefficient" refers to growth regressions in her simulation's source study; it does not mean every aid program is harmful (she said carefully directed aid could help).
Session 14 · 27 Oct 2026 · Allard · complete notes

Session 14: Country investment decision, risk and opportunity (Prof. Gayle Allard)

The last class. Allard used her deck "Country Investment Decision" to pull the course into a way of reading country data for investment. The deck covers how to analyze an economy, Kobrin's definition of country risk, risk vs uncertainty, what investors seek, how business-cycle imbalances turn into risks, internal vs external shocks and a shock-effects grid, the best phase of the cycle to invest, how the same indicator can signal risk or opportunity, how indicator rankings change with the type of investment, risk scorecards (ICRG, Doing Business, CPIA) and governance data. It ends with the debrief of the country investment simulation and the reveal of the 10 disguised countries. This feeds the named short-answer topic "indicators of risk/opportunity for an investor".

Slide-by-slide

Slide 2: How can I analyze an economy?

  • "Eyeball" recent data and find where the economy is in the business cycle (potential GDP, gaps).
  • Do any data surprise you (contradicting models, Okun's law, the Phillips curve)? Explanations and implications?
  • What policies is the government following, and are they right for the gap or abnormalities found? What results can you forecast from them?
  • What do the data say about the exchange rate? What is the growth strategy and its implications? What do institutions look like?
  • What does the overall picture, institutions included, say for business operations and outlook? The key is the purpose of the investment.

Slide 3: What is our objective in country economic analysis?

  • We may have policy objectives, but the most common objective is to assess the risks (or opportunities) of business activities in a country.
  • Country risk (Kobrin 1979): "the probability that political (and economic) events in a country will negatively affect a firm's financial performance."
  • Is risk the same as uncertainty? Is risk necessarily quantifiable (probabilities)?
  • To invest, investors must perceive that expected returns at least compensate for calculated risks.
  • Risks are elements that would reduce revenues, raise costs, or reduce the value of the investment.

Slide 4: What economic conditions do investors seek?

  • Good revenue potential: GDP growth; a large market with incomes high enough to buy the products.
  • Good operating environment, reasonable costs: ease of doing business, minimal bureaucracy, good regulations; low, predictable inflation; reasonably predictable government policy; rational labor markets; low taxes; good infrastructure and public services; stable financial markets.
  • Reasonable financial conditions: moderate interest rates.
  • Access to foreign markets: stable currency, trade agreements.
  • If these conditions are present they are opportunities; if absent, investment presents risk.

Slide 5: How do business-cycle imbalances become risks?

ImbalanceRight policy and resultIf not addressed
Inflationary gapRestrictive fiscal and monetary policy; currency rises; slower growth and profits; balance restoredHigher inflation, bubbles
Recessionary gapExpansive fiscal and monetary policy; return to growth and profits; possible debt problemsConfidence falls, I and C fall, recession, currency falls
High or rising deficits/debtHigher taxes, spending cuts; worse investment conditions and slower growth and profits, then recovery and balanceConfidence falls, currency falls, interest rates rise, debt rises (doom loop)
Falling currencyMore exports, faster GDP growth and profits?Higher inflation, falling confidence, rising debt (doom loop), higher interest rates?

Slide 6: Internal vs external risks

  • Internal (endogenous) shocks, generated inside the economy: domestic economic imbalances, financial market bubbles, financial market crises, banking crises, technological change, political change, violence.
  • External (exogenous) shocks, generated outside: oil or commodity shocks, large immigration flows, pandemics, geopolitical crises, crises of confidence, currency crises, weather events and acts of God, war.

Slide 7: How do external shocks affect the economy? (Remember AS/AD and the components of GDP)

ShockGrowthInflationDeficits/debtConfidenceInterest ratesCurrency
Oil/commodity price shockFalls (in importing nations)RisesRiseFallsRise?Reserve currency rises; others fall?
PandemicFallsRisesRiseFallsFallReserve currency rises; others fall
Geopolitical crisis (disrupted alliances)Falls if trade affectedRises if trade affectedRise?Falls??
Large immigration flowsRisesFalls????
War?RisesRiseFallsRiseReserve currency rises; others fall

Slide 8: What is the optimal moment of the business cycle to invest?

  • Higher or lower unemployment? Higher or lower inflation? Higher or lower interest rates?
  • Phase 1 (expansion): the previous downturn left GDP below potential (recessionary gap); unemployment is high and inflation has come down. The central bank cut rates in the downturn and is in no hurry to raise them. A good phase for investment.
  • Phase 2 (peak): GDP moves above potential as the recessionary gap becomes an inflationary gap. Inflation starts rising and the central bank starts raising rates from a low level. Worse for investment than phase 1.
  • Phase 3 (contraction): growth falls below potential. The central bank raised rates in phase 2 and waits to cut because inflation is still high. Higher rates dampen investment. Less positive than phases 1 and 2.
  • Phase 4 (trough): growth below potential or contracting, the recessionary gap widens, unemployment has risen, inflation is low. Investment is weak until investors are sure the trough has been reached. Lower rates will eventually boost demand, but demand is weak. Once the trough seems near, this is a good phase.

Slide 9: Indicators from the exercise and their role in risk/opportunity

  • The slide sorts indicators into economic/financial risk, institutional risk, economic/financial opportunity and institutional opportunity. Many indicators appear in both a risk and an opportunity column: unstable currency, large current account surplus or deficit, recession or recessionary gap, inflationary gap, high unemployment, rigid labor markets, high interest rate, high Gini coefficient, political instability, regulatory change, conflict, corruption, bureaucracy and red tape.
  • Risk-only items include high inflation, deflation, high fiscal deficit, high public debt, absence of a Phillips curve. Opportunity items include population growth, large market size, free trade arrangements and quality of infrastructure.
  • Lesson: whether an indicator is a risk or an opportunity depends on the investor and the purpose (the slide layout is garbled in the text; exact column placement UNSURE).

Slide 10: Ranking key economic indicators by type of investment

RankFinancial investmentLong-term productive investment (selling in the local market)Facility to export to foreign markets
1Interest rateMarket sizeProduction costs (labor cost, inflation)
2Exchange rateProduction costs (labor cost, inflation)Exchange rate
3Inflation rateInstitutionsTrade agreements
4Fiscal indicatorsDoing BusinessInfrastructure
5Balance of paymentsPolitical stabilityInstitutions
6Reserve levelsInfrastructureDoing Business
7Investor protectionExchange ratePolitical stability
8Political stability
  • Column order reconstructed from the garbled two-column text; the middle column's lower rows (political stability vs infrastructure) are UNSURE. In class she gave the middle column as market size, production costs, institutions/doing business, infrastructure, exchange rate.

Slides 11-31: Simulation charts by indicator (disguised countries, grouped by income)

  • GDP growth: how fast do we want an economy to grow? Where in the cycle should you invest?
  • Inflation: how much is acceptable? Trends vs levels? Which is riskier, inflation or deflation?
  • Unemployment: is the data good enough? What is the "right" rate? Can unemployment be too low?
  • Fiscal deficits: when is a deficit risky? Trends or levels? Cyclical explanations.
  • Debt service (% of GDP or of exports?): level or trend?
  • Current account: is deficit or surplus "better"? Different growth models; cyclical movements.
  • Interest rates: what is the right rate? Are rates right for the cycle? Is monetary policy effective?
  • Institutional: Gini coefficient as risk or opportunity; population growth as risk or opportunity; Doing Business sub-indicators (licenses, construction permits); investor protection; protecting minority investors; corporate tax rate; corruption perception; political stability; rigidity of employment.
  • FDI net inflows as % of GDP (2001-09): how investors actually perceived these countries.

Slides 32-38: How might risks of a productive investment be quantified? ICRG

  • Any scorecard combines economic, financial, political/institutional and other data. Examples: a private institution's scorecard (RGE Monitor, mainly financial), the International Country Risk Guide (ICRG), World Bank Doing Business, World Bank resource allocation index.
  • ICRG Economic Risk Rating: a country's current economic strengths and weaknesses. Components: GDP per head, real GDP growth, annual inflation, budget balance (% GDP), current account (% GDP). Bands: 0-24.5% very high risk, 25-29.9% high, 30-34.9% moderate, 35-39.9% low, 40% or more very low. A poor economic rating can be compensated by better political or financial ratings.
  • ICRG Financial Risk Rating: ability to pay its way (finance official, commercial and trade debt). Components: foreign debt (% GDP), foreign debt service (% exports of goods and services), current account (% exports of goods and services), net international liquidity (months of import cover), exchange rate stability. Same bands.
  • ICRG Political Risk Rating (100 points): government stability 12, socioeconomic conditions 12, investment profile 12, internal conflict 12, external conflict 12, corruption 6, military in politics 6, religious tensions 6, law and order 6, ethnic tensions 6, democratic accountability 6, bureaucracy quality 4. Component bands: under 50% of points very high risk, 50-60% high, 60-70% moderate, 70-80% low, 80-100% very low. Overall: 0-49.9 very high, 50-59.9 high, 60-69.9 moderate, 70-79.9 low, 80+ very low.
  • ICRG Composite: political risk 50%, financial 25%, economic 25%. CPFER = 0.5 PR + 0.25 FR + 0.25 ER. Higher score = lower risk (theoretical maximum 100). Bands: 0-49.5 very high, 50-59.5 high, 60-69.5 moderate, 70-79.5 low, 80-100 very low.
  • Example: Rwanda's ICRG political risk scores 2003-04, totals about 60-63 (moderate risk).

Slides 39-41: Doing Business; CSR and welfare

  • World Bank Ease of Doing Business top 20 and bottom 20; the deck says it "will be replaced by the Business Ready index in 2027".
  • Does welfare in the host country matter? Interested parties: impact investors, firms concerned about image and relations with NGOs and shareholders, NGOs, and the World Bank, whose country assessments "reward" good policy performance.

Slides 42-43: World Bank CPIA

  • The Country Policy and Institutional Assessment rates the quality of a country's policy and institutional framework, meaning how far it supports sustainable growth and poverty reduction. Ratings are based on actions and implementation, not promises. Annual, 136 countries. Used in IDA's performance-based allocation (with IDA Country Performance ratings derived from it) and the Global Monitoring Report.
  • 16 equally weighted criteria in 4 clusters. A. Economic management: macroeconomic management, fiscal policy, debt policy. B. Structural policies: trade, financial sector, business regulatory environment. C. Social inclusion/equity: gender equality, equity of public resource use, building human resources, social protection and labor, environmental sustainability. D. Public sector management and institutions: property rights and rule-based governance, budgetary and financial management, efficiency of revenue mobilization, quality of public administration, transparency, accountability and corruption in the public sector.

Slides 44-46: Governance and other data sources

  • What difference does governance make, and how can it be measured? Sources: Governance Matters (World Bank), CIA World Factbook, Transparency International, World Development Indicators, a political stability index, the Global Competitiveness Report, the AT Kearney FDI Confidence Index, environmental performance indices and other welfare indicators. What do real-world investors look for and what risks worry them?

Slides 47-58: The country reveal

  • Closing questions: would your choices have changed if you had known who they were? How much of a story does the data alone tell?

What the professor added in class

  • Admin: midterms (Angola case) were all graded and were excellent; grades sit in two systems (hers and Garland's) and get averaged. In a class this small there is no curve. The simulation took most students 30-40 minutes, and nothing needed submitting beyond the platform. For exam questions, write to Garland or both, copying both.
  • The point of the session: step back from the "trees" (individual countries) to the forest. A former student once asked her for a template because his employer asked him to do exactly this.
  • EXAM: Main reason you will use this course: to assess risks and opportunities in a country for investment, export or import. Country risk = the probability that political and economic events will negatively affect a firm's financial performance. The leap is from "is this indicator abnormal?" to "how does this deviation affect a firm's financial performance?"
  • EXAM: Risk vs uncertainty: risk can be identified and roughly quantified in advance (the indicators studied). Uncertainty is unquantifiable and unexpected (an earthquake). They are not the same.
  • Investors need expected returns to compensate for risk: to invest in the DRC you would need very high returns (China invests heavily there).
  • How difficult countries attract investment: (1) cut risk by creating a credible, predictable operating environment from the top, as Kagame did in Rwanda (announce reforms and follow through; he is a dictator); (2) communicate intentions and deliver, like Modi's goal of reaching the top 50 on Ease of Doing Business (a student said India went from 142nd in 2014 to 63rd); (3) incentives such as tax holidays, free land and industrial parks. In Ghana she saw an industrial park with its own power and water supply and lawyers to help with paperwork; Ford assembles cars there. A young, cheap, flexible labor force is Africa's advantage; firms sometimes feed and train workers.
  • Doing Business was suspended because China was found manipulating it (her account).
  • If a country that "washed its face" then fails investors, that is a strong warning to future investors.
  • Corruption measurement (her account in class): the governance indicators on a -2.5 to +2.5 scale come from a group of scholars ("Princeton or a top American school", UNSURE), updated about every 5 years (UNSURE), built from many criteria scored and weighted. She said Transparency International's Corruption Perception Index surveys people in about 186 countries on whether they had to bribe someone in the last month. Every year Indian, Chinese and Latin American students raise their hands to having bribed; Americans and Europeans do not; the countries that say yes tend to score as more corrupt.
  • Class debate: petty bribery vs systemic corruption (lobbying, procurement). A student's Delhi example: a bucket worth under $5 procured for about $1,500. US lobbying was described as making payments to legislators transparent. Singapore pays civil servants well to remove the incentive to take bribes. Hard-to-get government jobs may raise the pressure to recoup "investment" through bribes (student view).
  • EXAM: Investors she knows say corruption adds about 10% to costs in Africa.
  • Revenue potential: growth plus a large market. The Gini coefficient fits here: the middle class is very small in every African country except South Africa, so a firm selling locally must check how many people can afford its goods.
  • Operating environment: inflation should be low and predictable so you can plan. Unpredictable policy example: new US visa charges hitting foreign students (and IE's New York campus). Labor markets: ease of hiring and firing. Taxes fine as long as not abusive. Infrastructure is the hardest to measure.
  • Financial conditions: if debt is high and a crisis looks near, interest rates rise, and many economic indicators feed into the interest rate a firm pays. Foreign-market access: currency and trade agreements.
  • Imbalance chains: in an inflationary gap with bubbles, the right response (restrictive policy) slows you down but prevents a burst. Identifying a bubble is hard: an asset price misaligned with other indicators (house prices rising faster than inflation or incomes) for a sustained period. In a recessionary gap the right response is expansive policy, but some countries cannot borrow more; France last balanced its budget in 1974. Doom loop: debt rises, confidence falls, rates rise, debt rises more, the currency falls; it must be cut somewhere (Milei in Argentina). The fix (tax rises, spending cuts) worsens investment conditions short-term but leads to recovery. A sudden currency fall (like the yen) helps exports but raises import prices and burdens foreign-currency debt.
  • If you know the relationships and the right policy, you can see a crisis coming: either the government does the right thing (some pain, then recovery) or it does not (crisis).
  • AI/tech bubble debate: Allard expects it to pop because every technological innovation in history was followed by a bubble and a crisis. Students argued government support for the US-China race would prevent it. P/E ratios measure stock bubbles; dot-com P/Es were higher.
  • EXAM: Same indicator, risk or opportunity: a financial market crash is a risk, but if you are in property it lowers asset prices so you can acquire cheaply. A high Gini is bad for a country and for a firm selling locally (small middle class), but a firm exporting with cheap labor may see it as an opportunity, "as long as there's no revolution". High unemployment can mean available labor. The point is the investor's purpose.
  • Violence and conflict are the worst institutional failure (Ethiopia back at war, set back 10 years).
  • EXAM: Walking the shock grid in class: commodity price shock depends on whether you export or import. For an importer, growth falls, inflation rises, deficits rise (expansive fiscal response, higher rates), confidence falls, rates rise (to fight inflation, and on lower confidence); a reserve currency rises and others fall. Pandemic: growth falls broadly; inflation rises because supply falls faster than demand (chips, car parts); deficits rise; confidence falls; rich countries cut rates (poor countries cannot), which is why Africa worried them. Geopolitical crisis (disrupted alliances, e.g. hostility with Canada): confidence falls, investment and consumption suffer, trade is lost; inflation rises if you depend on the lost partner's imports; deficits rise; rate effects unclear. "No one has ever won a trade war." Immigration: growth up, inflation down, fiscal position can improve; confidence depends on how the media portray it. War: growth up for arms producers (the US benefits); inflation up with supply limits; deficits up; confidence down; rates probably up.
  • EXAM: Indicator ranking by investment type. Financial investment (e.g. buying Nigerian bonds): interest rate and exchange rate first; then inflation and fiscal indicators (they drive both through confidence); balance of payments (deficit countries' currencies should fall); reserve levels (in the Asian crisis her bank checked reserves every day; Mexico's case is the same story); investor protection; political stability. Reserves here mean official reserves in the vault (India's 1991 crisis: about two weeks of imports), not the commercial-bank reserves involved in quantitative easing.
  • EXAM: For the simulation (long-term manufacturing investment) her ranking: (1) market size (GDP growth, trade agreements, size of the middle class and Gini), (2) production costs (labor cost, inflation), (3) institutions and ease of doing business (you have to live there), (4) infrastructure, (5) exchange rate. She said the ranking is a bit arbitrary.
  • EXAM: For a facility to export: production costs and exchange rate move up, trade agreements possibly higher, then infrastructure and institutions; market size barely matters. Doing Business does not appear for financial investment because it is short-term. The exchange rate matters a lot in some cases and less in others.
  • Many students moved political stability to the top after seeing the data; often students treat it as an entry condition.
  • Growth: students liked stable, consistent growth. Year 6 in the charts was the global financial crisis (hidden on purpose).
  • EXAM: Never decide on an emerging economy using unemployment data alone. It comes from surveys (India's figures look constant because data are collected only in cities), and must be read against demographics.
  • Somerville had about 1,100% inflation in its crisis, coinciding with its deep GDP collapse. Trinity Island showed deflation and big population loss (emigration). Tweedworth ran surpluses some years and deficits others, "which is what we should have". Plata del Mar ran constant deficits, acceptable given its fast growth.
  • Political stability index: negative = less stable. She called it reliable and available at the World Bank, and better than the Economist Intelligence Unit's, which "totally missed the Arab Spring".
  • Reveal comments: Isla de la Paz = Hong Kong, the second-easiest place to do business, "a great place to hold money but a poor place to make things" (a student's phrase), with China pulling it back and forth. Plata del Mar = Vietnam: fast growth, controlled deficits and inflation, thanks to one-party rule ("communism" now means a political system); you cannot buy land and public meetings are limited. Trinity Island = Czech Republic: EU market access and a flexible labor market. Tweedworth = Mauritius, the most popular pick: mostly services and finance (and money laundering), poor for manufacturing, but the easiest place in Africa to do business, so firms used it as a base for East Africa. Pineway = Israel: easy to do business but five wars in 20 years. Somerville = Bulgaria: past hyperinflation, now a good investment with EU access. Liberty Kingdom = Turkey: customs union with the EU, less stable under Erdogan, still a good manufacturing option. Princeton Island = Egypt, also a good investment. Malkinia = Kenya: a startup economy, now attractive (less obvious in 2009). Brandt Republic = Venezuela: one of the worst places to do business, very unstable, stopped investing in oil.
  • Closing message: you can read a country's story from its data. Paid providers weight and package such data; it is a business in itself.

Likely exam angles

  • Short answer (named topic): "Indicators of risk/opportunity for an investor": revenue potential (growth, market size, incomes and middle class), operating environment (low predictable inflation, predictable policy, flexible labor, taxes, infrastructure, ease of doing business), financial conditions (interest rates, debt), market access (currency, trade agreements), plus institutions and political stability; their weight depends on investment type.
  • MCQ: Kobrin's definition: the probability that political (and economic) events in a country will negatively affect a firm's financial performance.
  • MCQ: risk vs uncertainty: risk is identifiable and quantifiable; uncertainty is unquantifiable and unexpected.
  • MCQ: for a financial investment the top indicators are the interest rate and exchange rate; for an export platform, production costs and exchange rate; for local-market production, market size.
  • MCQ: ICRG composite weights: political 50%, financial 25%, economic 25%; a higher score means lower risk.
  • MCQ: best phase to invest: phase 1 (early expansion: recessionary gap closing, low rates, moderate inflation); also the trough once it seems near.
  • MCQ: shock grid: e.g. a pandemic raises inflation (supply falls faster than demand) and rich countries cut rates; immigration raises growth and lowers inflation.
  • MCQ: "which indicator can be both risk and opportunity?" e.g. high Gini, high unemployment, a financial crash, an unstable currency.
  • Internal vs external shocks: banking crisis, bubble, political change, violence (internal); commodity shock, pandemic, war, immigration, geopolitical crisis (external).
  • CPIA: 16 equally weighted criteria in 4 clusters, based on actions not promises, used for IDA allocation.

Traps and look-alikes

  • Kobrin's definition says negatively affect a firm's financial performance. Distractors may say "any change in GDP" or "probability of sovereign default".
  • Risk is quantifiable; uncertainty is not. A distractor may reverse them.
  • ICRG: political risk is half the composite; economic and financial are a quarter each. Higher points = lower risk (not higher risk). Within the political rating the 12-point items are government stability, socioeconomic conditions, investment profile, internal and external conflict; bureaucracy quality is only 4.
  • Market size ranks first for local-market production but barely matters for an export platform.
  • Doing Business is absent from the financial-investment column (short horizon).
  • Phase 2 (peak) is not the best time to invest even though growth is strong: inflation and rates are starting to rise.
  • Pandemic interest rates fall (rich countries ease) while commodity-shock and war rates rise.
  • Commodity shock effects are for importing nations; exporters gain.
  • Reveal names in the summary notes are garbled ("Platinum" = Plata del Mar, "East Belfast" = Isla de la Paz, "Grand Republic" = Brandt Republic, "Meltingia" = Malkinia, "Pine Way" = Pineway). The summary also listed "Kenya" in the low-income group: it is Malkinia. Use the deck.
  • Corruption indices: what Allard described in class for the Transparency International CPI (a survey of whether individuals paid bribes in the past month) is not how the CPI is usually described (expert perceptions); treat the method as her class account, UNSURE. The -2.5 to +2.5 scale belongs to the governance indicators, not the CPI.
  • Modi's target: in class she first said "number 70", then accepted a student's correction to top 50 (142nd in 2014 to 63rd, student's figures).
  • Doing Business replacement: deck says Business Ready in 2027; in class she said the index was suspended over China's manipulation.
